Foreword
From Zero to $83K/month in 3 Years
I was just a normal software engineer making $105,600/year at a regular corporation,
living in a $700/month rental room in Singapore.
In September 2021, I quit my job to start working on my own "startup." It was a special
type of startup: there was no funding, no investors, and no team. It was just me working
on my product ideas. 
I became a full-time indie hacker.
I didn't come from a rich family, had no experience in startups, no connections in the
business world, no social media presence, no followers, no community.
In next 3 years:
I made the first $9 internet money from my software product and built an audience
of 10,000 followers on Twitter.
Then, I built a new SaaS business and grow it to profitable at $14K/month revenue,
but then lost it all to a surprised incident.
Then, I built a new AI product, expanded to B2B, and bounced back to $45K/month
revenue in 6 months.
I continued to grow my audience to more than 100K followers, build more products,
growing total revenue to $83K/month with 85% profit from my products by the end
of 2024.
And I was able to do all this while living my life in freedom, traveling, doing what I
love, not having to report to a boss or manage a big team.
How I was able to achieve this is not a secret, it's quite the opposite. I share my stories,
ideas, updates, and even revenue numbers publicly all the time — people call this "
build
in public
."
Since I started this journey, I have posted more than 25,000 tweets, 45 long-form
articles on my newsletter, websites, forums, and appeared on several podcasts and
YouTube videos.
In the past 2 years, I've been gathering them all, rewriting, updating, and structuring it
into one central place: 
My Indie Book
. This is the book where I share about:
My stories, who I am, why, how, and what I did to achieve this result.
How I find and validate ideas, how to build, find customers, launch, market, sell… all
without having to spend big money or having a big team.
What are the steps that I took, the tools I use, how I made my decisions, the
preparation, the mindset, the advantages… and many other related topics.
I want to make 
indie hacking
 become more popular so more people know about this
alternative way to make a living without having to work for a company or start a new VC-
funded unicorn startup.
Who Is This Book For?
This book is for people who want to become a full-time indie hacker.
Indie hacking is all about freedom. If you love working on your own products, building
your own business, making enough money that can cover your lifestyle, all while working
remotely from anywhere, not having a boss, waking up without a morning alarm, and not
limiting your true potential by performance reviews and corporate politics, this book is
for you.
This book will be most relevant to software engineers who are currently at a full-time
job, because that's where I'm coming from. But even if you are not that person, this
book may also be helpful by giving you a glimpse into what it's like to be an indie hacker
and how one can get there.
This book is not a "how to get rich" or "how to succeed" tutorial.
Indie hacking is a big topic with so many ways you can be successful. I'm contributing
my small part to the indie community by sharing my success story and the lessons I
learned along the way.
This book will not teach you everything about indie hacking, only the things that I know
and learned from my own experience or from observing other successful indie hackers.
It doesn't have step-by-step instructions on how to succeed — it's best to pick out
things that work for you and experiment with them.
This Book Has Two Parts
Part 1: My stories (~150 pages)
In this part, I will share with you my entire story from the very beginning, and all the
stories for each successful product that I have created, including the failed ones.
With each product, I will share about:
How I came up with the idea, what circumstances allowed me to see the problems
that can be solved by building the product.
What advantages I had when building the product, how I built it.
How I found customers and got my first revenue from the product.
How I grew the products to profitability, or how I failed.
After each story, I will share relevant takeaways and the lessons I have learned.
Part 2: Coffee chats (~150 pages)
In the second part, I want to share my opinions about other topics related to indie
hacking that I havenʼt mentioned in my stories. I organize this part into small individual
topics and group them into chapters:
The Indie Mindset
Finding and Validating Ideas
Building Your Product
Ways to Reach Customers
Growing Your Business
It's like having a morning coffee chat with me talking about indie hacking.
Part 1: My Stories
.
From 0 to $83K/month in 3 years:
TLDR version
I want to start the book by sharing with you a summary version of my indie
story from the very beginning up until I reached $83K/month revenue. This
chapter will go through all of the major events, and then in the later chapters,
we will go into more details one-by-one and share the related lessons I learned
along the way.
This is the TLDR (too long, didnʼt read) version of my story.
September 20, 2021, was the first day I became unemployed and started working for
myself.
Going full-time on indie hacking was the best decision I've ever made. Working for
myself gives me a freedom I've never experienced before. Over the past three years, I
have:
Created 4 successful products making total $83K/month revenue.
Built an audience of 100,000+ followers on Twitter.
Created a newsletter with 10,000+ subscribers.
Survived a drama that killed my business.
And jumped on the AI hype train and created a product with it, making my first
million.
At the moment, my total revenue across all products is about $1 million/year at ~85%
profit.
I want to take this opportunity to write a recap of my journey so far. I hope this will be
helpful for people who are looking to become their own boss one day.
A bit of background
I have always loved building software since I was very young. I learned Visual Basic 6
when I was 15 from books and the internet so I could build apps for Windows.
After graduate from software engineering school, I worked a software engineer for 7
years before quitting my job in September 2021.
Throughout my career, I have always had some side projects along with my full-time job.
Most of them were just for learning and experimenting with cool tech.
Below is a summary of my full career history up to the point I quit my job:
Thanks to 7 years working in the industry, I picked up a lot of useful skills: frontend,
backend, DevOps, mobile apps, game dev, and a bit of UX/UI design.
These skills became my biggest advantage and have helped me tremendously later in
my journey.
Learned about Indie Hackers
In early 2020, COVID happened. I was forced to work remotely alone at home (in a
foreign country). It was incredibly boring.
I discovered 
IndieHackers.com
 and started listening to their podcast every day.
I had always dreamed of opening my own software company, but after graduating from
software engineering school, I felt the social and financial pressure to get a job and
pursue a normal career.
Besides, I had always thought "opening a software company" would require a lot of
management work, hiring many people, and dealing with clients - pretty much like any
other outsourcing company out there (or what people usually call a "dev shop"). It
sounded very boring.
It wasn't until I learned about Indie Hackers that I discovered it's possible to build a one-
person company that just builds and sells software (SaaS, to be specific) to the global
market, making a decent living.
I got really inspired by the stories of successful indie hackers like 
Pieter Levels
, 
Kyle
Gawley
, 
Jon Yongfook
 (and many more).
The inspiration, the boredom of COVID, and the extra time I got from not having to
commute to work led me back to my passion: building software products.
So I gave it a try. I started working on a new side project with the hope of generating
some revenue.
First failure
My first attempt was to build a log viewer macOS app.
As a good software engineer, I wanted the app to have a beautiful UI, tons of features, a
comprehensive architecture, and a unit test suite with >95% test coverage.
[1]
[2]
[3]
This is a screenshot of the app in development, I still have it in Xcode:
I worked on it for ~6 months. I felt like the project would never finish. Then I got bored
and abandoned the project.
I was over-engineering.
The project failed spectacularly, but at least I got something from it: I gained a lot of
experience working with Swift, which means I can build macOS and iOS apps much
faster now.
First internet revenue: DevUtils
It took me a while to recover from my first failure.
About a month later, I decided to give it another try (and this time, you can be certain
that I wrote zero test cases!).
I built the first version of 
DevUtils
 in about 2 weeks. It's an app that bundles all the
frequently used developer tools into one unified interface that works offline on your
macOS.
I sent the app to friends, family, and co-workers to use (for free) and received some
good feedback. People loved the app!
That was the sign I was looking for. I was excited and decided to add payment to the
app: $9 — a one-time purchase to use the app forever.
Then, I 
posted the app on Hacker News
. I think I got lucky: the post received some
positive comments, and I got to the top of Hacker News for a few hours.
[4]
[5]
And that's how I got my first-ever internet dollar. I jumped like crazy in my bedroom in
Singapore (I was still working full-time at the time).
Struggle to reach new customers
Things started to slow down after the initial traffic spike from the Hacker News front
page. I posted the app 
on Product Hunt
 and got another traffic spike, and that was it.
A week after that, I rarely got any visitors to the website. No more sales.
I knew that posting the app to websites and forums on the internet and hoping for traffic
spikes wouldn't work in the long term. I couldn't get lucky forever.
[6]
I tried a lot of things to do marketing for DevUtils, hoping to make more people know
about the app. I tried Google paid ads, wrote SEO articles, looked for sponsorships on
newsletters/YouTube channels, and tons of other things.
There were some small results, but in the end, I didn't see a way that could give me
traffic for the long term without continuous effort. And doing these types of marketing
activities really stressed me out.
I was close to giving up and accepting that this might be just another side project that
managed to make some revenue but was unable to grow into a real business.
But I gave it another try. I started to look for long-term distribution channels. This is
when I thought about Twitter and the Build in Public community.
Find a distribution channel: 
Twitter
I decided to try becoming a Twitter influencer.
The plan was that if I could get a lot of followers, I would have a consistent flow of traffic
to DevUtils without doing much.
And so, I reactivated my old Twitter account and started building my personal brand.
Most of my tweets were about DevUtils, and it was quite boring.
I quickly learned that tweeting about DevUtils alone wasn't going to help me gain
followers. People just don't care.
Sure, I engaged with other people in the community, replied to their tweets, made jokes,
and was just hanging around. But in the end, if I don't have something interesting on my
own timeline, people have no reason to follow me. Just tweeting about DevUtils wasn't
going to cut it.
I needed to do something interesting to get attention.
So I started doing a lot of fun stuff, using my skills and advantages: coding.
For example, this experiment is 
my first ever "viral" tweet
 with 100+ likes:
[7]
In summary, my Twitter strategy was:
Build interesting stuff and share it in public
Engage with other people
Write threads
A lot of memes and jokes
Overall, be an interesting person and be nice
After 6 months of consistently doing this, from 100 followers in November 2020, I grew
my account to 
700 followers
 in May 2021.
First “real” business: Black Magic
I considered 
Black Magic
 to be my first ever "real" business because it has a
subscription pricing model with recurring revenue.
This is how it started.
Around May 2021, when I was approaching 1,000 followers on Twitter, I decided to do
something special to celebrate it (and also to get more engagement!).
By looking through the Twitter API documentation, I learned that I could update the
profile picture via API. So, I built a small script that shows a progress bar around my
profile picture.
The progress bar
 would get closer and closer to 100% as I got closer to 1,000
followers.
[8]
[9]
[10]
People loved the idea so much! So I turned the script into a web app and added a
$4/month subscription fee for the "Pro" version, where people could customize the
progress bar color.
And that was how I got my 
first ever recurring revenue dollar
!
[11]
At this time, I was still working at a full-time job.
Embracing the traction, I worked on Black Magic with all the free time I had.
I added many other features to Black Magic, mostly Twitter tools that help users create
more engagement.
By building and tweeting at the same time, both my MRR (monthly recurring revenue)
and my followers were increasing fast. My other product DevUtils was also enjoyed the
benefit of my larger audience, website traffic and revenue increased significantly.
Quit job
By August 2021, I had:
~$300 MRR from Black Magic from 3,000+ users (most are free users)
~$1,100/mo from DevUtils (not recurring revenue, so it fluctuates a lot)
~8,000 followers
Overall, there was great momentum. I felt confident that I might actually have a shot at
making a living from Black Magic and DevUtils.
That was when I handed in my notice, officially quitting my job. September 20, 2021,
was my last working day and my first day as a full-time indie hacker.
I remember I went outside for a walk, took a fresh breath of air, and felt the freedom I'd
never experienced before.
The park near my place when I was working in Singapore.
Around the same time, I started writing a newsletter. I write one post every month and
publish it on Substack: 
https://news.tonydinh.com/
I had savings to last 2 years in the bank. If I lived in Vietnam (my hometown), I could
easily stretch it to 4 years without revenue. My backup plan was to just return to a full-
time job if things didn't work out.
At the time, I didn't have a family yet - no wife and no kids. It was the best time for me to
take the risk and make the biggest bet of my life. So I did.
My goal was to get to $1K MRR in the first year, which would be enough for me to live
comfortably in Vietnam forever.
Ramen profitability in year one
Since quitting my job, I spent all of my time working on Black Magic/DevUtils and still
kept tweeting to build an audience.
The growth started to pick up. I added many new features to Black Magic, most
noticeably 
the Magic Sidebar
 – a Chrome extension for Twitter that provides
Analytics & CRM features.
This feature single-handedly changed Black Magic as a product forever - from being a
fun engagement tool to becoming a true painkiller, solving major problems that many big
Twitter accounts had at the time.
Later, I pivoted the whole product around this feature. I changed the landing page to
exclusively talk about this feature instead of the profile progress bar and other fun
features. You can see it at 
BlackMagic.so
.
[12]
Within a few months, by building, tweeting, and launching the product on Product Hunt,
my monthly revenue grew to $4K MRR.
By February 2022, I had reached $5K MRR and 28K followers on Twitter, far beyond my
goal.
Travel, Work, and Play
In the first few months after quitting my job, I worked a lot. Probably 12 hours a day, or
even 16 hours/day if you also count Twitter as "work".
So when I reached $4K MRR, a decent amount considering my living cost in Vietnam, I
wanted to slow down.
I still wanted to get more revenue, but I realized that this is a moving goalpost, and it will
never stop. $10K, then $20K, then $50K. I knew I would never be satisfied.
I love building products, writing code while making a living from it, and I have achieved it.
There is no reason to push myself to work harder if I don't feel like it. I can keep at this
until I'm 80 years old.
Instead of enduring hardship working for 40 years and taking the rest 40 years to retire,
it's much better to work and play at the same time.
So I traveled. I went for a trip around Vietnam.
My average working hours during this period were about 4 hours/day. I still tweeted a lot.
Around September 2022, something magical happened: I was invited to the Indie
Hackers podcast! The very podcast that I listened to every day when I started out. It was
like a dream come true!
The podcast was released on 
22nd September 2022
, almost exactly one year after I
quit my job.
Fast forward to October 2022, Black Magic grew to $14K MRR steadily, with many happy
customers. I used the product myself every day. I watched the metrics increasing month
by month in disbelief at what I had created. I was very happy.
[13]
Build more products
Besides working on my existing products, I experimented and tried new things all the
time.
I built several small products, some of which failed (EmojiAI, AskCommand) because
they didn't catch enough attention and gain enough users.
One of them, 
Xnapper
 - a screenshot app, picked up my 
audience's interest
 and
became my 3rd successful product. It was making $6K/month consistently for a while.
[14]
[15]
Working on multiple products allowed me to switch between them when I felt bored with
one product, reducing my stress.
It's also a good way to have fresh new content for the Twitter audience. I was embracing
"build in public" and I shared everything when I was building the app.
For example, 
this tweet
 showing the mobile app demo got 1,700 likes! It's almost like
free marketing.
From $14,000 MRR and profitable to zero again
Around February 2023, shortly after Elon Musk bought Twitter, they announced that
Twitter API (which Black Magic was using) would no longer be free.
I didn't think this would be a big deal. I was making good money from Black Magic and
would be happy to pay.
[16]
Little did I know that the price was announced at 
$42K/month
 (per month, not per year).
There was a smaller plan at $100/month, but the restrictions and limits were so low that
you couldn't even run a weather bot with it.
Black Magic was at $14K MRR at the time. That meant there was no way I could afford
the API price.
I was "forced" to either shut down or sell. Long story short, I sold Black Magic for
$128K
. As of now, I no longer own Black Magic, but I still love the product and use it
every day.
The funny thing is, a few months later, Twitter changed their mind about the pricing.
They introduced a new plan at $5,000/month.
This plan comes with some restrictions and API limits, it may or may not work for Black
Magic, but I didn't even bother to check, because it no longer mattered.
So that was my first product exit. Not the way I wanted, but there was nothing I could
do.
Around the same time, I started working on a new product: 
TypingMind
.
Joining the AI scene: TypingMind
OpenAI 
announced the ChatGPT API
 on March 1, 2023. I had been using ChatGPT
via the web interface for a while at that point.
The web interface was very limited and I got annoyed by it for a long time. For example,
you couldn't search your previous chats, the text output was slow, etc. Most annoyingly,
the app logged you out and you had to log in again every day, which was extremely
frustrating.
At some point, I was thinking of writing a Chrome extension to scratch my own itch.
OpenAI releasing the API was exactly what I was waiting for. I immediately thought of
writing a better UI for ChatGPT using the API.
The next day, I registered the domain name 
typingmind.com
 and started working on
the prototype for a few hours.
The Twitter drama was still going on and kept me busy for the rest of the week, but I
managed to get the first version of Typing Mind finished on the weekend.
On Monday, 6th March 2023, I released the first version to the public and 
announced it
on Twitter
.
[17]
[18]
[19]
The app immediately received a lot of traction.
I added a paid plan at $9, then slowly increased the price as I added more features to
the app in the following days.
Within the first day of releasing the app, I made $1K in revenue, then $2K the next day,
then $4K the next day. In 7 days, I made a total of $22K in license revenue.
It was clear that the product was addressing a real problem, and a lot of people are
happy to pay for it. This was all I needed to decide to spent all of my focus on
TypingMind.
Going B2B
One of the most significant contributors to the growth of TypingMind was the
introduction of the 
team version
 for business customers.
The initial version of TypingMind was a one-time purchase. Even though it made a lot of
revenue, it was not recurring revenue, so it wasn't sustainable. I thought the next
obvious step was to introduce a team version that would help companies set up an AI
workspace allowing their team members to chat with AI models.
I released the first version of the team version on Mar 29, 2023. I called it "Custom
Deployment," set the price at $79/month, and advertised it in a corner of the personal
version of TypingMind. Slowly, people who were using the personal version started to
notice it and tell their teams to adopt the product. More and more business customers
started to try the team version.
In the first week, I got a few business customers to sign up and reached ~$600 MRR.
[20]
By July 2023, three months after introducing the team version, I reached $6K MRR.
I started to learn a lot more things about how to sell to businesses, B2B workflows, get
on meetings, do demo calls, hire a security firm to do pen-testing, get SOC2 certified,
open up a reseller program, etc. I landed my first big contract with an enterprise
company in Europe in August 2023, bring in $20,000 of revenue for that month.
In the next few months, the recurring revenue from the B2B side started to increase
steadily, and the one-off revenue from the personal version was still going strong.
By September 2023, the app was making ~$40K/month revenue on average, only about
25% of that was recurring revenue coming from B2B.
My first million
With all of my time working on TypingMind, I released more than 100 updates to the app
for the next one year, heavily relying on word-of-mouth to reach new customers. The
B2B revenue stream reaching almost half of the monthly revenue.
By November 2024, TypingMind reached $1 million TTM revenue (TTM = twelve trailing
months).
I'm really proud of achieving the magic number $1M with TypingMind, but what I'm more
proud of is being able to do that while enjoying life at the same time, without an office or
a big team. In fact, when TypingMind reached $1M, I was surfing in Bali.
I still work roughly on average 4 hours/day, but it's all productive and meaningful work, I
enjoy it. Every chunk of 2 hours I spend, I get a lot more done than at any previous
companies I worked for. I no longer have the concept of "weekend".
I truly think that people should work when they feel the most productive, not from 9am
to 5pm.
And that's where I am today.
Lifestyle changes, the good & bad
I absolutely love working for myself and not having a 9-5 job. However, there are pros
and cons to everything. After 3 years on this journey, I can understand why this is not
the way for everyone.
The good
The first and most important benefit to me is the freedom. When I have reached the
ramen profitability level, I can choose to change my "work-life balance" level as how I
want it.
I give myself a lot of free time to pursue other interests I have in life, like 
learning
hardware
, 
surfing
, 
skiing
, 
traveling
, 
playing games
, etc. I surf almost
every day now.
I work remotely while traveling around the world. I constantly travel around in Southeast
Asia (Vietnam, Bali, Singapore, Thailand, etc.) looking for waves to surf, working from
cafes and hotels.
[21]
[22]
[23]
[24]
[25]
I can choose to work on any day as I feel like it, or I can spend the whole day watching
Netflix without asking anyone for permission.
The money is also great; there is just no way I could achieve this amount of money just
by working a normal job. I no longer have to think about money all the time on the
smallest things like I used to. Instead, I spend more time thinking about what I want to
do, learn, and experience.
The learnings are also one of the benefits. Running a company requires a wide range of
skills that I learned along the way: marketing, legal, finance, partnership, sales, etc. Even
though I'm at a very small scale, I feel much more confident talking about business now
compared to when I was just an employee doing what I was told.
The bad
The cold start
Nothing comes for free. It could be very challenging and stressful to find a product and
make it work, especially in the beginning.
I always tell people not to quit their job without stable monthly revenue, a lot of savings,
and backup plans.
Working on side projects while having a full-time job can be stressful too. I was lucky
because I hadn't yet had a family and only needed to take care of myself when I started.
For people who have a full-time job, wife, and kids, there isn't much time and energy left
to work on side projects, and the risk factor is also much larger in case of failure.
The risks
Even when you have a successful product, the risks still remain.
Revenue may fluctuate, the market may change, new competitors may show up, or
maybe you do something stupid. It could affect the business and thus affect you.
Look at what happened to Black Magic - if I couldn't have managed to get the new
product (TypingMind) generating a new revenue stream for me at the time, I would have
been extremely stressed, which could have caused a spiral downturn, who knows. This
is also the reason why I prefer to have multiple products, to reduce the risk of one dying.
The social life
I had to sacrifice my professional network and social life. It gets very lonely.
When I quit my job, I didn't think this would be a big problem, so I didn't really pay
attention to keeping my connections with people around me.
Now I'm starting to build back connections, but it's difficult to keep in touch with people
when you no longer work in the same office and have the same topics to talk about.
My friends all have full-time jobs, not a lot of indie hacker friends around me, and they
all have different favorite countries/cities to live in. My best option if I want to fix this is to
move to some popular indie hubs around the world like Bali or Lisbon, but I still think this
isn't going to work long-term. It's just the trade-off I have to make going on this path.
Lucky for me, at least I still have the online Twitter community to hang out with.
Today and the future. Takeaways.
So that's my entire story from the very beginning.
What's next? I don't really know.
Right now, I don't have a long-term plan. I only have a general guideline that I live by:
stay healthy, make money by doing things meaningful works that I'm interested in, build
relationships, all of that while not sacrificing my freedom.
Work and enjoy life at the same time.
As for my short-term plan, I'll keep working on my biggest product, TypingMind. Maybe
even launch new products in the near future!
I hope by sharing my story, this will be helpful for people who want to pursue the same
path.
If you are looking to do the same thing as I did, here are some of my key takeaways:
First and foremost, keep in mind that all of this is my story, "my way." It's definitely
not the "only way," and probably not "the best way." All you can do is cherry-pick
what I shared here and see if it works for you. There is no formula to guarantee
success.
If you plan to work alone like me, try to become a generalist: know a bit of
everything. For example, if you're a developer, don't restrict yourself to only working
as a frontend dev — try to develop backend too, and mobile apps, and design, then
also learn marketing, etc. Make use of the 80/20 rule: use 20% of your effort to
capture 80% of the value.
Build unfair advantages for yourself. I considered my coding skills as an unfair
advantage. I can build apps very fast because I've been doing it for years. If you
don't have any unfair advantages, find one or build one today.
Build an audience. This is not for everyone, but if you can, give it a try. Build an
audience or a community of your own on Twitter/Reddit/internet forums. Anything
you do later will be much easier. The benefit is compounded. I built an audience of
100K+ followers on Twitter over the past 3 years and now that's one of my unfair
advantages too.
Ship early, ship small, ship frequently. Don't get stuck on one idea for too long if it
doesn't work. Practice shipping products more frequently to build "muscle
memory."
When building a product: focus on the core value it brings to the customer. Practice
viewing the product from the customer's point of view to avoid over-engineering.
Talk to your customers and involve them in your building process.
Be patient and be prepared for luck. It's a long-term game.
Finally, thank you for all the support!
I'm grateful to have received a lot of support from the community, whether it's on
Twitter, on the Indie Hackers forum, or from this book, especially in the early days. The
community is a big factor in my success.
I hope this book will contribute my small part back to the community, and I hope to see
more indie hackers in the future.
About indie hacking and me
What is “indie hacking”?
If you are not familiar with the term "indie hacking," here is a quick introduction.
"Indie hacking" refers to individuals building and launching online products
independently, often without external funding. It's a concept popular among developers
and entrepreneurs seeking freedom and sustainability. These individuals are called
"indie hackers" or sometimes "indie makers".
Indie hackers enjoy a freedom-based lifestyle, making a living by building and selling
their own software products to worldwide customers on the internet while they travel
the world working remotely.
Most indie hacker products are in very specific niches, well-scoped, and serve very
specific problems, for example, a tool to convert bank statements from PDF to Excel, a
macOS app to browse and interact with SQL databases, or a SaaS product that provides
website screenshots as an API.
Most indie hackers are happy with their product making just enough revenue to cover
their lifestyle. Usually, a revenue of around $10,000/month would allow them to live
comfortably from anywhere in the world.
By working alone (or in a very small team) and doing everything by themselves, from
design to code to marketing, they are very cost-efficient and able to live off a level of
revenue that most VC-funded startups would consider "unsustainable" or "not worth
pursuing". Imagine a company that makes $10,000/month revenue — that company is
considered a failure in the VC world, but if a single person can achieve that revenue
while living in a low living cost region like Bali, Indonesia, it's considered a huge success.
However, being an indie hacker doesn't mean their products are always small and niche.
Some indie hackers have built incredibly well-designed, high-quality, large scale
products that are used by big companies and make hundreds of thousands or even
millions of dollars in revenue per year. Just some examples:
Damon Chen created Testimonial.to and grew it to $33K/month revenue with no
employee.
Bannerbear, a marketing image automation SaaS created by Jon Yongfook, reached
$10K/month in one year.
Plausible Analytics, a Google Analytics alternative, was built by Uku and Marko,
reaching $26K/month revenue in 2021.
Pieter Levels, who is considered the most influential indie hacker, created several
software products, including PhotoAI — a service to create AI photos making
$130K/month, RemoteOK — a job board making $35K/month, Nomads.com making
$22K/month, and several other successful products. Making a total of $240K per
month revenue, all while working alone.
Photopea, a photo editor web app similar to Photoshop, built by Ivan from Ukraine,
making $200K/month revenue.
When an indie product reaches a certain level of success, some indie hackers start to
build out a team of their own to continue growing the product, while some prefer to work
with contractors while staying small and free.
All indie hackers have one thing in common: they all value independence and freedom,
and they all have the same starting phase where they kickstart the business from
scratch without a big team and VC funding.
A new way for individuals to make a living
The whole indie hacking thing became possible relatively recently.
The payment infrastructure innovation, which started with PayPal in the early 2000s,
made it possible to "move money" via the internet. The platform economy emerged
around 2005-2010 with new marketplaces and distribution models like Etsy, Shopify,
app stores (Apple, Google), and FastSpring. Stripe was founded in 2010, making
payments accessible and developer-friendly, now supported 45 countries in 2025.
Paddle was founded in 2012, helping even more companies around the world (support
150+ countries) sell subscription software much more easily.
The global developments in FinTech, banking, and regulations around 2010-2016 made
it easy to set up new businesses online and overseas, with easier tax handling and
remittance. Stripe Atlas made it easy for anyone to incorporate a new company.
The tech infrastructure became easier with platform-as-a-service providers like Heroku
(2007) and Digital Ocean (2011), making it easy for anyone to run and maintain their
software without needing a big team behind them.
All of this made it easy for anyone to start their own business and sell software to the
global market today. There are no more technical barriers, no more payment
infrastructure barriers, and no more regulatory barriers. A teenager with a laptop can
now build and monetize a global business in a weekend.
I remember back in 2010 when I tried to sell software products that I'd built, the only
option was to charge customers via mobile top-up cards, and the telco company took a
50% fee on the transaction revenue. Not only that, I needed to have some "secret
network relationship" to be able to work with the telco, and I was only able to sell to
people in Vietnam. I felt that was so unfair, and I decided to make all of my products
free.
Today, 15 years later, all of the modern infrastructure has made it possible for me to sell
my software to everyone around the world at just about 5-7% transaction fee.
My first experience with Indie Hacking
Indie hacking is relatively new. I think the term itself started when Courtland Allen
popularized it with his 
Indie Hackers forum
 in 2016-2017, around the same time
when I learned about it.
Indie hacking opened my mind about a completely new way a person can make a living
without working for a company or building a startup themselves. To me at the time,
"startup" implied raising VC funds, gathering a team to build big, life-changing unicorn-
type products (the next Facebook/Uber types of thing).
The idea that one person can build a small software product, sell to worldwide
customers, and make a living from it was completely alien to me. I had heard about
people who built apps for iOS and Android and made good money from it, but I never
knew how much they made, and they never told me what they did, so I assumed it was
something in the "gray area" of breaking the law, similar to what people do with make-
money-online get-rich-quick schemes. I didn't really want to get involved in those.
Indie hacking is different. I remember reading stories from Pieter Levels and other indie
hackers at the time. They were building legitimate software products, they openly
shared about their products, how they did it, and even revenue numbers. The special
thing is they did all of that by themselves, usually just one person, without a team,
without VC funding, while traveling and living in freedom, not having to report to a boss
or investor. For the first time in my career, I was aware that it's possible to make a living
by building and selling software like that.
I have always loved building software, so I thought it was really cool if I could make a
living from doing that like these indie hackers. But for many reasons, I didn't have
enough motivation to take any action yet. At the time (2017), I was working at a startup
[26]
in Vietnam making a good salary of $3,500/month, but I was too busy making money
and upgrading my skills that I couldn't spend time thinking of anything else.
I remember trying to sign up for a Stripe account. A lot of indie hackers I had read about
online used Stripe to collect payments from customers around the world. To me, it
seemed like a must-have requirement to start this indie thing. When I learned that Stripe
didn't support businesses in Vietnam and I couldn't sign up, I gave up and didn't look
further. I assumed that without Stripe, I couldn't do what other indie hackers were doing
(which was incorrect, there are many ways, as I will share in the later chapters), so I
continued with my life.
Only until much later in 2019, when COVID hit, I was forced to work remotely, and I was
bored with the enterprise company I was working at, I started to think about indie
hacking more seriously. This time, I was living and working in Singapore, and Stripe was
available in Singapore. My dream of becoming an indie hacker was closer to reality this
time. That's when I started to give indie hacking a real attempt.
But before I write about my first attempt at indie hacking, let me give a brief introduction
about myself.
A bit about my background
I was born and raised in an average-income working class Vietnamese family. I was
lucky to have found my passion very early. I got exposed to computers via video games
from internet cafes when I was 11 and I loved computers immediately. I got my own
computer when I was 15, which was when I learned computer programming with Pascal
and Visual Basic 6, and I was obsessed with creating software.
By the time I was 17, I had created about 15 different software products that I shared
online for free. I created a website to put them on under a made-up company named
"Perfect Software". A few of these products helped me win some small prizes from local
computer and innovation competitions! I dreamed of having my own software company
in the future where I would just build software and sell it for a living.
In 2011, I went to college to study software engineering, where I learned to write code
"the right way". I also started to do freelancing on the side building Android apps and
WordPress websites. I still kept building side projects but instead of using Visual Basic 6,
I used JavaScript, PHP, C#, and all the things I learned from college.
I especially loved doing things in the frontend with JS/HTML/CSS because it was fun to
see the result of my code in the browser immediately. In my freelance projects, I got to
work with talented designers who gave me beautiful website designs to implement,
which was both challenging and fun.
I remember in one of the freelance projects, I needed to implement a design for a
wedding venue's website. I handed over my delivery and it wasn't really good — the
actual code looked about 90% similar but not exactly the same as what the designer
had created. The padding was a bit bigger, font size and letter spacing were a bit off, the
opacity level of transparent elements wasn't at the right value, things like that.
My boss asked to see me and told me something I remember forever. He told me in
Vietnamese: "
Listen, we are a web design company. However the designer has created
it, I need you to follow it to the best of your ability. Whether we can make money or not,
the difference between winning and losing comes down to just these few pixels.
" —
that's where I learned the work ethic and attention to detail when building products.
From that day on, I always made absolutely sure that all of my design implementations
were pixel-perfect whenever a designer handed them to me. Thanks to that, I slowly
picked up some skills in UX and UI design. I started to notice patterns that designers
always used — the spacing, colors, contrast, alignments, layout. It didn't make me a
designer, but it was just enough to help me tell the difference between good design and
bad design, and it also helped me land my first job as a frontend software engineer at an
outsourcing company after I graduated.
For a fresh graduate in 2014-2015 in Vietnam, the obvious option for me was to join the
workforce and start the rat race, slowly climbing up the career ladder. For the majority of
fresh graduates in Vietnam at that time, being able to join the rat race was already an
achievement, so I did the same without a second thought. My dream now was to get into
big tech, like everyone else in the industry.
I ended up working as a software engineer for about 7 years. I learned a lot and enjoyed
every year of it. I learned my way from a frontend engineer to a full-stack engineer,
jumping from small outsourcing companies to startups and big slow corporates. I also
picked up a lot of skills and experiences working with all kinds of tech, from small
startup apps to large-scale global enterprise microservices.
I still love building software products. Throughout my whole career, I always had some
side projects to work on, sometimes for fun, sometimes to learn a new tech to put on my
CV. I'm that type of person who writes code for work during the day, then comes home
and continues to write code for fun at night and on weekends.
When COVID hit in 2019, I had been working and living in Singapore for about 3 years.
Being forced to work remotely, with no more commute time, and with the slow-moving
nature of a big corporate company, I was given a lot of free time to think about my future
and my career.
I realized that even though I considered myself an above-average software engineer, I
was probably not good enough to get into big tech. And even if I did, then what? Looking
at my friends who were already in big tech, I still wasn't sure that's the life I wanted to
pursue.
Having learned about indie hacking, I was now reminded of my original dream of building
my software company, but now I knew that I could do it myself the indie way without
having to build a big team or going into the traditional path of VC funding, which to me
was pretty much just another rat race. I looked at the lifestyle of the people in the Indie
Hackers podcast and forum and I just knew that's what I wanted, not a corporate job at a
big tech company.
That's how I decided to give indie hacking a try in early 2019, and it changed my life
forever.
Is indie hacking a good fit for you?
I believe anyone can be an indie hacker. These days you can learn almost anything from
the internet, and the amount of resources is unlimited. You only need a computer with
internet connection and your time.
Indie hacking is hard, but rewarding.
By talking to many people over the years, I can see some people are a great fit to be
indie hackers, while others thrive more in a team/startup/enterprise career path. No path
is better; it's just more "fitting" for each person.
Below is my idea of "not a good fit" vs “a good fit” to be an indie hacker, all of the points
I listed here have more to do with the mindset and goals more than about skills or
background of each person. Hopefully it can help clarify a few things if you are
considering following the indie path. I'm happy to see more people going indie.
To build the next unicorn
If you want to change the world and create a million/billion dollar company — going indie
is probably not the best way to do it. You will be better off making use of capital funding
from VCs and help from a lot of people to achieve that big vision.
Indie hacking is all about achieving independence and freedom for yourself by escaping
the rat race, playing your own game with your own rules. I'm not saying you can't build a
billion dollar company or the next Facebook/Uber while doing that, it's just difficult.
To make a lot of money
If you just want to get rich, indie hacking could be one way, but probably not the best
way. Most success stories you read about indie hackers who make more than
$50K/month are outliers (including myself).
According to my 
poll
 from 2,000+ people, only 50% of indie hackers actually make
any money, and less than 5% make more than $5K/month. And remember my poll is
biased, the actual real data is maybe even worse.
When it comes to the money, whether in the traditional rat race path or in the indie path,
there are outliers who makes a lot more than the majority of the whole. The top 5%
engineers from big tech makes 10 - 50 times more than average engineers, indie
hacking is similar. Donʼt look into the outliers and assume thatʼs what a typical indie
hackerʼs revenue is like. People choose the indie path largely because of the freedom,
not just the money.
The common thing I have heard in the indie hackers community is that people who quit
their well-paying jobs to work on their indie products, end up making way less than their
salary, but they are much more happier with their freedom.
If you just want to make more money, give indie hacking a try, but also consider other
options like saving, investing, working for startups with equity, etc.
To work on a flexible schedule
If you just want to have the freedom of a flexible work schedule that allows you to work
anywhere, anytime, there are plenty of easier ways to achieve that — for example, work
as a freelancer, contractor, or consultant.
To play boss
Indie hacking is not a way to be "the idea guy" where you point fingers and tell people to
build your ideas.
[27]
Most indie hackers work alone and are 100% hands-on, which means they take care of
all aspects of the product and business like coding (frontend, backend, devops,
database), design, marketing, sales, accounting, company setup, etc. If you can't (or are
not willing to) do some of that, it's probably not a good fit.
I said "probably" because you can still hire other people to do things that you don't want
to do. It's still possible, but then you need money (sometimes a lot) and you also have to
organize the team, discuss, communicate, collaborate, etc. This means you lose the
most powerful advantage of being an indie hacker: the efficiency.
You enjoy building products and seeing people use them.
Most indie hackers I know, including myself, love building products and solving
problems, even when it doesn't make any money. I loved building software products
since I was in high school, and I have always had some side projects going on during my
7 years working at companies.
The goal of indie hacking is to turn the love of building products into a career. So if you
love building products and solving problems with code, you will be a good fit.
You enjoy working on all aspects of the product and the business.
If you know backend and don't know frontend, you will need to learn frontend. If you
can't do design, you will need to learn design. Basically, you are willing to spend time
(and sometimes a bit of money) to acquire all the skills necessary to build a product
from zero.
If you already know how to build a product, you will learn how to do business. Learn how
to do marketing, how to find customers, how to sell. Then learn how to monetize, charge
customers, and generate revenue. At some point, you will probably have to learn how to
set up a company and a bank account, learn how to create invoices, do accounting and
taxes, etc.
There are just a million things to learn and to do as an indie hacker - some are
interesting, some are boring, but you have to do them all. With the help from AI these
days, you can learn anything, as long as you are willing to. If you don't mind that, it's a
good fit for you.
You are ok with making just enough money to sustain yourself and family.
Being an indie hacker means you can reach your unlimited potential, not limited by your
boss, your investors, performance reviews, or the job market. You can actually have a
chance to make your first million. But realistically, only a few people can do that.
Assume you are working a full-time job with a salary of $200K/year, and you go on this
indie path but can only achieve $100K/year revenue for the next 10 years, but you love
what you do, have more freedom, are happier, and can still sustain your lifestyle and
family. Would you still be ok with that?
If the answer is yes, then indie hacking is probably a good fit for you.
You enjoy working alone or in a very small team.
As mentioned earlier, most indie hackers work alone. If you struggle to deliver output
when working alone, it can be difficult for you to be an indie hacker.
Do you enjoy working and collaborating in a team with each member specialized in their
specific roles? Or do you enjoy doing everything by yourself and deciding everything? If
it's the latter, you are a good fit.
For me, being able to build a product without having to have a team is a big part of why I
wanted to be an indie hacker. I enjoy working alone, I don't want meetings, I don't have
to spend time discussing or arguing with my cofounder, I don't have to take care of other
people's salaries, mental health, and their career growth.
The tradeoff I have to pay is that I have to do everything on my own, with little or no help
from other people. I probably canʼt build something big, changing the world, and Iʼm
probably wasting my “true potential”. But thatʼs ok, itʼs an acceptable tradeoff for me.
If you just want to build a successful product, get customers, get revenue, get paid, and
don't really care if you have to do it alone or with a team, I would say choosing to do it
the bootstrapped way - hire a team with your own money, or leveraging VC money - is
not a bad option.
What I learned
Indie hacking is an alternative way to make a living that doesnʼt involve working for
companies or building a VC funded unicorn startup.
Indie hackers learn and handle all aspects of their business, make a living from
building and selling software products, prioritize personal freedom and
sustainability, not necessary to get rich or to change the world.
You don't need special skills or background to start - just a computer, internet
connection, and willingness to learn everything needed to build and run a business.
First attempt at indie hacking: the log
viewer app
In 2019, when I learned about indie hacking and decided to give it a try, my first product
idea was to build a log viewer desktop app. I never finished the product, but thanks to it I
have learned some important lessons. It was the most significant mindset change for me
coming into the indie hacking path, and it has helped me on all of the future products
that I have built.
Here is how it started.
How I came up with the idea
When I was at my day job, I had to process a lot of raw text log files from servers. This
involved browsing the log files, looking for patterns, filtering by regex, etc. I needed to
do that every time I had to investigate a server issue from my team.
Of course, there were a lot of command-line tools to help me do the task, and they were
working quite well. However, something in me just felt that it would be so cool if I was
able to do that with a desktop app instead of relying on command-line tools! And it
would be so much cooler if I could make money by selling this app.
The idea sounds ridiculous but it's not entirely delusional. I know that some desktop
apps were making good money just by simply providing a native, beautiful, easy-to-use
interface to their command-line counterparts. Most famously, I know that GitFork was
one of the real-world examples.
GitFork is a git client and a macOS/Windows desktop app. It allows users to interact with
git using their mouse instead of the traditional git command line. I didn't know how
much money they were making, but I knew at least they were selling licenses for $49
per user, and they had a lot of users.
So my idea of building a native desktop app to view log files wasn't that bad. Based just
on that, I started building it right away.
I learned Swift to build a native app
Among all things that I wanted the app to be, the most important thing was for it to be a
native macOS app. A true native macOS app would be fast, lightweight, beautiful, sleek,
and follow all the platform UX/UI standards. I love native macOS apps. GitFork is a native
app too.
Nowadays, too many apps are built with Electron. They are slow, sluggish, their build
files are always a minimum of a few hundred megabytes, and they always consume all
the RAM of your computer. So building a native app was a big advantage to please
users, giving them more reason to buy the product.
At the time, I didn't know how to build macOS desktop apps. So I spent months learning
Swift and macOS app development. It was extremely difficult — Apple's documentation
was terrible, and there weren't many resources and tutorials about macOS app
development on the internet, as most people only teach iOS. ChatGPT wasn't a thing
back then.
The only decent resource for learning macOS dev I found was
hackingwithswift.com
, but even that didn't cover all aspects. I resorted to looking for
examples from open source projects on GitHub and learning from them. A lot of copy-
paste was involved.
It was so painful that at some point, I was thinking, when I become good at this, I'll
create a preset containing lots of examples with working code that people can just copy
and learn from. I even got distracted and made a website for it on Google Sites, but then
decided to abandon it and go back to focusing on the app.
[28]
I got distracted for a while and created this website
Eventually, I got a hang of Swift and was able to build some UI parts of the product.
Nothing worked yet, but at least I had the UI with panels, buttons, textboxes, etc. I could
drag and drop, resize the window, scroll through some text, open/close the side panel,
etc.
I started to think about implementing the core logic of the app. And this is where things
started to go wrong.
Building the product
Coming from a hard-core engineering background, I wanted everything to be perfect.
I started writing unit tests for everything, even the UI part. I learned how to write tests in
Xcode and filled everything with tests until I reached more than 90% test coverage. I
wanted the codebase to be stable and "long term."
Now to the core logic part. The app needed to read a lot of log files, and these log files
were huge. I wanted it to be able to read terabytes of data without hiccups, seek
through chunks of the log files easily without lag or crashes. It was hard, but to me,
because it was hard, I wanted to do it even more.
I could have written all of the file reading logic (the core module) in Swift because I was
building for macOS at the time, but then I thought: what if I want to have a Windows and
Linux version of the app in the future? I can't spend all of my effort building the most
difficult part of my product in Swift, only to find out that I have to do it again in C# when
building for Windows, or whatever language it would be to make it run on Linux, right?
I didn't think Swift was the ideal language to build the core module of the product. It was
good for building UI for the macOS app, but to get the most value out of my time, I
needed to build the core module in a language that I could reuse for the future Windows
and Linux versions.
So I decided to use Golang for the core module. At the time, I had just finished delivering
a huge project with Golang in my job, and I loved Golang so much, I thought it was the
perfect language for this module.
But now that the core module was in Golang, and the UI was in Swift (or C#), I needed a
way to communicate between these two. I knew exactly the right tool for the job:
Protobuf and gRPC. Then, I needed to define an interface that would allow the UI and
the core module to talk to each other, and the interface would be reused in the future
Windows and Linux builds. Perfect.
So now my product would have this beautiful architecture of a shared core module
written in Golang that could read super big log files with efficient memory management
logic, a native and fast UI built with Swift, and a communication layer powered by
Protobuf and gRPC.
Reading this, you may think that I was kidding, but I'm not. It was exactly what I did.
I spent months and months building every part of the product, one by one, then putting
it all together, fixing bugs, and planning new features. And of course, everything had to
come with its corresponding unit tests, ensuring a 90% test coverage target.
Like most software engineers, I loved spending time on these challenging technical
problems. I dove into the most exciting part of the project and worked on it day and
night, whenever I had free time.
After 6 months on the project, I had about half of the product. Some parts worked well
— it could open and browse through some decent large log files with basic features.
However, some other parts were completely non-functional; I hadn't had time to glue it
all together yet. There were also a lot of bugs.
But then I realized another problem. I got bored.
Then I lost motivation
It was fun to solve the technical challenges, but when I got past that, I realized I was no
longer interested in the rest of the project, the "boring parts": things like creating a
release build, creating an installer, building the onboarding flow, handling updates, and a
million things in the UI that needed to be polished.
I still had to create a website to introduce the product. I hadn't got a single idea on how
to find customers to sell this app to. I was having too much fun solving the technical
challenges and forgot the reason I started it: to make money from selling a product.
I considered starting to work on marketing and building a website for it, but I didn't have
a functional product yet — it was only half-baked, I couldn't sell that.
But if I continued to build the product, I couldn't see myself finishing it for another 6
months. This complicated architecture I had put together still needed a lot more time,
and until everything was finished and glued together, the app was just not functional. I
couldn't let anyone use it; I couldn't even use it myself.
And then I also figured out another existential crisis of the product: nobody really
needed to open big log files on their desktop. All log files are usually from a remote
server, and nobody stores terabytes of log files on their computer for my app to open.
With that, I started dreaming about ways to address this. I had already put too much
effort into the product, so I had to make it work. I thought about setting up a server-
client architecture, then using some sort of real-time streaming technique to stream the
files back to the client — it would be an amazingly fun technical problem to solve! But
then I realized if I continued to go with it, the product would take much, much longer to
finish, I was just inventing new fun technical problems to satisfy my curiosity again.
I got extremely frustrated and slowly lost my motivation. Then one day, my day job got
me busy, and I couldn't spend my nighttime on the project for a while. A few more weeks
went by, and I had completely abandoned it.
And that was my first failure when attempting indie hacking. For the first time, I
understood I needed to switch my mindset completely.
The first lesson I learned was to not over-engineer my product.
What I learned: The first mindset
change
Code is a tool
If you are like me - a typical software engineer - when switching to indie hacking, over-
engineering is the biggest enemy you have to overcome.
Since college, I was trained to seek and solve difficult technical problems, and I got
reward for it with the learning, the experience, and I could put it on my CV for the next
job seeking opportunities.
However, when going to the indie hacking, I realized I needed to change this mindset. I
spent more than 6 months to build the log viewer app with so many lines of code and a
lot of fun technical challenges to solve, but in the end, I donʼt have a functional product.
My product wasnʼt really a useful one, I was just invented a solution for a non-existing
problem to satisfy my technical curiosity.
In reality, my customers donʼt care if my product is written in Golang or Swift, they donʼt
care if the product is constructed with a strong architecture behind it or the “long term”
maintainable of the code base. They donʼt care about the code base at all. Only the
developers care about having a beautiful and clean code base. Customers only care
about solving their problems.
Building software for enterprises or mid-size companies is totally different from building
software for your indie product. You need to take a totally different approach; it's like a
complete mindset switch.
Many times, indie hackers who come from developer backgrounds fall in love with
technical challenges they want to solve just so they can play with the tech. They
constantly come up with new ways to do things and want to try new
frameworks/libraries/paradigms all the time, even coming up with their own new
frameworks sometimes. These are all distractions and just playing around.
Becoming an indie hacker means you'll have to sacrifice your satisfaction of solving
challenging technical problems and learn to appreciate solving real-world, day-to-
day pain points using the simplest tech possible.
Just use the simplest technical stack that you can. Stop thinking about how to scale to 1
million users, stop thinking about how to apply Docker, K8S, Kafka, Cassandra, Redis,
micro services, Protobuf, GraphQL, and all other fancy tech that you put in your CV over
the years.
Challenge yourself to build the product with the least amount of tech and dependencies
possible.
Building an indie product is different:
It's not about
 showing off how much tech you know how to use to impress your
future employers or to put in your CV.
It's not about
 setting up a scalable architecture that's easy to maintain, well
documented, and has 90% test coverage.
It's not about
 ensuring the product can serve 1 million users with 99.99% uptime.
It's not about
 experimenting with a new programming language, framework, library,
technique, programming paradigm, or architecture.
If you are thinking about any of those when building your indie product, you are not
being serious about building the indie product. You just want to try new things, learn
new things, or just have fun. It's not wrong, just not the right approach for indie
products.
Building an indie product is about:
What problem you are solving and how to solve it with code.
What's the least amount of effort to put together that code and make a product to
solve the problem.
When can you start showing the product to users so they can solve the problem.
The customers don't care what tech you use; they just care how you're solving the
problem for them.
If you spend too much time on over-engineering your product, you will have less time to
work on other things. Despite you trying to make it “perfect”, the product codebase will
become much more complicated with a tons of tools and dependencies that will soon be
outdated. You will spend more time on updating the tech stack and “improving your
workflow” than actually just doing the works that matter.
When I was building the log viewer app, I totally fell in love with solving the technical
challenges that I had put for myself, and I forgot to spend my time and attention on what
matters: a log viewer on a desktop computer is not a real problem; nobody stores
terabytes of log files on their laptop ever.
Even after realizing that, I went into denial mode by thinking "ok, then I'll make the app
able to remotely connect to a server to view the logs there," then I spent more time
thinking about the architecture of how to connect to remote servers, get log files, deal
with file permissions, streaming, batching log files, buffering, etc. - you see what I did
there, I invented new technical challenges and fell in love with them again.
I did all of that and I hadn't put a single second into thinking about how to get
customers. I worked on the product for 6 months, then got bored, and abandoned it.
Maybe I over-engineered because I thought it was the right way to build software, or
maybe I over-engineered because I wanted to boost my ego so I could later show off to
my coworkers and tell them how many challenges I had to get through to build the app.
In the end, I learned a big lesson: 
A product doesn't have to be insanely difficult
technically to have value for its users.
A simple product that solves a painful problem is much more valuable than a technically-
challenging product that solves a nonexistent problem.
Most products are not that difficult in term of engineering and tech, Iʼll go as far as
saying 99% of indie product has very boring tech. So the chance that you happen to
stumble on an extremely difficult technical problem or problems that require new fancy
tech while building the MVP of your product is quite low, you are most likely just
convincing yourself.
How to stop over-engineering
Start by learning to write simple code.
To avoid over-engineering, you must learn not only how to build products, but build
them in a simple way.
Some developers tend to try out as many advanced technologies as possible to learn
and gain experience because they naturally like to learn more. This makes their code
become more and more complicated because they think complexity means better.
The reality is that the more senior a developer is, the simpler their code is.
A senior developer can build a product with such a simple codebase and architecture
that any junior can read, understand, and continue developing it with ease.
For example, I used to use all type of tech for my side projects because I believe it is the
“right tool for the job”. A simple API app I made used NodeJS, deployed on AWS Lamda
(serverless), using DynamoDB (also serverless and “infinitely” scaleable), Redis for
cache, ElasticSearch for full text search, etc…, then in the code I would use Flow type
annotation to add types to my JavaScript, then in the process of building the actual
project, I also developed my own project template, published at least several useless
npm packages that I was “sure” Iʼm going to reuse it at some future projects, I installed
all the beta and alpha version of the latest framework so I can enjoy the “latest tech” at
the time, etc.
Now if I were to do it again, everything is just one simple JavaScript app, minimal npm
packages used, and a MySQL database for everything.
The next thing you can do to stop over-engineering is to change your mindset to focus
more on the actual value that the product brings to your users, and treat code as a tool
to achieve that goal.
Some developers treat code as a form of art and the final output of their work. These are
good developers and our industry needs people like this; however, if you want to be an
indie hacker, you should not be this type of developer.
Don't treat code as a form of art; treat it as a tool. The final output should be the value it
brings to the customers. No value delivered to customers means no revenue and no
business.
Shift your mindset to focus more on the actual product value. It will help transform how
you approach technical problems and help you avoid over-engineering.
Next, use the right amount of engineering at the right time. Over-engineering is bad but
"right-amount" engineering is good.
If you are a senior developer, you should know when is the right time to use the right
amount of "engineering" to solve a problem.
A problem can be as simple or as complex as you want it to be, depending on the
current state of the product. So choose the engineering solution that you find fits the
current situation. Don't use a sledgehammer to crack a nut. Let me clarify with an
example.
Let's say you have a SaaS product and a customer wants to have an "Import Emails"
feature because they have hundreds of emails and don't want to add them one by one
manually via the admin dashboard. Now this feature can be interpreted into different
levels of technical difficulty as follows:
Simplest version (Level 1):
Let user enter/paste the list of new emails in a textbox, separated by new lines.
We need 1 API endpoint to receive the list as JSON, validate, then run one SQL
query to bulk insert them all in one go, then show the success message in the UI.
Maximum can support up to 1,000 emails at a time; requests may run as long as 2-5
seconds.
Moderate version (Level 2):
Let user select a CSV/Excel file containing the emails to import.
An API receives this file, validates, runs the import SQL in the background, returns
status "importing" to the user.
Once the import script is done, send an email to the user notifying them that the
import is complete.
Maximum can support up to 10,000 emails; background job can be interrupted if the
request somehow crashes, in such case all emails are aborted or rolled back and
the user has to import again.
Advanced version (Level 3):
Let user select a CSV/Excel file containing the emails.
Let the user preview the file, mapping the email fields (if any).
An API receives this file, uploads it to S3, then creates a background job, then
returns "pending" status to the user.
Background job picks up the file, runs the import script, saves to database, then
updates status to "finished".
Background worker logic to ensure no files are missed, no files are processed more
than once, retry and resume if the import fails or server crashes, handle partial
import where some emails are imported successfully but some fail, compile
progress reports and save to database, delete the file from S3 after the task is
done, etc.
Show the import status and progress to the user via the admin panel.
Maximum can support up to 10 million emails per task.
As you can see from this example, something as simple as "import email" can be
interpreted to be a single API call, or an entire background job worker setup. If you are
an indie hacker, what do you do?
Assume you only have about 100 customers on your platform and your customers never
need to import anything more than a few hundred emails at a time; obviously it would be
a waste of time to go with the Level 2 or Level 3 solution. The Level 1 solution can be
done in a an hour, while Level 2 or 3 may require several days or weeks.
Start implementing with the simplest solution that you can immediately deliver to the
customers, then upgrade to higher levels only when you need it.
The "right time" is the keyword. How do you choose the right level of technical
complexity given the current state of the product? That's where you will need to use
your expertise in software engineering: choosing the right trade-offs.
Even though you pick the Level 1 implementation, you are aware of the higher solutions
and when the product grows to a certain level, you know what to do next when that
happens.
As an indie hacker, you have a deep understanding not only from the technical side, but
also the business side. This allows you to pick the right trade-off and take the right tech
debt that both help you deliver value to the customers fast, while ensuring the codebase
is as clean as it could be.
Ship fast, ship small, ship frequently
Another big lesson I learned while building the log viewer app is to reduce the time it
takes to release the first version of the product.
You should slim down the product features, ship the first version to customers faster,
then release small updates one by one to improve the product over time instead of
waiting for one big bang release of the fully featured product.
The feedback loop of a product consists of a few important steps, and the "building"
step is just one small part of them.
1. 
Build the product.
2. 
Let the customer use it.
3. 
Observe, get feedback, analyze.
4. 
Come back to step one, and repeat.
Whenever you start a new product, try to close the loop as fast as possible. This means
you should not spend too much time on step 1 (build the product) because there are still
2–3 more major steps ahead.
I believe every product can be simplified down to a version that can be built within a few
weeks or even days. The first version of your product should be simple and do one thing
very well. You should try to get the MVP released as soon as possible so it immediately
becomes useful when a user uses it. Then you will iterate on that by releasing updates,
listening to your customers' feedback, and keep doing it over and over again.
Another reason to build fast is your motivation. You see, motivation can only last for so
long, and there isn't a better way to boost your motivation than seeing other people use
your product and getting revenue. When you are motivated, you are more productive
and creative, and it will help you build a better product.
I was doing everything wrong with the log viewer app. I spent way too much time on
building the product (and over-engineering it). I actually never got to the next step,
which is delivering the product to the customer — I didn't even have a website. If I had
forced myself to ship faster, maybe I wouldn't have fallen into the over-engineering trap,
and I might have spent some time talking with my customers about the product, quickly
realizing the product wasn't what they wanted.
How to ship fast
To ship fast and ship small, there are two most important things that I always do: scope
down your MVP and break down your tasks.
Scoping down your MVP needs practice and discipline. We developers often spend way
too much time thinking about all the wonderful features we can add to the product, but
just because we can doesn't mean we should.
For the first version of the product, the MVP, always narrow down the scope to only one
single valuable feature, the core feature that defines your product.
For example, I have been thinking about building an email client for busy people to read
and scan hundreds of emails faster. It will eventually become a fully featured email client
where users can read emails, write new emails, reply to email threads, get analytics, AI-
assisted summaries, etc., but I will ship the first version with only the "read email"
feature.
I would focus 100% on making the "scan and read email" feature special and valuable,
something that users can never achieve with any other email clients and they have to
download my app just to read emails instead of whatever app they are using.
The reason is that my vision for the app is to help busy people, and busy people have a
lot of emails, so scanning and reading emails is the most important thing my app would
do, and that's what I'm going to ship first. Then only after that, I'll start to slowly add
more features with every new update.
The most popular excuse I hear is "my idea can't be simplified further otherwise it
doesn't have any value." I'm sure there are exceptional cases, but in most cases, you
can break down your "big value" into smaller sets of "small value" features, and focus
on delivering those first.
Once you are able to ship the MVP fast, then you focus on shipping small and shipping
frequently. It means you deliver updates incrementally one by one to the customers.
Instead of releasing a 2.0 version that brings a whole new set of features all at once
every 6 months, deliver each feature one by one every week.
The best way to do it is to break down your tasks. When I work on a big feature, I always
try to split it into multiple smaller tasks or phases where I can release individual phases
that can already be used by customers. Each task or phase should never last longer
than 1–2 weeks. Most of the time, I break down tasks to things that I can complete
within one sitting (2–4 hours).
This helps me reduce the risk of breaking the system because of a big change in the
product, and even when it happens, it's easier to control and narrow down the issue if
you release each small piece one at a time. It also helps to monitor the reaction of the
customers, so if I were delivering something they don't want, I can quickly validate it. I
think a smaller and faster feedback loop is the best way to build product.
Delivering the product to the hands of your users fast and frequently will help you see all
the nuanced steps in between that you may have missed. In some cases, it can actually
shape your product or change the direction of your product entirely. As an indie hacker,
moving fast and iterating fast will be your advantage compared to the big giant
competitors out there.
Shipping fast doesn't mean you have to compromise quality.
In terms of quality control, no matter how carefully you write code or test it, bugs will
always happen. Instead of spending too much time on architecting, planning, setting up
automation tests, unit tests, etc., I think it's much more important to stay responsive and
able to fix issues fast when a customer reports them. There should be a balance so that
you don't ship sloppy code to production, but also don't spend too much time on
coding/planning. Of course you can have both, but if I have to choose, I will prioritize fast
iteration.
Building fast also allows you to catch short-term trends and hype that's good for short-
term engagement building, or if you get lucky, that short-term trend may turn your
product into something big.
What I learned
A valuable product doesnʼt have to be one that difficult or challenging to build in
term of technical.
Customer only care about solving their problems, they donʼt care about the inner
working of the product.
Treat code as a tool to solve customerʼs problems, code is not the final output of
your work.
Validate your product to see if it really solving a real problem.
Scope down your product to build and deliver it to customers quickly, then iterate
from their feedback.
DevUtils: My first internet revenue
DevUtils is my first successful product ever. I considered it "successful" because it was
the first software product that I sold a license for with actual internet money. Later, it
reached a consistent ~$5,000 per month in revenue.
It took a long time until DevUtils reached the $5,000/month mark. Even at the time when
I quit my job, it was only generating $300 to $400 per month. However, the process of
building DevUtils and finding customers for it was the most significant part of my
journey.
I think going from $0 to $1,000/month is the most difficult part for every indie hacker —
it was the same for me, and DevUtils was the product that started it. I learned another
big lesson as an indie hacker while trying to grow the product.
Here is a detailed story on how I built DevUtils.
Came up with the idea
It's around May, 2020.
Feeling a bit burned out from the previous failure with the log viewer app, I took some
time off from coding side projects to recover. I still had a full-time job at the time, so I
wasn't really rushed. After a few weeks of gaming, watching movies, and listening to the
Indie Hackers podcast, I gained motivation again.
