From what I learned in the Indie Hackers podcast and its forum, building a SaaS is the
holy grail of indie hackers — it looks so easy and there is so much money in it.
But for some reason, I still wanted to build a native macOS app. There was something
about it that made me really want to do it. I love using native macOS apps and it would
be so cool if I could make one. Apps like GitFork, TablePlus, and Sublime are some
examples. I wanted to build something like that.
Building a SaaS is cool but I didn't know anything about SaaS or have any ideas in that
area. I also didn't use a lot of SaaS myself back then. I got paid a good salary but I was
so cheap I would never spend $8/month on a SaaS, and because of that I didn't have a
lot of insights and ideas.
I wanted to build something that I could use on my own. Learning from the previous
mistake, if I don't use the product on my own, I will lose motivation.
When I was building the log viewer app, I never used it for any "actual" usage.
I was developing it on my personal laptop, which doesn't have any log files that I wanted
to inspect. All the important and relevant log files are from my work computer, and they
are all stored on servers. And even if I got the app on my work computer, it's not like I
could access them remotely because of all the security and privacy restrictions.
So of all the times I ever used the log viewer app, it was "fake usage". I was just testing
my app with random log files. For the entire 6 months of building the app, I never
benefited from it even once.
So this time, I wanted to build something that I would use every day and get benefit from
it everyday. It's the best motivation I can create for myself, and it's also a way to validate
if the product has real value. If I'm finding the product useful, there must be someone
like me who would also find it useful, right?
That's how I decided to pick the "developer toolbox app" out of my idea list.
I have this idea list that I have been keeping and updating over the years. It's mostly side
project ideas, listing things that I find annoying during the day and maybe can be fixed
or improved with a product or a tool. Most of them are just excuses to try out new tech,
but some of them are actually a bit useful.
The initial idea was to build an app that could help me quickly convert UNIX time to
human-readable time. For example: "1749028339" would get converted to "Wed Jun 04
16:12:19 +0700 2025", or "23 seconds ago", or "2025-06-04T09:12:19Z".
If you are a developer, you've probably needed to do this at least a million times in your
career. I usually go to Google and search for "unix time converter", click on the first
result, then paste my timestamp to it. The whole process takes about 5 seconds each
time.
I am the kind of person who wants to optimize and squeeze out every last drop of
productivity in my day, and I knew that this kind of stuff should take like 1 second max.
For example, when I see a UNIX time like "1749028339", I can just copy it to my
clipboard, then press a hotkey, and then something will happen and I should
immediately see the human-readable format. Simple and fast. I knew this was
something that could be done with a macOS app.
But then I thought, there are a lot of tasks like this where we can save time in the same
way. Think of the last time you needed to decode a Base64 string, or inspect a JWT
token, or format a JSON string. If you are a developer, you probably need to do that even
more frequently than converting a UNIX time!
The app should be smart so that if it sees that I copy a UNIX time, it should convert it; if
it sees a valid JSON string, it should format it; if it sees a Base64 string, it should try to
decode it, etc. Everything is automated — this saves me a few more seconds by not
having to select the right tool every time. Besides, by using the app, I can avoid pasting
my JSON string or JWT token to a random website on the internet, which could be a
privacy problem!
That's what I had in my idea list at the time. I didn't come up with all of this suddenly; it
happened over a long period of time, observing and collecting the pain points from my
day-to-day activities. This "developer toolbox app" idea was prioritized way below the
log viewer app. It seemed to me (at the time) that this "developer toolbox app" was too
easy to build, trivial to the point of being embarrassing, because it didn't involve any
fancy tech or challenges.
But I had learned the lesson. This time I wanted to build something simple and useful,
that didn't have to be technically impressive, and that I could use myself. That was all I
needed to start building DevUtils, my developer toolbox macOS app.
Building the first version
Other than learning a big lesson of over-engineering from the log viewer app, I actually
learned a lot of knowledge about Swift and macOS development. That was the only
thing that I didn't feel I wasted my time on.
When starting to build DevUtils, I was able to do it much faster, reusing old code,
spending less time on Google, and doing fewer rabbit hole diggings. ChatGPT still
wasn't a thing back then, so I had to write all the code myself, but learning from open-
source helped a lot.
I didn't write a single test in the codebase this time. The last time I did it with the log
viewer app, it was a complete waste of time. There are no reasons to aim for high test
coverage if it doesn't serve a purpose.
In my opinion, tests are only useful after the product has reached a certain size, and the
stability of the app becomes more important than the speed of development. It can also
be useful when you work within a big team.
Being an indie hacker switched my mindset. Software changes all the time, there's no
point in writing tests for a function, then finding out you need to change the function the
next day and rewrite the tests again. This applies from the smallest unit of code like a
function, to bigger things like a feature, a module, and sometimes the entire product.
I don't even know if the product is going to make money. There's no point in having 90%
test coverage and then the product makes $0 revenue. If I were to build this app to learn
something or to try some new test framework or practice my unit test writing skills, then
yeah I would write tests. But this time, I'm trying to make indie hacking work, by having
revenue.
I also needed to build fast, as my motivation only lasts for a while. It's probably only a
few weeks before my day job gets busy again, and whenever that happens, I'd come
home late and super tired, unable to focus at night. And then I'll get distracted and
maybe it will make me give up.
So I focused on removing all the unnecessary steps and only building the actual features
that matter the most. Within a few weeks, I had the first version, which I would call the
MVP. I could open the app and let it run in the background, then copy a UNIX time, press
a hotkey, and immediately see the converted result. I started using it in my day job right
away.
It feels incredible to use your own product. Not only do you benefit directly from it as a
user, but you also become your own "customer". I have insider knowledge on what I
need, what features I want the app to have, and I notice all the small details during the
flow of using the app.
I take note of bugs and ideas for the product while using it in my day job, then I come
back to improve the product when I'm at home, then create the next build, and repeat.
Thinking about finding customers
When DevUtils started to become a "proper" product with an icon (that I designed
myself) and a way to start at launch with macOS, I started thinking about how to sell the
app. It only had 3 features back then: convert UNIX time, decode Base64, and format a
JSON string.
I created a simple website so I could easily share it with friends and coworkers. The
website literally had one title, one screenshot of the app, and a download button.
Everyone could go there and download the app; I didn't bother to add a paywall or
anything.
When I was using the app at work, a few coworkers started to see me using DevUtils and
were surprised how fast I was able to format a JSON string. I can't describe how proud I
was to tell my coworkers that I built the app myself, and they loved it. They also
contributed some ideas on small tools that I should integrate into the app.
For a moment, I thought word-of-mouth would be a great way to get people to know
about DevUtils. So I intentionally put the app name "DevUtils" in a corner of the main
user interface to make sure that whoever was using the app, if someone was looking at
their screen (or they captured a screenshot of the app and shared it), other people
would see the app name, the website, and they would know where to get the app.
But my plan was destroyed. COVID happened, everyone was forced to work from home.
We no longer saw each other in the office, nobody was looking at anybody's screens
anymore. It would be impossible to get word-of-mouth this way. So I started looking for
other ways to sell the product.
The first thing I needed was to have a way for people to pay. Lucky for me, I was working
in Singapore where Stripe is available. I knew Stripe was a popular payment processing
service — it had been mentioned everywhere — so I signed up for a personal account.
I had some concerns about legal issues with selling my app while having a full-time job,
so I checked my employment contract with my employer at the time and learned that as
long as I wasn't using company resources (computer, internet, work time, etc.) and
wasn't building a competitor, I was fine. I also contacted the IRS in Singapore to ask
about tax-related questions and how to declare tax for income from side projects (if I
was going to have any). The process wasn't as hard as I thought it would be, and
everything was done online.
Once I had the payment system set up with Stripe, I set the price to be $9 per license to
use the app. However, I was still so insecure about the price; I felt guilty for letting
people pay for this little simple app. I would never pay this price myself (because again, I
was dirt cheap), but I understood that for some people from somewhere else in the
world, $9 is just a coffee to them — at least someone would pay, right?
I worried so much about the pricing that I decided to make the app open source for free,
but I still allowed them to pay $9 to download the pre-built version. The idea was that I
would sell the pre-built version of the app, where people could pay $9 to download the
build and use it immediately. Everyone else who didn't want to pay could still download
the source code and compile it themselves in Xcode.
I later changed my mind about the whole open-source thing and decided to make it
closed-source again after a few months. But for a while, DevUtils was an open-source
app. A big part of the reason was that after a while, I no longer felt guilty about selling a
paid app.
DevUtils is a useful app — it saves people time, and no matter how small the usefulness
is, it's worth something. When you multiply that something with a lot of people, it
becomes a big thing. That's the beauty of selling software: I can build an app once and
sell it to a million people around the world. If people are happy giving their money in
exchange for the app, it's a fair trade, nothing to feel guilty about. I may never pay $9 to
get the app myself, but to somebody else, $9 for the time they saved is a steal.
I didn't manage to sell DevUtils to a million people, but it was the first software I had
ever sold a license for to someone on the internet. At least I made some revenue from it,
and it kicked off the chain of successful future products that I built later.
But first let me tell you about the first sale.
The first sale
I didn't really know how to find customers. The only channel that I had ever had some
success with was SEO (Search Engine Optimization), which I did a few times in the past
with my side projects. I was going to do a lot of SEO for DevUtils too, but SEO is a long-
term game. I needed to do something to get some revenue first.
I've been a reader of HN (Hacker News) for a long time. It's a news site that discusses
technical topics where most tech people hang out. On Hacker News, there is a "Show
HN" category where people post things that they have made for people to try and
comment on.
I decided to post DevUtils there and I think I got lucky — my post received a lot of
upvotes and positive comments. It stayed on the front page of Hacker News for a few
hours, with more than 200 votes. Here is the original post:
A user on Hacker News commented: "
I really appreciate apps like this that are just 'apps'
— not services, not subscriptions, just a little stand-alone, offline, limited scope app that
does what it does well. I bought a copy. Looking forward to playing around with it.
"
And with this post, I got my first ever revenue of $9 from DevUtils! It was the happiest
day of my career! I got so hyped up with all the comments, feedback, and ideas to
improve the app. I checked my Stripe dashboard the whole day.
About a week later, I posted the app again on Product Hunt — a website that features
new products. I learned about Product Hunt from the Indie Hackers podcast.
Same as when I posted on Hacker News, I didn't prepare much when posting on Product
Hunt. Simply just took a few screenshots of the app, wrote a description, and told my
story. I asked a few of my friends and my brother to help me with an upvote but that's it.
By just pure luck that to this day I still don't understand, the post got 200+ votes on
Product Hunt and ranked as the #3 product of that day.
Getting featured on Hacker News and Product Hunt was a big deal. Even for a short few
hours getting featured on the home page, there are other sites that constantly scrape
the front page of Hacker News and Product Hunt, and repost to other websites and
newsletters.
So for about a week after the posts, I still got some traffic to my website. I remember I
sold about ~100 licenses, which was around $900 of revenue from the two posts, which
was a lot to me!
I think one of the reasons DevUtils got attention is because it was a new kind of app that
people hadn't seen before. People are tired of googling and pasting their data to random
websites all the time, and this provided a new way — faster and easier — to solve their
practical problem. The app's value is clear: it saves people time.
Another reason I think that contributed to this is because DevUtils was open-source.
Product Hunt audience won't care, but Hacker News audience is famously nitpicking
and sensitive about the tech. Having the source code available makes a lot of Hacker
News people happy and I may have earned an upvote just for that, even though they
never checked out the GitHub repository.
Overall, I still think I got extremely lucky with these two posts. Previously, I posted a few
times on Hacker News and once on Product Hunt but never got any attention. This time I
got attention from both with my simple app.
However, getting lucky this one time doesn't mean much in the long term. Just about
one week later, I barely got any visitors to my website at all. And I got absolutely zero
sales the weeks after. Even though I was still hyped and continued pushing out new
features for the app, looking at the dashboard with zero sales started to get on my
nerves.
I realized something important: if I stop doing marketing, the revenue stops. Simple as
that.
My original plan with word-of-mouth was destroyed — COVID had spread to the whole
world, everyone worked from home, so I couldn't rely on that. And even if not because
of COVID, I still didn't think the word-of-mouth effect would be significant enough.
My SEO efforts were still in progress; I posted a few articles to the websites and made a
blog, but this SEO game has an extremely slow and long feedback loop. I wasn't even
really sure it would work; I would have to wait for at least 6 to 12 months to see its result.
I would still do it, but it was too risky to solely rely on SEO — I needed to do something
else too.
By this time, I knew the app was valuable, people would buy it, but I had no one to sell
to. I needed to do some kind of marketing to bring DevUtils to more eyeballs so they
would know about it, then some of them would buy it.
I got lucky with posting on Hacker News and Product Hunt, but I couldn't get lucky again
anytime I wanted. I definitely couldn't post on those two websites again all the time,
otherwise people would call me spam and hate the product. There were some more
smaller sites I could post to, but I would eventually run out of places to post. This was
not a long-term way to do it.
I needed to do a lot of marketing work.
Doing marketing
I started to split my limited free time to building and marketing. On one hands, I still have
a lot of ideas and features I want DevUtils to have, itʼs a long list. On the other hand, I
needed to find customers, without customers, there is no revenue. No revenue means
no business, and this time I want to build a business, not just another side project.
I learned from the Indie Hackers podcast that people find all sort of ways to sell their
product. Some do cold emails, some run paid ads, some do SEO and content marketing,
there are just so many ways. I tried most of them.
First off, on the SEO front, I consistently writing new articles on DevUtils website under
the blog section, these articles focusing on explaining the technical concepts that are a
bit relevant to the app, then promote the app at the end of each article. I created a
separate page for each tool in the app. UNIX time converter has its own page, JSON
formatter has its own page, etc., auto generated from the list of features, descriptions,
screenshots of the app.
I listed the app on the official macOS App Store, this helps increase some visibility to the
app. I also find out about Setapp - a marketplace for premium macOS apps, so I submit
my app to Setapp, after a lot of back and forth talking with the Setapp team in the
approval process, DevUtils got approved! Setapp turns out to be a significant revenue
source for DevUtils years later!
I joined an app bundle program called Bundle Hunt, where they bundle DevUtils with
other macOS apps and sell at a discounted price. I had to agree to sell a license for
cheap at $4 per license and they also take 50% of the revenue, so I get $2 per license
sold. But in exchange, they will help marketing the app to a lot of new users, which I
desperately needed at the time.
I was still dreaming of having the word-of-mouth effect, but to do that I need a certain
number of existing users. So I would do anything to get people to notice and look at
DevUtils.
The Bundle Hunt campaign end up making me $2,298. It was some decent cash, but
more importantly, I got 1,149 new customers, who will help me to spread DevUtils even
more (or so I hope).
I also joined a campaign on App Sumo to sell the app as a lifetime deal. To be clear, the
pricing is already a lifetime deal, so it wasnʼt hard to list it on App Sumo. I got a few more
hundreds customers from it.
Speaking of pricing, I increased the price a few times during this period as I add more
and more features. From $9 to $15 to $29. I also created 3 separate plans based on the
number of devices the user can use the app on. I improved the website a lot with more
screenshots, demo video, marketing copy, FAQs, documentations, etc. These activities
also counted as marketing (it helps with SEO when the website has more content).
From the Setapp marketplace I joined, I was invited to a Slack community of all app
developers who have their app listed on Setapp. I didnʼt use this Slack community as my
marketing channel, but I connected with some people in the community like Philip Young
who was building Session, a macOS productivity app, and Noah Tran, who was building
Proxyman - a developer tool app like mine.
From these friends, I learned more ways to do marketing.
I get on Twitter and start tweeting about DevUtils, hoping to reach more people. I
contacted a few newsletter authors telling them my story and asking them nicely to
feature my app in exchange for a free license, a lot of them were happy to do so (some
do it and still buy a license to support me, which I am forever grateful). Among the
newsletters I submitted, DevUtils was featured in a big one named “iOS Dev Weekly”,
which brought me a lot of sales!
Around the time of Black Friday, I also participated by creating a 50% discount code,
then post the deals on Reddit and other sites that list Black Friday deals. There was a
few popular GitHub repository that gather Black Friday deals every year, I created a PR
to list DevUtils on those.
I also run paid Google Ads, only $100 (because again, I was cheap), but it didnʼt work
out. I felt like it was really expensive, and all the traffic bounce immediately, like they are
all bots.
A Long-term Distribution Channel
Overall, I tried almost everything to do marketing for DevUtils. Some things worked and
brought revenue (like that Bundle Hunt campaign and Setapp), but some didn't work
and I wasted a lot of time.
One thing I realized in common: most of the activities I did were one-time types of
activities, and they didn't always work. I can only run Black Friday discounts once a year.
I can only reach out to sponsorships one by one hoping to get featured, or I would have
to pay to get sponsored. Whether I pay or not, I still have no control over whether the
campaign is going to work. If I pay for sponsorship, it can get expensive very soon, and I
wasn't sure if I could make enough revenue to offset the initial cost.
I have already listed the app on all the websites where it can be listed, joined all the
marketplaces I can join.
Whenever I stop doing marketing, sales slow down and eventually stop. The word-of-
mouth effect I was hoping for is still nowhere to be found, and SEO is still slow.
I felt like I was pushing a boulder up a hill. If I continue to push marketing, it may
generate some revenue, but if I stop, it goes back to zero. I also wasn't really enjoying
doing all those marketing activities, and it started to feel really exhausting.
A few months after I launched DevUtils on Hacker News, excluding all the major
marketing outliers, DevUtils was making less than $100 per month. Around February
2021, I started to rethink my strategy.
What I learned about product and
distribution
A good product is not enough
One of the big lessons I learned from building DevUtils is that without a distribution
channel, no matter how good the product is, there will be no revenue.
I know for a fact that DevUtils is a valuable product. If I show it to 100 people, about 1 to
3 people will buy the product (or you can say it has about 1-3% conversion rate). This
has been proven from my experience while showing the product on Hacker News,
Product Hunt and from other marketing activities.
My problem now is finding a way to show the product to more people, and it's not
something I'm particularly good at. For all my life and my career, everything I do and
every skill I have acquired is around building products: coding, frontend, backend,
engineering, devops, design, etc. But now I realize that without the skills to market and
sell the product, I won't have a sustainable business.
Now, I understand that I can fix my lack of sales and marketing skills with money. One
can solve a lot of problems just by throwing money at it. I can hire marketing people, or
pay to get sponsored, or buy paid ads, hire influencers, sales people, etc. But for many
reasons, I didn't want to go on this path.
I'm obsessed with staying profitable. I don't want to spend too much when I wasn't sure
if I can make enough revenue to cover the cost. I actually tried to buy paid ads from
Google for $100 and it didn't make a single sale, so I didn't want to go further.
Knowing what I know now, I think it was the right decision not spending money on
marketing. DevUtils' price and conversion rate were too low to cover the cost of running
paid ads, or sponsorships, or any other paid marketing activities. My Customer
Acquisition Cost was too high.
At the time, my thinking was simply that spending money on marketing is a big bet that I
wasn't ready to make for DevUtils, because I wasn't really sure if it would really work. I
was also not in a rush. I still had a full-time job with stable income, there was no reason
to take that big bet. Instead, I looked for other options.
Organic growth doesn't always work
Another fact that I knew at the time is that a product can grow big without spending a
single dollar on marketing. I had heard about it before and had seen one example that
was very close to me.
TablePlus is a database explorer macOS app, created by one person who was also a
fellow Vietnamese. DevUtils would be in the same category as TablePlus because both
are desktop macOS apps for developers. They were able to grow to more than 100,000
customers, selling licenses at $99 per license, and they spent zero dollars on marketing
according to their official blog post in 2018.
TablePlus was able to do this by simply creating a great product. A product that is so
good that it makes their customers happy. Then word-of-mouth kicks in, people start to
tell other people about the product, and it grows naturally. All they need to do is to keep
improving the product and make existing customers happy, then their customers will do
marketing for them.
Honestly, it's the best type of marketing. It only has a few drawbacks: word-of-mouth is
something you can't control. It takes a lot of time for the momentum to build up, and it's
not guaranteed to work.
I dreamed of achieving organic growth with DevUtils just like how they did it with
TablePlus. I poured my heart into every small detail of the app and made it the best
product I could. I had done some marketing work for the past few months to get to
around 2,000 initial users. I strongly believed that with these initial users and time,
DevUtils would start to grow organically.
However, I didn't want to just sit there and wait. If I wanted to increase my chance of
success, I needed to do more. I didn't want to build another product because if I did, I
would have the same problem as I do with DevUtils — I don't have an effective way to
market and sell it. I needed to upgrade my marketing and sales skills.
I needed a way to continuously generate traffic to my website passively. No traffic, no
new customers. And I needed to find something that I could do and enjoy doing,
something I could do for the long term, something that could help me reach people
without having to pay someone to do it.
Ways to build a distribution channel
There are so many ways to build a distribution channel, the trick is to find one that works
well for you. By watching other successful indie hackers, I know for certain that there are
plenty of options to attempt.
Uku and Marko built Plausible and used content marketing, especially blog posts, to
grow their product to $83K/month.
Danny Postma used SEO to grow his Headshot Pro product to $300K/year.
Nico used paid ads to grow his TalkNotes app, sold for $200K.
Dashiell used cold email to grow her product WishTender, reached $83K/month in
the first year.
A distribution channel is something that you can use to help market and sell the product.
It could be your skills (like running ads) or some assets you own (like owning a large
Facebook group of 200K members) or both.
In 
Part 2: Coffee Chats
 of this book, I listed 14 ways to build a distribution channel that I
think will be helpful for any indie hackers. That chapter will hopefully give you some
ideas of what's possible and what's involved in building your own distribution channel.
Again, you will need to find the one that works well for you.
My distribution channel
Among all the options, I decided to give it a try to build an audience on Twitter.
A small note: Twitter is now called "X" after Elon acquired it, but I'm too used to
calling it Twitter. So throughout this book, I'll call it "Twitter" and sometimes
"X" — both are the same thing.
My idea was simple: if I could build a Twitter account and gain a lot of followers, then I
could simply put DevUtils in my profile and promote it. The more followers I had, the
more traffic DevUtils would have, and some of that traffic would convert to revenue.
So I decided to set my focus on two things.
First, I still want the organic growth to work. So I want to make sure all of my existing
DevUtils customers are happy, as this will be the fuel for the word-of-mouth effect. I do
this by continuously improving the app and making new updates, fixing bugs, talking to
my customers and listening to their feedback.
Second, I want to start building an audience that I can use as a marketing and
distribution channel. I want more people to know about DevUtils, and to do that without
spending a lot of money or my sanity. And unlike my previous marketing efforts from the
last few months, this time, I want it to be a long-term channel that I can keep doing
forever without constantly pushing the boulder uphill.
And that's how I started my journey of building an audience on Twitter, which opened a
whole new chapter in my indie journey.
Building an audience on Twitter
How I Got Started
I started posting on Twitter/X around November 2020 when I tried to do marketing for
DevUtils. I wasn't really serious about building an audience at that time — all I did was
tweet about DevUtils and hope to get some views/traffic from my tweets. I had an old
account that had been on Twitter for a while; it had about 100 followers at the time,
mostly just bots.
My tweets had absolutely no effect whatsoever on DevUtils. Imagine a random guy (me)
with less than 100 followers self-promoting his app every day on Twitter. Nobody cares.
Actually, I think nobody saw my tweets at all. But I didn't care much because Twitter was
just one of my marketing channels at the time.
Only in March 2021, when I started to consider using Twitter as a long-term distribution
channel, did I start seriously thinking about how to grow an audience. My idea was
simple: if I could get a lot of followers, I could put DevUtils into my bio and occasionally
tweet about it — that should get some traffic to the website.
Choosing the Audience I Want to Build
DevUtils is a developer product, so I wanted to build an audience around developers by
posting developer-related content. Initially, I wanted my account to solely post about
developer content, trying to gain as many followers as possible. If you've been on
Twitter for a long time, you probably have seen similar accounts (like @iamdeveloper)
that constantly post tweets and threads about one type of content all the time.
However, posting the same thing over and over again is really boring. I later learned
about the "build in public" community where people post about their progress building
their products — most of them are indie hackers too.
So I switched to posting about my progress of building DevUtils, updates on what I'd
done, sharing the revenue numbers, and everything. I slowly switched my account from
a developer content account into a personal branding account.
Since I was tweeting about what I'm doing, it's easy for me to think about what to tweet.
My content is just what I do in a given day. If I add a new feature, I share the
screenshots/videos of the feature. If I fix a bug, I share about the mistake I've made.
When I have revenue, I share the revenue. When I sell a license to a big company, I tweet
about the selling process. I also tweet about what I learn while trying to be an indie
hacker too.
It was easy and fun to tweet. I never have to spend time planning my "content". I never
used any tweet scheduler at all. By tweeting about indie hacking and building in public,
my audience slowly became mostly indie hackers.
Personal branding account vs. Specific content account
So instead of building a specific niche content account, I decided to build a personal
branding account instead. This comes with some trade-offs.
Imagine if:
I build an audience about travel, then I build a product about travel — it will be a
perfect fit.
Or build an audience about design, then build a design tool.
Or build an audience about investing, then build an investment portfolio app.
You get the idea.
Now that I've built a personal branding account, and I'm an indie hacker, my audience is
mostly indie hackers too. Let's say if I later build a product that's not related to indie
hackers or developers, the traffic from my audience won't have much value.
Of course, I can stick to building products for indie hackers, but I don't want to limit
myself to that niche. In fact, trying to sell to indie hackers is a bad idea because most
indie hackers are broke (including myself at the time). I wanted to sell to rich people.
That said, building a personal branding account comes with many more benefits that I
think totally outweigh every other niche. It's much more difficult, but if it works, it's
awesome.
With an audience around my personal branding, if I fail one product, I can just move on
to another one while keeping the content all related. Even if later I build a product that's
not entirely related to indie hackers, if I have a big enough audience, surely there is a
small percentage of the audience who will be the target customers. That should be good
enough — at least I have an initial user base, then I can rely on other methods to reach
more target customers.
The best thing about building a personal branding account is that you can actually make
friends with people. I love my audience and I enjoy engaging with them every day. Think
about those accounts that post news and threads all day long on Twitter — even though
they have a lot of engagement and followers, the whole account looks like a robot.
Nobody really thinks about that account as a person or thinks about being friends with
them.
The Slow Start
Building an audience, especially a personal brand account, is extremely slow and
difficult in the beginning. It took me 1 year to grow to 10,000 followers, spending about 3
hours a day on average on Twitter almost every day. Most of my tweets in the early days
got zero likes and engagements.
I had never built an audience before, so this was all new to me. I decided to buy a cheap
Twitter course to learn the basics of Twitter — like how mentions work, what a Twitter
thread is, what the algorithm likes, how to be seen by other people, etc. Among the
things I learned, one of the most valuable lessons from the course was to go and engage
with other people on the platform instead of just tweeting on your own.
It makes perfect sense. Twitter shows my tweets to my followers, but if I only have 100
followers, they can only be seen by 100 people (or bots). Instead, if I go to an existing
tweet of someone who is in the same community as mine and post a reply, I have a
much better chance to get seen. The problem is finding something useful, fun, or
valuable to reply to in an existing thread. If I just spam random things under every tweet,
it's the fastest way to destroy my reputation and get blocked.
So I started to follow people in the developer niche and started replying to their tweets. I
always try to reply with useful content, my unique thoughts on the matter, or just be
funny and entertain people. For example, this is a reply to a tweet where I got more than
200 likes, which helped me gain some initial followers.
Building a personal brand
Getting seen on Twitter is just the first step. After people see my account and click on
my profile, I needed to give them a reason to follow me.
So I updated my profile. I do a lot of things to market myself as a developer and an indie
hacker, a talented and trustworthy one. Every tool Twitter gives me, I make use of it. I set
my profile picture to use my real face, I write a short bio, set a banner, create a pinned
tweet, set a profile link, everything.
I started tweeting consistently. I remember my longest tweeting streak is 500 days since
April 2021, which means for almost 1.5 years, every single day, I tweet at least one tweet,
and I never missed one. Tweeting consistently trains my habit and makes it easier for me
to think about what to tweet next. Other than tweeting on my main timeline, I also always
keep engaging with other people on Twitter with thoughtful replies.
As for the content, I tweeted about a lot of things, mostly developer-related. I love
coding, so I created a lot of fun and interesting experiments with JavaScript/HTML/CSS
and tweeted about them. Those experiments helped me gain a lot of engagements and
followers.
I remember one time, Twitter changed their like button animation, I thought it was really
cool-looking, so I re-created it using CSS and shared the code on Twitter — it gained
more than 800 likes. One experiment particularly helped me go from 1,500 followers to
5,000 followers in just a few days.
I also tweet general developer content. Sometimes I post threads of useful developer
resources that I spent a whole day collecting, sometimes I tell my stories and experience
being a developer, trying to help junior developers with their careers, sometimes I create
funny memes, and sometimes even inspirational short sentences.
I also love building products, so I built a micro SaaS product called DriveStats that
visualizes the storage usage of my Google Drive in a beautiful chart. I deployed it, added
payment, launched it on Product Hunt, made $53 of revenue after a month with 164
users. I later listed it on MicroAcquire to sell the product, and someone bought it from
me for a grand total of $1,800.
I shared the whole process including the revenue numbers and acquisition process on
my Twitter. It wasn't a lot of money, but I had a lot of fun, and it also helped me gain a lot
of engagement.
Going from 0 to 10,000 followers is the most difficult part. It's hard to condense
everything I've done, so I created a dedicated special chapter in this book that will go
through every single small detail and my tweets during this period.
I picked out the most representative tweets that reflect what I have done in each period
to build a following, with the direct link to the tweet and its exact time. I think it would be
helpful to give you a realistic glimpse into how I was doing at the time. You will find that
chapter right after this one.
The results
During the whole time building an audience, I also tweeted a lot about DevUtils. I self-
promoted it on my own timeline, sharing my ideas to improve the app, how much money
I make from it, what new features I'm adding to it, telling the story of how I started it in
the first place, etc.
After all, my initial plan was to use my personal branding to promote DevUtils to make it
a profitable business.
Around August 2021, with more than 8,000 followers, a dedicated link in my bio, and
constantly self-promoting the app, the revenue from DevUtils grew to about $200 per
month. That's not much, but still something, way better than $0/month.
I was very excited and happy. Even though DevUtils was not doing as well as I thought it
would be, I had successfully built an audience of my own. The audience I had was still
small, but the momentum was great.
I also realized I love doing this — tweeting seems natural and fun to me, I like to engage
with people, and my audience was also highly engaging with me. I made a lot of new
friends on Twitter, a lot of them sharing the same indie dream as mine. I still keep in
touch with most of them to this day.
I'm also happy that I decided to build an audience around my personal brand instead of
building a faceless random Twitter account that just posts threads and inspirational
quotes all day long. It was very personal — it's the place I share my wins and struggles,
tell my stories to inspire, and read other people's stories to get inspired. The Twitter
community is very positive and encouraging; it helped me a lot during my entire indie
journey.
I was confident that if I kept building and growing my audience, I would eventually have a
sustainable distribution channel, or at least something to kick off my future product
launches. I know that it may not work for any product I build — I still need to build a really
good product to capture this opportunity, but I will try a lot of things, and I just need one
successful product to escape the rat race, that's all. It doesn't matter if DevUtils will be
a success or not; as long as I have an audience, I can build new products, and it would
be an extremely valuable advantage to get my new product to thousands of eyeballs for
free.
At the time of writing this book, I have more than 150,000 followers on Twitter. Looking
back, the decision to build a personal brand on Twitter is the most important decision of
my indie career, and it contributed most significantly to helping me get to where I am
right now.
Tips on building an audience on
Twitter
In this chapter, I want to share my experience and some tips to build an audience on
Twitter.
Depending on whether you are building a specific content account or a personal
branding account, the approach can be different.
Specific content account
 means an account that only focuses on posting one kind
of content like travel, interior design, investing, engineering, etc. People follow
these accounts to get news, knowledge, or for entertainment.
Personal branding account
 means the account is about you as a person; people
follow you because they are interested in what you are doing, your opinions, and
what you talk about.
I will focus more on how to build a personal branding account, because thatʼs what I
have experience with the most. However, there are some general tips that will work for
both types that I think will be very helpful anyway, especially in the early days (less than
1,000 followers).
Start by being an interesting person
The most important thing to build an audience around your personal branding is to be an
interesting person - give people reasons to follow you.
Later in this chapter, I will share tips and tactics to speed up the process, but at the end
of the day, if you don't have something interesting about yourself, it's going to be
difficult.
"Being interesting" is a general statement; it could be about anything. Some people get
a lot of followers because they teach other people to code in an easy way to understand,
some people make beautiful designs, some build products and share progress, and
some other people get followers because they are hilarious and make really funny
memes.
Telling your story is a great way to stay authentic and interesting. Learn to do
storytelling, make it interesting or inspiring. Be real and don't be afraid to show your
vulnerabilities. Use your story, talent, and advantages to catch people's attention.
When I started building my account, I tweeted about coding and building fun
experiments with code. I built so many "useless" but interesting experiments and shared
them on Twitter to get attention. This tweet is an example of a fun thing I made to get
attention on Twitter:
Later when I started to have some initial followers, I built SaaS products and apps in
public. I shared with everyone about the products I built, how much money I made from
those products, what I did to get users, shared my lessons as an indie hacker, etc.
By consistently creating interesting content like this for about a year, people started
following me. That's how I gained my first 10,000 followers.
Think of something that you are good at and people find interesting, then write about it
and show it to everyone. Be patient and be consistent.
For example, if you are a designer, share your designs and show proof of work. It's the
easiest way to prove your authenticity and creativity. Tweet one design that you created
per day for 90 days straight.
Engaging with people using reply/comment
If you have less than 100 followers, don't tweet inspirational quotes, in fact, donʼt even
bother to tweet in your timeline at all. Go find accounts that you like and comment on
their tweets - that's the only way people will see you on the platform.
Make sure to write actual informative, meaningful, valuable, or helpful comments. Don't
just comment for the sake of creating a comment (like "nice", "thanks", or using AI-
generated comments). Be yourself and be interesting.
The goal is to get people to click on your profile from those comments.
Make use of the Community feature on Twitter. Community is like a Facebook group or a
subreddit. By posting and replying in a community, your tweets have a better chance of
being seen than when posting on your own timeline.
Set up your profile properly
The goal here is to give people a reason to follow you when they click on your profile,
and Twitter gives you a lot of tools to do that.
Use your name (instead of "The Dev Guy"), show your profile picture with your face,
write a bio, set a banner, create a pinned tweet, set a URL for your website if you have
any, etc. Everything is an opportunity to let people follow you.
Keep your timeline “warm” by tweeting at least once a day or once every two days. If I
visit a profile and the last tweet I see from the person is 6 months ago, I lose all of my
interests.
Tweet often, don't retweet or quote too much
Start posting original content once a day, can be more often if you like, but be
consistent. This is to make sure when people go to your timeline, they know roughly
what you are tweeting about, and that you are still there and active, not some
abandoned account.
Don't retweet or quote tweet other people too much. When people scroll your timeline,
they want to see your tweets and not other people's tweets. Retweeting once in a while
is ok but if I scroll someoneʼs timeline and itʼs all retweet/quote, it look like a bot account
and there is no reason why I should follow the person.
What to tweet about
If you are building a personal branding account: be yourself, tweet what you think, what
you find interesting, share your knowledge, tell your stories, be interesting, make
memes, tell jokes, discuss the news, etc. If you are also building in public like me, you
can start a side project and tweet about the progress of building it.
There are some types of tweets that often have a higher chance of getting more
engagements:
Asking a question
Posting threads (news summary, share a collection of useful tools, create TLDR
from articles, etc.)
Create and post funny memes
Good takes and unpopular opinions about something
Share personal stories that are inspiring
Most tweets with images and videos tend to do better
Create and share infographics or interesting data
But remember these types of posts only work when you start to have some followers; if
you are still under 200 followers, focus on engaging with people first.
Things not to do on Twitter that are not obvious to new people:
Don't use hashtags; they are ugly and look spammy.
Don't spam tag people in your tweet. If you tag someone to tell them something
publicly for a specific reason, that's fine; otherwise, tagging other people will get
you blocked very quickly.
Links in the first tweet can reduce its reachability potential (officially confirmed by
Twitter), so unless it's necessary, people usually put the link in the second tweet for
maximum reach.
Make friends, talk to people via DM
On Twitter, people can set their Direct Message (DM) option to public, meaning they are
willing to accept DMs from everyone on Twitter. If you see someone has left their DM
open, don't be afraid to drop in and say hi, introduce yourself, or ask questions.
When two people interact a lot on Twitter, the algorithm tends to show each other's
tweets more often. So if you make friends with people with bigger follower counts and
tweet something that's actually funny or interesting, you have a higher chance of getting
a like/retweet from them, help gain more views and engagements.
This only works if the other person accept your DM and reply back, so donʼt go spam
people. Reach out to someone that you have legitimate reason to reach out. Offer
something useful to the person without trying to sell something to them. For example, if
the person is building a product that you are using, simply sharing your feedback or a
bug that you see in the product would be extremely helpful and easy to get a reply back.
Get the blue tick if possible
It's no secret that Twitter will boost your tweets more when you have the blue tick. Yes,
it costs money, but I think it will be worth it. Getting a blue tick also helps make your
account look more reputable on the platform, so it's a good first impression.
Some big accounts also only open their DMs for people with blue ticks (to avoid spam
and bots), so having a blue tick allows you to DM them when you need to.
Be patient
Good things take time and effort. It took me about 1 year to get to 10,000 followers,
spending ~3 hours a day on Twitter every day. So be patient!
In the next chapter, I will share with you real world examples that demonstrated
everything I shared here, taken directly from my Twitter timeline.

Twitter.

1,000 → 6,000

6,000 → 10,000:
 still stay consistent, build in public, share my journey, threads,
memes, giveaways, advice, etc.

Conclusion
In short, my strategy to build an audience on Twitter can be summarized into 4 bullet
points:
Engage with people.
Be interesting, do cool and creative stuff, make noise.
Build products in public, show your work.
Be genuine, be nice to people, and spread positivities.
Black Magic: From zero to $5K MRR
Black Magic started out as just another fun coding experiment I did on Twitter to gain
attention. But when it started to catch the interest of so many people, I discovered a
niche and pivoted the product into a Twitter Analytics and CRM browser extension. It
ended up making $14K MRR revenue at its peak. Here is how it started.
Came up with the idea
It was around May 2021, I was still trying hard to build an audience, I had about 800
followers at the time. I was building a lot of fun experiments with code to gain attention
and followers. As I was approaching the big milestone of 1,000 followers, I thought of a
small fun idea to celebrate it.
I thought it would be cool to update my avatar with a circle progress bar around it. I
would use a small JavaScript code and the Twitter API to update my profile picture with
the progress bar every minute, making it go from 0% to 100% as I approached the 1,000
followers mark.
On Twitter, your profile picture shows up in a lot of places: when you reply to people's
tweets, on your own tweets, in your DMs, in the notifications panel — everywhere. So by
doing this, I may make a lot of people curious, and when they find out what the progress
bar represents, they may help make the progress bar move faster by following me!
And it worked! When people see the progress bar, they get really curious. It looks cool
and interesting; people haven't seen anything like this before. I gained a lot of followers
fast and blew past the 1,000 milestone.
A lot of people who saw the profile progress bar wanted to do the same thing with their
profile picture. So I turned my JavaScript code into a simple web app where people can
log in with their Twitter account, then my app will update their profile picture the same
way it does with mine.
On May 15, 2021, I tweeted about the tool and got 150+ likes, which was a lot to me! In
the tweet below, I talked about the tool with a short video demo showing how it works,
then I asked people to reply under my tweet in order to try the beta version.
The "reply below, then I'll DM you" was a really great trick, as it helped with engagement
when people replied to my tweets. After they replied to my tweet, I would DM them with
the link and instructions to try the beta version of the product, then talk to them and ask
for feedback. After a few days, I had 123 people signed up and using the product — it
was still a free product at the time.
All of the 123 early users of Black Magic
I thought that this would be a great opportunity to somehow turn this into a business. So
I decided to add a paid tier to it. I called it the "Black Magic Membership" — it costs
$4/month to unlock all the progress bar "premium colors" and faster refresh rate. I
tweeted about it but didn't make any revenue.
On May 20th, 2021, I posted the product on Product Hunt and also tweeted about the
launch. I had less than 2,000 followers at the time, but the launch tweet got more than
300 likes, and the post on Product Hunt also received more than 250 upvotes with more
than 80 comments. It got into the top 10 products of the day on Product Hunt.
At this point, even though I hoped to make some money from the paid tier, I was still
considering all this as yet another experiment to build engagement on Twitter, as part of
my effort to build an audience.
I had done this once with my previous micro-SaaS product DriveStats. I would build in
public, share the progress, launch on Product Hunt, then see what happened. The
attempt with DriveStats didn't turn out to be a sustainable business; it didn't have
subscription revenue, and although it made some revenue, it wasn't a lot.
For that reason, I didn't have much hope for Black Magic either. At least I had fun, got a
lot of likes and engagement, and earned some new followers in the process.
Doubling down on the momentum
I was having a lot of fun with Black Magic. Even though it hadn't made any money, I got a
lot of attention and engagement from the community. I decided to add more features to
Black Magic to have even more fun.
Black Magic turned into a suite of small tools to help people do fun things on Twitter and
help them get more engagement, just like I did when I first created the profile progress
bar tool.
And I went crazy with it. For the next few weeks, I added so many fun things to Black
Magic, you can call it "engagement tools". Just to mention a few tools I added:
A "Sleep indicator" feature, where your profile picture will show a "zzZ" overlay
when it's sleep time in your timezone.
A "Big Picture" feature, where Black Magic will visualize a grid of all the profile
pictures of everyone in your followers list. People love this because they want to
show off who follows them, and see all of their faces in one place. They usually take
a screenshot of it and reshare on their timeline, making more people know about
Black Magic.
A "Real-time banner" feature, where Black Magic will update your banner with real-
time information like stock price, date and time, a countdown timer, etc. I used the
same technique with the profile progress bar to update the banner every ~1 minute.
A physics simulation thing where every time someone likes or comments on your
tweet, an emoji will be added to your banner.
In one particular feature, I created a special script that automatically updates my Twitter
banner with the profile pictures of the last 3 people who follow me. It looks like the
picture below, and this feature blew up like crazy.
Every time someone followed me, their profile picture would show up on my Twitter
banner about 30 seconds later. It was amazing. People have always assumed Twitter
banners are static, so something like this happening is really strange, almost like… black
magic!
"
Holy... This is probably the coolest thing I've seen on here.. Great job!
" - one of my
followers commented about the banner.
With just some lines of JavaScript and the Twitter API, I made the magic happen. People
started following me just to see their face show up on my banner, and they loved it! The
tweet got more than 2,000 likes, 300 replies, and I gained more than 4,000 new
followers from just this feature within just a few days.
Suddenly, my account grew from ~1,500 to ~5,500 followers.
I later added the feature to Black Magic so everyone could do the same on their Twitter
banner. But it's a bit sad that I haven't seen anyone able to replicate the same results as
mine with the feature. I think I got a little lucky with that one.
This is also when I created my newsletter. I never had any tweet with this much
engagement, so I wanted to capture it not only through followers but also in an email list.
So I signed up for an account on Substack and started promoting my newsletter
everywhere. I later wrote a recap of my updates once a month in the newsletter. It
became my "backup plan" in case I somehow lost my Twitter account — at least I would
still have access to my audience via email.
And then something magical happened about a week later: someone bought a
subscription! It was the first time I had subscription Monthly Recurring Revenue (MRR)
from a product I made. I was super excited!
Having revenue hyped me up on another level — I was incredibly motivated. I'm building
an audience on Twitter, Black Magic helps me build the audience by creating more
engagement, and then it also makes money for me? It was a perfect combo. This could
be a business!
I felt like I was enjoying some sort of compounding benefits, and everything I did just
supported every other thing. My followers were growing fast, my revenue was also
growing, I was having fun building and sharing new features with my audience, and I got
lots of likes and engagement from all of this.
On July 22nd, I 
launched
 the real-time banner feature of Black Magic on Product
Hunt. With the help from my Twitter audience of about 8,000 followers at the time, the
launch was a great success! It became the #1 product of the day, #4 product of the
week, more than 1,000 upvotes, 5.36k website visits, 968 new users, 12 paid customers,
and Black Magic was now at $194 MRR.
I spent the next few months building out new features, improving existing ones, making
sure existing customers were happy, while still sharing everything I did on Twitter,
including revenue numbers. Every new announcement tweet about new features of
Black Magic had more than 100 likes, some even 300 to 600 likes. It was the most active
period of my Twitter account.
And remember, I was still working on DevUtils at the time, so I was building both Black
Magic and from time to time coming back to DevUtils to release a new update. All of the
new followers and engagement on Twitter I got also helped with DevUtils too. More
people started to know about DevUtils, and web traffic and revenue increased.
DevUtils made $1,194 in May 2021, $1,319 in June, and $874 in July, all from organic
traffic coming mostly from my Twitter profile. Even though DevUtils revenue is not
recurring revenue, which means it fluctuates a lot from month to month, reaching more
than $1,000 revenue per month for the first time with $0 marketing spent was amazing.
Things are working!
Quitting my job, going all in
By August 2021, I felt really confident about going full-time on indie hacking. I've got
some revenue, about $1,100 per month from DevUtils and $300 MRR from Black Magic.
With $1,400 of total revenue a month, I could come back to Vietnam where the living
[30]
cost is much lower than Singapore, and it's already sustainable! It would be just like
reverting back to my first-year salary when I started my career, but instead of working
for a company, I would work for myself.
The revenue from DevUtils isn't recurring, it's a one-time purchase, and it fluctuates, so
I had some concerns there. But Black Magic is recurring revenue, and I feel confident
that I could grow it to be much bigger than DevUtils.
More importantly, I have successfully built an audience of about 8,000 followers on
Twitter and about 1,000 more in my newsletter, and it was still growing fast. It's the first
time in my life that I have access to a valuable distribution channel like this, it would help
me a lot in growing my products.
When I started my indie journey, I only knew how to build products, I didn't have a
distribution channel to market and sell them. Now I have both. Even if it turns out both
DevUtils and Black Magic fail, I still have my Twitter account, and I would just build new
products. I was sure eventually, something would work. I just need one successful
product making $1,000 MRR, then I will be set for life.
And then even if everything fails, which is the worst-case scenario, I would just go back
to find another job. That's my backup plan. I'm okay with going back to the rat race at
the same salary level or even lower if needed. I am an above-average software engineer,
looking for a normal job won't be a big problem for me.
After some calculations, I figured I had about 2–3 years of savings to make indie hacking
work, assuming I continue living in Singapore and I make $0 from my products. If I move
back to Vietnam, my runway could easily triple. I had a decent runway to build a
successful indie product, a "success" for me would be $1,000 MRR minimum. I was
already at $300 MRR with Black Magic, the target of another $700 MRR doesn't seem
too far away! I also have revenue from DevUtils, so my safety net was great!
With that in mind, I talked to my manager about my plan, he was incredibly supportive of
my decision. I shared with him that I plan to continue my indie journey in Singapore, he
wrote me a letter of recommendation to support me in applying for a visa with the
Singapore government, but also may help if I later need to find a job or return to the
company in case my plan fails. I am forever grateful for his support!
I handed in my notice, ending my 2 years of employment with the company. My last
working day was September 20, 2021. On the first day I became free, I took a walk
outside and felt the freedom I had never felt in my life.
Last day at work!
I quit my job around the peak of the COVID-19 pandemic in September 2021. It was
about 1.5 years since I started giving indie hacking a serious attempt with the log viewer
app.
Initially, I wanted to continue my indie journey in Singapore, as I have many friends there.
I also enjoyed the modern city life and the booming startup ecosystem that I might
benefit from. I tried applying for a visa, but without a job and my products still making
penny money, the Singapore government didn't accept my application. Indie hacking
was not something that people recognized widely at the time.
Coming back to Vietnam was the only option, but it was actually the best option for me.
With the low living cost in Vietnam, I had a much longer runway to make indie hacking
work, with much lower stress. A small issue was that I would lose access to my Stripe
account, which still didn't support Vietnam. But this turned out not to be a problem at
all, as I learned that there are many ways to collect global payments without having to
use Stripe.
Paddle.com
 was one of the options. I first used it when I added payment to Black Magic
because I wanted to try out a new payment processor. Paddle supports almost every
country in the world, including Vietnam. So I switched my payment processor from
Stripe to Paddle to continue getting revenue payouts from my products.
Flying back to Vietnam during the pandemic is a whole story on its own, I won't bore you
with all the details. All I remember the most is that I was happily working on my products
everywhere I went: at hotels, the airport, on the plane, at the quarantine center… I was
completely immersed in building new features and tweeting. It was the most exciting
time of my indie career.
The $40,000 acquisition offer
For the next few months, I continuously worked on Black Magic and DevUtils, all while
still tweeting a lot to grow my audience.
In October 2021, when Black Magic reached $326 MRR, I received an offer to acquire
the whole product for $40,000 cash. The offer came from MicroAcquire, the
marketplace to buy and sell startups. I used this marketplace to sell my previous micro-
SaaS DriveStats for $1,800 the last time. I listed Black Magic on the marketplace just to
see how much people would pay to acquire it, just to validate if what I was building was
valuable, and maybe if I got a good offer, I might actually sell it to extend my runway!
The offer was good — it's more than 10 times the annual revenue of Black Magic at the
time ($326 × 12 × 10 = $39,120). Black Magic was still a collection of engagement tools
for Twitter at the time. It had about 5,147 users, mostly free users, and a Twitter account
with about 800 followers. That's all the assets.
However, after careful consideration, I decided not to sell. There were many reasons.
Firstly, I don't really need the cash at that time. Before quitting my job, I had piled up
enough savings to live without any income for 2+ years (even more after I moved back to
Vietnam). Considering I'd get to keep ~$30k from the deal after tax and costs, it's not a
life-changing amount for me. Sure, I'd have some more breathing room for my indie
hacking journey, but it's not quite that significant.
Secondly, I still have so much love and plans for the product. My to-do list for Black
Magic is long, and I'm nowhere finished. Black Magic was the only product with
recurring revenue I had, and there were still so many things I wanted to do to provide
value for my followers and customers via Black Magic. The project has helped me grow
my Twitter account from 1k to 10k followers, and I believe it can help me grow from 10k
→ 100k soon. I also believe I can grow the product to $2,000–$3,000 MRR within the
next year or two. I feel it is way too early to sell at this point.
And lastly, while talking to the buyer, I learned that their plan to grow the product after
the acquisition didn't quite resonate well with me. I didn't want my customers to feel
"betrayed" when I sell it, as many of them use the product just because I'm the one
behind it. I also didn't have a clear idea of what project I would move on to after I sell
Black Magic.
In the end, I decided to decline the offer, take the listing offline, and continue to build
Black Magic. It turns out to be the right decision, as just a month later, I pivoted Black
Magic to something that completely changed its future.
The big pivot
Up until this point, Black Magic was still a set of engagement tools for Twitter, most of
them created to make fun things to attract engagement. It wasn't really solving any real
pain points, more like nice-to-have features. Even though I spent a lot of time working
on it, the revenue didn't move much after the initial viral moment.
I wasn't really concerned about this because I was still gaining engagement and
followers by building Black Magic in public anyway, and DevUtils was still making one-off
revenue. However, seeing the MRR stuck at the same level for a month started to get me
nervous in the long term.
I have always wanted to make Black Magic become a "real" product by being actually
helpful and solving pain points, but I didn't know how.
I used Twitter a lot, and I also talked to my audience and Black Magic's customers a lot.
So over time, I collected many ideas I wanted to improve on Twitter. A few examples:
I want to be able to view past interactions from a person with me: have they
liked/replied to my tweets before? How long have they followed me?
View a tweet's performance over time: how do my tweet impressions change in
their entire lifetime, down to each hour/minute? How did a like/retweet from
someone with 100K followers affect my tweet's reach? (Yes, I'm a data nerd, and I
want to see a lot of data).
Many smaller things like: adding notes to a profile, setting reminders for tweets,
tweet categories
Advanced analytics for tweets and profiles would also be incredibly helpful, as the
default Twitter analytic metrics were really poor.
Overall, I had a very long wishlist of things I wanted to improve on Twitter. I just didn't
know in what form I should deliver them — should it be a SaaS web app? Or a Twitter
bot? Or a mobile app? I put a lot of thought into how to deliver the ideas in a way that's
most convenient for users.
One day I saw Twemex.app for the first time (created by @geoffreylitt), it's a browser
extension that adds a sidebar to the Twitter web page, and I thought that was genius!
The Twemex extension itself does a few things that are cool, but I was most impressed
by the idea of embedding the whole app as a browser extension that stays on top of the
Twitter UI.
I immediately knew how to deliver all of my ideas: it would be a browser extension — it
made so much sense. I didn't want to build a whole new Twitter web client; it would be
too much work. Instead, I could bundle them all into a browser extension that stays on
top of the Twitter UI and provides all the features I need.
And I would make everything happen inside the sidebar; it communicates with Twitter
via their official API. I didn't want to touch any of the existing UI components from
Twitter, so that my extension wouldn't be affected when Twitter changes their code.
Later, I could move the whole extension to a separate website or app if I really had to.
Over the next week, I created an MVP for the extension. I called it the "Magic Sidebar,"
and it would be a feature of Black Magic, pretty much like other tools, but much better!
Then I used it for myself for a while, and I really liked it! The MVP version of Magic
Sidebar only had 2 features. I released it as a beta version, then invited some of my
Twitter friends to try it. They also liked it a lot!
From $318 MRR to $2,164 MRR in 60 days
I shared some small demos on Twitter, and people got really excited. It helped gain some
traction for Black Magic as a whole. Some people even started asking to pay early to use
this feature! I called it "a completely new Twitter Web experience" and posted a
screenshot of the MVP — the tweet got more than 600 likes!
I started an invite-only beta program where people could try the extension free of
charge and invite their friends to try it. Then, I asked people for their feedback, made
improvements, and added more features.
For a few weeks straight, I added new features almost every day. My entire day was like
this: Morning: add a feature. Afternoon: release the feature, tweet about it, then invite
new beta users. Night: talk with beta users, collect feedback/suggestions, fix bugs.
Then repeat the next day.
Almost every tweet I made about Black Magic went "mini viral". Every feature I published
became a marketing tweet. Just for the record, I collected all of those tweets into 
a long
thread here
. Each one gets ~50 to 100 likes on average, some good ones get to
~300 likes.
Among all the CRM features like taking notes, viewing past interactions, monitoring
follower counts, tracking tweet engagement timeline…, Black Magic was most famous
for having the most detailed and comprehensive analytics for tweets and profiles that
none of the products on the market were able to do at the time. I spent so much time
coding and optimizing the Twitter API for this!
Simon Høiberg, a popular creator on Twitter with 100K+ followers said: 
"By far the most
superior Twitter analytics product in the indie hackers space."
Daniel Vassallo, 128K+ followers, said: 
"Black Magic has revealed insights I never knew
about. The ability to see past interactions with individual profiles is the killer feature for
me."
Joe Previte, a popular developer with more than 30K followers tweeted this:
[31]
With countless praises from many big accounts on Twitter at the time, Black Magic
became popular among Twitter users. The Magic Sidebar feature had completely
changed the product's fate. I was super happy that I managed to convert Black Magic
from a nice-to-have product into a useful painkiller product that provided massive value
to its users.
When inviting people to the beta version, I asked people to drop a "
👋
" to my tweet to
get an invite code. It helped me keep track of who to send the invite codes to, and also
helped with the reach as well.
During that time, every day, I talked to about ~20 people, gained ~100 new followers,
and marketed the product at the same time. I did this for about 2 months from October
to December 2021, talking with users and improving the products. I ended up with about
920 beta users. I offered an early-bird discount (33% off) for everyone who wanted to
subscribe early while the feature was still in beta.
And with just this, the Magic Sidebar helped Black Magic go from $318 MRR to $2,164
MRR with 248 paid users in just about 60 days. Not to mention my followers also
increased to about 20,000 followers during this time!
Magic Sidebar, the feature that changed Black Magic forever
I 
launched the Magic Sidebar feature
 on Product Hunt on December 16th, 2021. I
used all of my effort to promote the launch: my Twitter, the early beta users, all of the
previous free users, my newsletters — all combined for the launch date. I sent them an
email before the launch date asking them to support me and leave their reviews of the
product.
The launch ended up being the most successful launch I have ever made on Product
Hunt. With more than 1,000 upvotes and 330 comments, it was awarded #1 Product of
the Day, #2 Product of the Week, #2 Product of the Month, and Black Magic was
[32]
nominated for the "Golden Kitty Award" — a special award for best products launched
on Product Hunt in 2022.
Everything, all at once
The period from November 2021 to December 2021 was the craziest time in my indie
career. Not only Black Magic, but DevUtils also benefited from all the activities I was
doing on Twitter. Words cannot describe how happy I was during this period.
It was just like everything happened all at once:
I successfully pivoted Black Magic to a real Twitter SaaS.
The launch was incredibly successful.
Someone posted DevUtils to HackerNews and it got viral again, resulting in $3K of
revenue.
And then in November, there was a Black Friday promotion campaign, which also
brought in a lot of revenue. I made $23,109 of revenue just from November alone.
By February 2022, just about 6 months after quitting my job, I had reached $5K MRR
and 28K followers on Twitter, far beyond my goal.
What I learned from build in public
Get product ideas by doing hard things
I believe when you are committed to doing something, ideally things that are difficult,
and embed yourself in the process, you will start to see problems around that thing, and
then with your background as a software engineer, you can come up with new original
product ideas from that.
For me, that “thing” was building an audience. If I wasn't being serious about building an
audience, I wouldn't have been trying hard to build all kinds of experiments on Twitter to
get attention, and Black Magic would never have been created.
I see this pattern happen a lot; it is a great way to come up with new product ideas.
When Pieter Levels was trying to relocate to Portugal via a special program to attract
immigrants called NHR, he found out that the process was extremely painful and slow,
with all the paperwork, connecting with lawyers, contractors, government officials, etc.
He then created 
rebase.co
, a service to help streamline all of this. The product helped
more than 1,000 applicants go through the process much more easily and saved a lot of
time. The product had a great run from 2021 to 2024, making more than $200,000 of
revenue before the government program officially ended on January 1, 2024.
The downside of this approach is that sometimes, the thing you are trying to do is not
always high-value, so people are not ready to pay to solve those problems. Or, there are
just not enough people who do the same thing as you do (too small a niche), which
makes it difficult to reach those people. Building an audience is a high-difficulty, high-
value activity, and there are a lot of people like me who want to build an audience, so it
works well in the case of Black Magic. You will need to keep this in mind when trying to
generate product ideas this way.
Build in public
I learned about build in public around the same time as when I learned about indie
hacking, and I immediately loved it and followed this practice in all of my indie products.
The idea of build in public is that you build and show your work in public — not just the
result, but also the progress of how you build it, the struggles, the small wins, talking to
people, teaching what you learn, etc.
By doing this, you turn strangers into supporters of your products, maybe even turn
them into customers. You learn faster and build better products by talking with people
while you're building. It opens up opportunities and lets people understand more about
you and what you're doing, building up your credibility in the community.
Sharing your progress in public allow other people to follow you along with your journey,
keeping you accountable with your goals.
Marc Köhlbrugge, who was a big advocate for build in public, stated it really well on his
website 
buildinpublic.com
:
The best ideas don't happen in isolation. They thrive in the open, strengthened
by collaboration, refined through feedback, and accelerated by momentum.
Building in public isn't just about transparency—it's about building smarter,
faster, and with the people who care most.
Instead of waiting for perfection, share your journey. Show your work. Let the
world be part of the process.
Black Magic was built in public. I shared almost everything about the process of building
the product, from when it was just a script that I used on my own profile, up until I turned
it into a product with fun engagement tools, I launched it on Product Hunt, all the way to
when it became a real product making $5K MRR.
Other than Twitter, I also shared my updates on the 
Indie Hackers
 forum, 
Hacker
News
, my personal newsletter, and sometimes on Reddit too. For example, while
building one of my side projects, Xnapper, I wrote 
a post on Indie Hackers
 sharing
about how I came up with the idea, how I launched the product, what I did to drive
traffic, how I acquired 4,600 beta users before the launch, what my advantages were
when I started, along with all the metrics and revenue numbers.
By building in public, I was not only getting attention to the product but also to myself as
a builder and an indie hacker. It was one of the big reasons why Black Magic became a
success.
But it's not without the downsides.
One of the most popular comments about build in public is that people will steal your
idea and build a competitor, or they will build copycats. It's true, and it happened to me
all the time, but in my opinion, those risks aren't as big as you would think.
Firstly, your product idea is probably not unique, and the idea alone doesn't have much
value. What's more important is how you execute it.
This means that when building in public, sharing your product idea isn't really a big deal.
People can see your idea, but most of the time they aren't you, they can't think like you,
they don't know what you know, and they don't have your advantages. Worrying about
someone stealing your idea is not a good reason for not building in public.
[33]
[34]
[35]
Secondly, someone might copy your products — people call them copycats — and yes,
they are very annoying. All of my successful products have copycats. After I released
DevUtils and shared about how much money it makes, there were at least 10 more
similar products popping up. All of my other products are similar; Black Magic, Xnapper,
and TypingMind all have copycats. People wanting to make a quick buck and skipping all
the hard parts are showing up everywhere. At first, I was really furious about copycats,
but slowly, I learned to ignore them. Similar to the previous point, they can only copy
what you do, but they don't have your execution. I used to keep track of all the copycats
