of my products; all of them didn't turn out to be anything significant, and they left the
product abandoned after a while. Most of the copycats are not serious enough to
compete with you.
But even if they are serious, in a way, copycats can be actually helpful. My copycats
helped me think hard about how to create a "moat" for my product, something that only
I can do, creating a competitive advantage. When Black Magic was released, there were
at least 5 more similar products that appeared around the same time, doing the same
things (profile picture, real-time banner, engagement tools, etc.), but when I pivoted
Black Magic into a powerful analytics platform with advanced metrics and tracking
methods, none of the copycats were able to follow because it was technically difficult to
do so. I had to use a lot of "secret" optimizations around the Twitter API, combined with
a few other "secret" methods to crunch out the useful insights and numbers that no
other products on the market were able to provide, creating a moat for Black Magic.
Lastly, even if your idea is "copied" but they have good execution with their own take
and are doing it in a good and legitimate way, it's still not the end of the world. The
market is big; it can fit another friendly competitor — there is enough room for everyone.
Indie hackers are very cost-optimized; you only need to capture a small percentage of
the market to stay profitable.
One example of this that I have seen is on my Twitter. Pieter Levels was building
PhotoAI, and then Danny Postma also built AvatarAI, a somewhat similar product with a
slightly different niche. Instead of hating on each other, they actually entered into a
friendly competition, even helping each other out on technical stuff like how to save
costs or how to fine-tune good image generation models.
Even though they were competing with each other, by building in public, both Pieter and
Danny were making more people aware of the AI-generated photos trend, which was still
very new in 2023. According to Pieter, that increased the total market size, and both
ended up making more revenue.
Of course, the reality is more nuanced with all kinds of exceptions. The good news is, if
you want to build in public, no one is forcing you to share everything that you are not
comfortable with (although that would be the best to show your authentic self). For me, I
chose to share revenue numbers because it was a big thing that made me realize people
can actually make a good living while being an indie hacker. I was inspired by it, so I
decided to do the same hoping to inspire others.
Build with your customers, iterate fast
It was a big advantage that I was building an audience, but then also building Black
Magic — a product for that audience. The benefit compounds.
I could imagine people can replicate this in other contexts. For example, if you run a
Facebook group of rental property owners (having an audience) and build a product for
the owners to manage their properties, it would have the same compounding effect.
This makes it extremely easy to talk to customers, as they are all in the same place.
In the first few months of building Black Magic, I got feedback and ideas from the users
and implemented most of them. Then later, when I found out which features were the
most important and valuable, like the Magic Sidebar feature, I put more focus on that
and slowly removed the other unused features. Talking to customers helped me shape
the product to be better.
Iterating fast also contributed a lot to the success of Black Magic in the early days.
I feel like for some reason, when you start to get people's attention, the network effect
kicks in, or maybe Twitter's algorithm catches you, it will start to promote your account
to even more people. I was able to capture that opportunity by building whatever people
asked for, implementing all of their ideas and features, making people happy, so they
helped spread the word. For example, someone wanted to show real-time gold prices
and make it show a graph on their banner. I added it the next day and tweeted about it —
they were really happy and shared it with all of their audience too.
One of the most effective things I did to capture the hype was to embed some small viral
loops in Black Magic. The Profile Progress Bar and the Real-time Banner are good
examples: when one person uses it, other people start to see it and learn about the
product. They would ask "how do you do that with your profile picture/banner?", and
people just shared about Black Magic naturally.
I was able to iterate fast because I had learned the lesson of over-engineering from my
last failure. Instead of rewriting the backend or migrating the database or doing things
that the customers cannot see, I focused on delivering what they see and love, and did
that in a way that didn't compromise the technical side of the product. And it helped get
their attention.
By talking to the customers and iterating fast, I was able to explore all possible paths I
could grow the product into, and it helped me discover and turn Black Magic from a fun
tool into a real painkiller product. Let's talk about it.
Vitamin Products and Painkiller Products
The vitamin/painkiller product is a popular analogy to describe products that are similar
to supplements, providing nice-to-have benefits (vitamins), and products that address
immediate pain points (painkillers). Both types of products can generate revenue, but
painkiller products always have a much higher chance of success, especially when there
isn't a solution on the market.
Black Magic started out as a vitamin product, just a fun collection of engagement tools
that people use one time, have some fun, then leave. But when the Magic Sidebar
feature was introduced, it completely changed the whole product.
Suddenly, Black Magic became a real painkiller product, providing analytics and CRM
features that people had never seen anywhere else, addressing their needs for
managing and monitoring their audience on Twitter, and Black Magic did all of that in the
form of a browser extension that's convenient to use.
The vitamin features of Black Magic also have their value. Even though those fun
features didn't bring a lot of revenue, they helped gain traction and free users, which
later helped with distribution when the painkiller feature was introduced. When I
launched the painkiller feature, Black Magic had more than 5,000 free users but only
$318 MRR.
Without the painkiller feature, Black Magic would have been stuck at $318 MRR for a
long time, but without the vitamin features, Black Magic wouldn't have existed at all.
When I started building Black Magic, it was still in the very early days of indie hacking. I
didn't have much revenue, and my time was cheap. Building a vitamin product was
totally fine, as it helped me gain attention, engagement, followers, and free users. And
then later, I captured that traction and converted those users into my painkiller products.
Now looking back, I think it was a great strategy.
About Quitting Your Job
When I quit my job, I had revenue ($1,400/month, not recurring), really good traction, a
clear plan for my products (Black Magic and DevUtils), a distribution channel (my
audience), great savings, and backup plans.
I would not recommend quitting your job just to have more time to work on your indie
products. It's too risky. Most startups with funding fail, and indie products fail even more
frequently. Quitting your job too early without a clear plan and preparations can easily
stress you out.
To decide when to quit your job and start pursuing indie hacking full-time, here are a few
things you should consider:
1. 
You know exactly what product to work on, or are currently working on that product.
2. 
You have a proven distribution channel that you can use to sell your product.
3. 
If your product is generating some revenue, that's even better.
4. 
You are confident that you can make it work and reach "ramen profitable" in the
next 1–3 years.
5. 
You have some good savings or other source of income that can help you live
comfortably for 1–3 years even if you have no revenue.
6. 
Backup plans if things don't work out.
7. 
Your specific situation regarding mental health, family responsibilities, external
factors, etc.
It's all different from person to person, so there is no "best practice" — you just have to
trust your judgment. In this modern era, it's generally safe to take more risks, the worst
that can happen is to go back to a job, but you have to take calculated risks, not just go
in blindly, so that even if you fail, you won't get stuck.
My only rule is: 
don't get into debt, ever
. Indie hacking is difficult, with a lot of things you
will need to spend your attention on. You don't want to put financial stress on top of all
that.
Quitting the full-time job is a big bet. You sacrifice your progress in the rat race, you lose
your professional network to go work alone, you leave the traditional main road of career
progress and go your own way to achieve success — it's scary, but if you can make it
work, it's rewarding.
Leverage Launch Platforms
Launch platforms like Product Hunt can help amplify your product a lot, but to get the
most out of it, you have to use it the right way.
Before having an audience, I used Product Hunt as just another website to post my
product to and hoped to get lucky with the algorithm. Sometimes you will get lucky, but
most of the time it won't work.
First, let me quickly explain how Product Hunt (and most other launch platforms) works:
every day, there will be a list of new products on the market that get posted on the
website. People will browse these products and vote for them if they like the product.
Product Hunt gets millions of visitors a day, so getting to the top spots on the front page
is a big deal. The top 10 products also get featured in their daily/weekly newsletter,
which then travels to a lot of places on the internet by other people reposting the top
list. By launching your product on Product Hunt and getting to the top launch of the
day/week, you can put your product in front of a lot more people.
But you can't just rely on the random algorithm and random visitors to see your product
and instantly like it.
What I learned from building Black Magic is that, way before you launch, you should
have users already. I called this group of users "Early Users" or "Early Birds," offering a
huge discount to thank these users for trying out the product early and providing their
feedback. Even free users will work.
Collect as many early users as you can, make sure they use and love your product, then
launch on Product Hunt and ask them to go and support you with the launch. People
who love the products won't mind spending a few minutes to write a good review for you
and upvote your product.
When you get a lot of upvotes, your product shows up early in the top spots, helping
more strangers see your products. And because you have spent a lot of time with the
early users to refine the product, it's now proven to be usable and valuable. When these
strangers try the product, they are more likely to love your product, giving you even
more upvotes.
Don't spend months to build your product in secret, then launch it on Product Hunt
hoping people will like it and pay for it — you're just wasting your launch opportunity. It's
much better to build the product with your customers, make sure people love (and if
possible, pay for) your product, and then launch it on Product Hunt to multiply that love
to many other people.
If everything goes well, the launch event would be a great way to get a lot of new users
for a short amount of time. However, in my experience, traffic from this usually dies
down within a week. That's why you should also look into developing other ways to grow
the product after getting this initial user surge, like product-led growth, viral loops, or
other long-term distribution channels.
Summary of What I Learned
Do "something" and find pain points while you are doing it. It's a great way to create
new original product ideas.
Build in public, the benefits outweighs the downsides.
Talk to your customers and build with your customers, iterate fast to capture the
hype.
Painkiller products are better, but vitamin products can also be a great tool to
generate initial traction.
Don't quit your job yet if you don't have a clear future plan and backup plans.
Get early users, work with them to improve your product, leverage launch platforms
to amplify your product initial launch, but don't solely rely on it as a distribution
channel.
Growing Black Magic to $14K MRR,
then back to zero
I started the year 2022 with great momentum. For the entire year of 2022, my main
focus was to continue growing Black Magic. I managed to grow it to $14K MRR,
everything was going incredibly well before a disaster struck. That disaster forced me to
sell the product for an undervalued price.
Let's talk about all that, but first, let me tell you about another significant change that I
made in 2022: I completely changed my lifestyle.
Living the indie lifestyle
Around the time I reached $5K MRR, I started to think about my work-life balance and
lifestyle.
You see, for the entire time since I started indie hacking, I have been working like crazy.
When I still had a job, I worked nights and weekends. Since I quit, I worked even more —
16 hours a day, building a product and building a distribution channel. I had to work hard
to reach ramen profitability of $1K MRR, my minimum level of revenue that was
sustainable for my living costs in Vietnam. When I reached $1K MRR, I was happy, but I
couldn't stop there. $1K MRR was just the point of sustainability; I needed to get as far
away from that point as possible to increase my chance of not having to return to a job.
Only when I started to reach $5K MRR did I start to feel safe. Assuming I couldn't grow it
any further, I could continue to get this level of revenue forever and I would be extremely
comfortable. Revenue of $5K/month is equivalent to the top 1-2% earners in Vietnam,
about 25 times more than the national average salary of $250/month.
It was at this point that I realized I had achieved my dream.
I'm not rich yet — $5K/month was still much lower than my monthly salary at my last job
— but I didn't choose to go on this indie path because I wanted to make a lot of money. I
wanted freedom, doing what I love, while still making money. More money is of course
always nice, but how much is enough? $5K MRR, $10K MRR, $50K MRR… I just knew
that I would always want more, and if I kept working 16 hours a day to reach that moving
goalpost, I would be miserable like I was while working at a job.
So I decided to purposefully cut down my working hours to spend them on other things.
I played video games, watched Netflix shows. I went on a trip visiting every city and
province in Vietnam. I went to explore caves in Quang Binh. I traveled to nearby
countries. I learned to surf and became addicted to the sport.
I learned about hardware programming, hired a hardware mentor to teach me all about
microcontrollers, MicroPython, ESP32, ESP8266, Arduino, solar panels… I created a
smart mirror, a remote slow-mo camera, and a few other useless hardware gadgets. I
also bought a 3D printer and built a tiny LED widget to show my MRR graph on my table.
Other than the in-real-life activities, I also built several new software products that are
not related to Black Magic — I still love building software after all. Most of these
products failed and didn't get anywhere despite me having more than 30K followers at
that point. With one exception, Xnapper — a screenshot app that I built — did really well
and made me some significant revenue on the side, but I ended up selling it later. I will
share more about Xnapper and all the failed products in a separate chapter later in this
book.
In August 2022, while I was still traveling, something magical happened. Courtland Allen
invited me to talk on the Indie Hackers podcast!
Indie Hackers podcast was the only podcast that I have listened to religiously since even
before I quit my job. It was the gateway for me to enter the indie hacker world. It inspired
and fueled my dream of becoming a full-time indie hacker, helped me discover and
connect with many legendary indie hackers on Twitter like Arvid Kahl, Pieter Levels, Kyle
Gawley, Daniel Vassallo, Justin Jackson, Andrey Azimov, and many more.
Needless to say, I'm super grateful and honored to be on the pod (plus nervous and
feeling like an impostor). We talked for about an hour on various topics, the episode was
published a few weeks after.
In October 2022, I traveled to Malaysia and gave a talk sharing my journey and the indie
hacker movement with the developer community in Kuala Lumpur. I later converted the
content of the talk into a long post on my newsletter summarizing my 2 years of indie
hacking; that post went viral for a while and became the backbone for the content of this
book.
Overall, compared to the year prior, 2022 was when I really started to live the indie life. I
was still working on Black Magic, but I only worked when I felt motivated and productive,
not because I was stressed or worried about finances.
If I have to pick a number, I would say I worked about 4 hours a day on average during
the entire time, and it really depended on my "mood". Some days I just didn't work at all,
and some days when I had something interesting going on and wanted to build it fast, I
worked 16 hours.
It doesn't feel like work to me at all, my work hours are very unpredictable, and I don't
have a schedule or a calendar anymore. There is one thing I know for sure: whenever I
work, it's always productive hours — I get much more done in 1 hour of focused work
than working 4 hours when I was at my day job.
The rest of my awake hours are spent on exercise, sports, travel, entertainment,
relationships, and experimenting with my other interests in life. I especially love surfing;
since I started discovering the sport, many of my other interests became boring.
Growing to $14K MRR
Now letʼs talk about Black Magic, where I spent most of my working hours in 2022.
I was still running DevUtils in 2022, but at some point, I felt that the product was
"complete". It had all the features I needed, I used it everyday, it ran well without bugs or
crashes, and I rarely got any customer support emails. It had become a passive product
for me, still generating revenue around $2,500/month from organic traffic, and I didn't
have to spend a lot of time on it at all.
Black Magic was different. There were a lot of exciting things I wanted to add to it, and I
was really excited to build and improve the product. It was also generating recurring
revenue, which was really motivating.
Over the entire year, month by month, I added a lot of new features to Black Magic.
I improved the metric tracking capability to report more detailed metrics and analytics
for tweets and profiles. I built a mobile app version for Black Magic with the knowledge
of Swift that I had. I ported the browser extension to other browsers like Firefox, Opera,
and Safari. I added daily and weekly email reports that would be sent to everyone
everyday. I also added tweet scheduling and threads features so people could draft and
schedule their tweets on Black Magic.
(Picture: An example of Black Magicʼs daily report)
Other than building and improving the product, I also did a lot of things for growth and
marketing.
I built a few small free tools to be used as lead magnets for the main product. For
example, Black Magic Glance is a tool to view analytics for any public Twitter profiles. I
crawled the Twitter profile page, combined it with the data I could get via the official
Twitter API to create a page that highlights the keywords the accounts were tweeting
about, their most active hours during the day, the other related accounts they interacted
with the most, etc. This feature also helped improve Black Magic's SEO, making it
appear more frequently on search engines.
In April 2022, churn was high at 17%. I integrated Mixpanel and started to track
conversion rates and optimize them. I made some changes based on the insights I got,
but I wasn't sure if they had any effect, though the churn rate did improve over the next
few months.
As part of my marketing effort, I got in touch with Twitter's developer relations team and
got Black Magic listed as an official product on their 
Twitter Toolbox page
. Thanks to
this, Black Magic also got a 
mention on Tech Crunch
 in April 2022!
I was still tweeting a lot about Black Magic every time I pushed out a new feature. The
top revenue source for me was traffic from my own Twitter account, so it really helped to
share updates frequently. I was both building in public, sharing my progress, and also
doing marketing for Black Magic at the same time.
I also tried running ads for Black Magic using Twitter Ads. The experiment didn't turn out
well — I spent $487.18 but only converted 2 customers, one of whom cancelled after the
first month. I have never had success with paid ads.
[36]
[37]
At some point, I started a Twitch stream channel and a YouTube channel, but I quickly
realized how much work was needed to run and maintain those channels, I didnʼt really
enjoy it, so I stopped just a few weeks after.
Around June 2022, I started working with freelancers to help grow Black Magic. I hired a
customer support to help me answer customer emails, and a developer to help me with
a lot of boring but necessary coding tasks. With the help from two other people, I got
back a lot of free time that I could spend on tasks that I enjoy more and have more
impact.
In November, I ran a Black Friday discount deal. It's the easiest marketing activity that I
can do without spending a lot of effort. It has become a tradition that I would discount all
of my products on Black Friday, and it helped bring in a lot of new customers.
Throughout the year, Black Magic grew steadily from $5K MRR to $14K MRR, all while I
was traveling and working ~4 hours/day.
By the end of 2022, I had the happiest year in my entire career, both in terms of financial
success and fulfilling life happiness in general. A quick look back on what I shipped in
2022:
I had a great work-life balance, enjoying my freedom as a full-time indie hacker.
Pushed out 9 major releases for Black Magic.
Built and released the mobile version for Black Magic for iOS and Android.
Released 4 major releases for DevUtils, each one with lots of new features.
Built 3 new products: EmojiAI, AskCommand (both failed), and Xnapper (a small
success). More on these later.
Reached $14K MRR with Black Magic
My Twitter account also reached 70,000 followers.
The $500K acquisition offer
Just when the year 2022 ended, in early January 2023, I was connected with someone
who was interested in acquiring Black Magic. This was the 2nd serious offer I'd got since
the $40K offer. This is how it happened.
I first got to know Alec via a random message on Twitter. We talked a few times online,
and in one of our video calls, Alec expressed that he and his business partner would love
to acquire Black Magic, as it would be a great addition to their portfolio of social media
tools.
We jumped on a quick video call, and I shared a few basic numbers like revenue, cost,
active users, etc. In the call, I asked for $1.5M total acquisition offer value. That number
came out of nowhere — it felt like the right number for me, I didn't use any formula.
There are a few reasons why I asked that much, mainly because I had no intention of
selling. I still loved working on Black Magic a lot, and currently, Black Magic didn't take
too much of my time while providing me a nice sum of "passive" income of ~$14K a
month.
After the first call, the buyer gave their first offer of $300,000 total value with $75K
upfront cash, which I declined.
(Picture: the first verbal offer I received after our initial discovery call)
The total deal value was just too low for me. Even if they offered $300K upfront cash, I
would still have had a hard time considering whether I should sell (and most likely I
wouldn't).
A few days later, the buyer came back with a new offer. They sent me a "
Letter of Intent
for a Purchase Agreement
" — it was my first time seeing this kind of document. Here is
the important part:
(Picture: a part of the Letter of Intent the buyer sent to me via email)
The offer was increased to $500K, with $100K up front and the rest as debt and revenue
sharing.
I also declined the second offer. However, this time, I decided to let go of my imaginary
$1.5M number and started to actually evaluate the value of Black Magic seriously. Here
is my analysis of what it's like to accept vs. decline the offer:
Decline the offer
Take the offer
Monthly
income
~$14K/month forever
(optimistically)
~$10K/month for the next two years
(debt payment from the buyer)
Work
I continue to work on Black Magic
Iʼm more free to work on other things
Upfront cash
$0
$100,000
Ownership
I still own the business, which I can
sell at any time later
I no longer own the business
Given that I still love working on Black Magic a lot, this looks like a very bad deal to me.
Even though I immediately get paid $100K, the downsides seem too much: I no longer
work on the thing I love, my monthly income is reduced, and also, I no longer own the
business. Why would I ever take $100K to exchange for all that? I can make $100K in
about 8 months assuming my MRR stays the same as it is today.
After giving it another thought from the buyer's perspective, I can think of a few reasons
why this can be a fair offer. This can be a fair offer if: I no longer enjoy working on Black
Magic, I no longer believe Black Magic will grow in the next 2 years, and I need a lot of
cash fast. Basically it seems like the buyer thought I was having a hard time growing the
business and needed a quick way out. Unfortunately, none of the above is true for me.
So I concluded that it was just not the right time for the acquisition to happen.
Throughout the process, the buyer was super nice and responded very quickly to my
questions. Unlike me, they have done a lot of acquisitions in the past. I even asked the
buyer if they were open to sharing more about the thought process of coming up with
the offer. We then jumped on another call where they shared their calculation
spreadsheet and the projections of Black Magic if the acquisition happened, and how it
would all make sense financially to acquire Black Magic. It was fascinating to me to learn
about all this.
Later on, I let go of my imaginary $1.5M asking price and asked for a more realistic, fairly
considered number. Unfortunately, we couldn't find common ground, so no deal was
made, but I learned a few things.
First, these acquisition deals are usually backed by a mix of loans and investments.
That's why they often can't do a lot of upfront cash.
Secondly, the "cost of operating" is much higher for them compared to me because I do
all roles and I know the business in and out, they don't, so they'll likely have to spend
more money on that after the acquisition (on training, hiring, etc.). Business profit will be
affected by this too.
It made me realize how crazily efficient it is for an indie hacker to run an entire business.
I imagine a minimum team that would be needed to run and grow Black Magic would be
one developer, one designer, and one product owner to make product-wise decisions. If
you pay each person $5,000/month salary, it's already a $15K/month cost for salary
alone, and Black Magic would have been at a -$1,000/month loss!
And lastly, a typical acquisition deal like this usually consists of upfront cash and debt
payments. The debt usually can pay itself using the profit from the business. So, lower
upfront cash means a faster return on investment. In this case, a $500K deal with $100K
upfront, I 
think
 they are expecting to break even at about 2 years in. After that, it's all
just pure profit.
Overall, it was a great learning experience for me. Now I know a little bit more about
what to expect the next time it happens.
How I lost Black Magic
I imagined I would continue happily working on Black Magic for as long as possible; it
would be my long-term business. But little did I know, that didnʼt happen.
Just about one month after I decline the $500K acquisition offer, a drama happened on
the Twitter platform that completely took Black Magic away from me. Here is how it
happened.
If you are on Twitter, you probably remember that around October 2022, Elon Musk
acquired Twitter for 
$44 billion dollars
 and took the company private. This was the
initial event that led to the downfall of Black Magic.
Twitter was a public company in 2022, and it hadn't been doing well financially since its
initial IPO in 2013. After the acquisition, Elon Musk implemented a lot of changes to the
company to cut costs and optimize its operation. One of which was to completely cut off
free access to the Twitter API, which Black Magic was using.
[38]
At first, I wasn't really concerned about this.
My rule was simple: I'll pay for the API as long as the business stays profitable. With
$14K MRR at ~90% profit, I was confident that the business would live on. I could
happily pay hundreds of dollars per month to use its APIs. I even added my credit card
to my developer account and prepared it carefully. But the situation was much worse
than that.
Around the end of February 2023, just a few weeks after the announcement, a big
company that ran one of the biggest Twitter apps at the time contacted me privately and
broke a big “private” news. They had been talking with the Twitter Enterprise team for
the past few weeks, and the new pricing would be, at a minimum, $42K per month (yes,
it's $42,000 per month, not per year). There would be no free plan and no smaller plans.
They were quite sure about the pricing, and they wanted to see if Black Magic could
survive the pricing change and, if not, explore some kind of partnership or an
acquisition. They also shared that Twitter would start to cut off API access "anytime
now," and planned to cut off all free API access of all apps in about two weeks. That was
all the information I got.
At the time, among other bad news on Twitter about employees being fired, there was
also bad news about popular 3rd-party Twitter clients and services getting shut down or
cut off access without notice. Products like 
Tweetbot
, 
Twitterrific
, 
Fenix
,
Talon
, etc. all became dead overnight. It was a chaotic time.
[39]
[40]
[41]
[42]
Knowing Twitter has a history of cutting off peopleʼs access without notice, the threat
from my private call "cut off free API access anytime now" was actually quite real. Black
Magic could lose access to the Twitter API at any time, which would mean nobody would
be able to use the product anymore.
I was put in a difficult situation.
First of all, there was no way I could afford $42K per month. I currently only make ~$14K
per month on average from the business. The whole product runs on the Twitter API and
could go down anytime now. If that happens, there would be no pivot, no way to adapt,
no way out. I would have to apologize to all current customers, refund everyone who has
paid, and shut down the business.
The only way for the product to continue to exist was to sell (an acquisition). If I decided
to sell, I would have absolutely no negotiation leverage. Also, if I decided to sell, I only
had about two weeks to complete the transaction and transfer everything. If Black
Magic's API got cut off before the transaction closed, customers would be mad and
start to leave, the whole business would be in a really bad state, and I don't think anyone
would want to close the transaction at that point. If this happened, the only option would
be to "sell for scraps."
I run a private Slack group called "
Twitter Maker
" (~900 members) where I gathered
people who build products on top of the Twitter API. The news was the main discussion
of the whole group during the entire time. Some people in the group got creative and
suggested a few ideas like using private APIs or forming a "partnership" where small
Twitter products unite with each other, use the same API key, and share the $42K/month
cost. However, all that was against Twitter's terms of service and was extremely risky for
anyone involved. So I did not decide to do that.
At some point, I considered just shutting down the business if my API access got cut off.
My thinking was that I kind of didn't want to go through an acquisition process. It's the
most boring and unproductive use of my time. I felt like it would have been okay for me if
I completely lost ~$14K of monthly income overnight. I still had income from DevUtils,
and I still had my Twitter account as a distribution channel and an interesting story to
tell. I also felt emotionally attached to the product and wouldn't want to sell it to anyone
else.
However, I decided not to do that, as the downside seemed much worse. Firstly, at the
end of the day, most customers don't care who owns the product — they just want to
use it. It would be sad and irresponsible if I shut it down, and nobody could continue to
use Black Magic anymore. Secondly, having a bit of final cash payout was still better
than nothing, plus I wouldn't need to refund existing customers. And finally, I guess I still
had a story to tell anyway!
My only option really was to sell Black Magic to a bigger company that could afford the
$42K/month cost.
When I say I would have "absolutely no negotiation leverage," it's not quite 100% true —
I had one leverage: I could pitch Black Magic around to see who wanted to pay the best
price. Given the time pressure, I only managed to get in touch with 3 potential buyers.
Out of the 3 buyers, I got 2 acquisition offers. I decided to move forward with the
acquisition offer from Hypefury
 — another big Twitter product on the market at the
time.
Everything went super fast.
[43]
[44]
I had previously known Samy, the founder of Hypefury, via the Indie Hackers podcast.
He started Hypefury as an indie hacker as well, which I admired. Our discussion on the
acquisition was quick; after some negotiation, we decided to settle the total acquisition
amount at $128,000, with $64,000 upfront and the rest paid as revenue share.
To put it into perspective, $128K is about 10 months of revenue of Black Magic. Usually,
a startup would sell for at least 3 times its annual revenue. In this case, Black Magic was
sold at about 0.8x its annual revenue. It was a really low price, but my thinking was that I
got at least something anyway, still better than nothing, and the product had a chance to
live on instead of completely shutting down.
So I went ahead with the acquisition.
After we agreed on the deal and signed the Letter of Intent, the first thing we did was tell
Twitter about it. Hypefury was on the train to bite the bullet, become a Twitter Enterprise
customer, and pay $42K/month to use Twitter API. By telling Twitter about our
acquisition, we let Twitter know that Black Magic was now a part of Hypefury, and they
could give us more time before they cut off Black Magic API access.
Around the end of March, Twitter started cutting off free API access for everyone.
Popular Twitter apps like Tweet Hunter and FeedHive were also cut off.
Since I was working with Hypefury to notify Twitter about the acquisition, we finished all
the paperwork and sent it to Twitter, proving that the acquisition was complete and
basically telling them "Hey, Black Magic is now part of Hypefury, and Hypefury is an
Enterprise customer, please don't cut off its API." However, internally, Twitter was also a
mess on their own with the workforce reduced by 80% — nobody could keep track of
anything.
On April 6, 2023, the worst scenario happened. Samy called me in the middle of the
night saying that Black Magic API access got cut off. I woke up and deployed a hotfix to
work around it by putting the system 
on read-only mode
; the whole product became
unusable, but at least customers who were still logged in could still open the app and
view past data without crashing.
[45]
Luckily, the Twitter enterprise team acknowledged our acquisition and restored our
access the next day. I was able to continue migrating the customers without affecting
the customers and revenue for the next few weeks.
On May 17, 2023, I sent out an email to all Black Magic customers, announcing the
acquisition. I didn't mention any of the Twitter drama and the real reason for the
acquisition because I felt like it wasn't a professional thing to do, but I guess most
people who were on Twitter knew why. My audience definitely knew, as I shared about
the whole thing shortly after on my Twitter and in the newsletter monthly update. Samy
was really open to me sharing all the numbers and process of the acquisition that's
usually forced to keep private with an NDAs.
And that's how I ended my time with Black Magic.
As per the acquisition agreement, I could optionally continue to work on Black Magic for
the next year with an additional salary that Samy offered. I'd already got paid $64K
upfront and I would continue receiving 20% of the revenue from Black Magic until the
remaining $64K was paid, so there was some motivation for me to continue working on
it. But to be completely honest, I was sad, and I lost all motivation. I ended up not taking
the salary and only spending minimal time supporting Samy in onboarding his team to
the Black Magic system, making sure everything was working normally without customer
interruptions.
In general, I have mixed feelings about this whole acquisition. On the one hand,
personally, I consider this a failure on my part for not making better business decisions
at some critical points. I would have ended up in a much better position financially if I
had done things a bit differently — for example: sell the business earlier at $500K, or
look for more potential buyers when I heard the news for a better price point, maybe?
(There was tremendous time pressure, but still, I think I could have reached out to more
potential buyers).
On the other hand, in terms of the business, I'm very happy with the outcome. I'm happy
that the business didn't have to shut down, that the product continues to exist, and that
people can continue using the product I created. I think Hypefury is a pretty good new
home for Black Magic. It is a unique and successful product, which I am very proud to
put Black Magic my portfolio from now on.
I guess it was also a good time for me to step away from the Twitter product market
after two years. The whole thing didn't come out as how I would want, but I think I made
the best decisions given the information I had at the moment. I think I have maneuvered
the situation as well as it could be.
A new start
Losing ownership of the first ever profitable business that I have built from scratch was
heartbreaking for me. However, it opened a new opportunity.
During this whole drama, something else also happened. Remember that I shared with
you that other than working on Black Magic, I also started building many other software
products? One of those products was TypingMind. Oh boy, TypingMind turned out to be
a great success, way bigger than DevUtils, way bigger than Black Magic. It has
completely lifted my indie journey to a whole new level.
But before telling you all about that, let's take a break and discuss the lessons I have
learned during this period from when Black Magic was at $5K MRR to $14K MRR to
when I lost it.
A few thoughts on Black Magic
Product Market Fit and Product-Led Growth
They say when you see PMF you will know it immediately, and I did.
I experienced Product Market Fit (PMF) during the time after "the big pivot" to when
Black Magic reached its peak at $14K MRR.
The signals I saw with Black Magic were obvious. I got new customers every day without
doing much marketing, my inbox was flooded with feature requests, influencers and big
accounts talked about Black Magic frequently and shared it with other people like they
had found a precious secret sauce, investors wanted to talk and invest, etc. That all
happened with Black Magic.
Picture: Jason Calacanis - a very famous investor - really love Black Magic!
Product-Led Growth (PLG) is another thing that contributed to the steady growth of the
product. Just by using the product, a user naturally made other people know about
Black Magic—that's free marketing.
I think PMF and PLG alone were the majority reasons that Black Magic was able to grow
from $5K MRR to $14K MRR in just 10 months. Even though I did a lot of things for
marketing, most of my effort didn't really work. Honestly, I think my marketing efforts
didn't affect how the product grew at all. My ads failed, getting listed on directories had
no effect, my SEO was weak, the free tools I created for marketing were fun but didn't
really get a lot of users.
It also made me realize that I suck at doing any type of marketing activities. The only
thing I can do and have control over is to self-promote the product with my personal
branding, and then, I heavily rely on the product being good enough for people to start
talking about it, kicking off the word-of-mouth, product-led growth effect. And I think
that's totally fine.
Personal branding is a powerful leverage to test the market and gather the initial user
base, and when the product hits PMF and PLG, it should be enough for an indie hacker.
It's much better than posting a product on Product Hunt or Hacker News and hoping to
get lucky.
How I work with a small team (as an indie)
When Black Magic was around $10K MRR, I started to work with contractors to help me
around the product. I worked with one support role to answer customer emails, write
documents, post change logs, etc., and I worked with another developer to help me do
various tasks around the product.
I have never wanted to manage a team, so the way I work with my team is very "low
touch", async, and remote. I didn't have to spend a lot of time on managing. I don't think
this way of working in a team will work for any team that has more than 3 people, but I'll
share a few things that I do in the team to give you some ideas.
The whole team works fully asynchronously. Everyone knows what to do at any time, as
most tasks are available to pick up from customer requests, our public roadmap, or bug
reports. So everyone just works on their own thing, not much discussion needed. We
have zero meetings; sometimes, we work for a whole week without talking to each other.
I don't do a lot of discussions. If any task is too complicated and requires a discussion, I
will just do it myself to save the discussion time. I'm all about less talking and more
doing. Occasionally, I would share my opinion and ideas on big technical features to give
opportunities for the team to learn and develop their skills, but honestly I don't think I
have done that enough — it's a trade-off. Most of my knowledge sharing and transfer is
done via PR reviews, short async videos, and learning by doing.
I also don't give deadlines on tasks. I chose indie hacking to be free, I don't have
deadlines myself, so I don't want the team to have them. I was able to do that because
the product is profitable, and I never make any promises on future features and release
dates with my customers. Tasks still get done and we still push out new updates at a
breakneck speed.
The team works fully remote with flexible working hours for the same reasons. I wanted
everyone to have the highest level of freedom possible. I pay (what I consider) generous
compensation for the team to make up for all the things they couldn't have while
working with me, like a fancy office, coworkers, gym memberships, and other traditional
company benefits.
Obviously this way of working requires a high level of trust and autonomy. I was lucky to
have found two people that I really enjoyed working with. At some point I was thinking if I
could grow a bigger team while keeping the same way of working like this, running a
team is actually not so bad.
That turned out to be a big challenge as I tried to add more people. As soon as the
number of people increases, more communication is needed, which means more
discussions and meetings — which I hate. So up until today in 2025, I still only work with
two other people, and I don't plan to change this anytime soon. I think this setup is the
best to maintain freedom while I can still enjoy doing what I love.
About platform risk
Many people asked me about platform risk after what happened to Black Magic. My
thought is that it wasn't fun, but it's a risk that I'm willing to accept.
I don't think people should stay away from product ideas that are subject to platform
risks.
Nobody can escape platform risk, it's everywhere: your service provider, your platform
provider, your payment provider, your company, or even your passport country. You can
spend countless hours making backups for every layer, or instead you can focus on
making the best out of what you are given in your current situation.
Building an entire business out of Twitter means that if anything happens to Twitter, or if
they ban me for a random algorithm flaw, my business is dead. But on the other hand, I
was able to leverage the entire Twitter user base who had the potential to become my
customers. That's a great risk-reward ratio for sure.
If I were to do it again, I would still do it without thinking twice. I didn't go into debt to
start that business, and I had a good run with it, so it's all acceptable risk for me. The
same can be said for all the other platforms that people are building businesses out of:
Shopify apps, Patreon, Etsy, or even the App Store of Apple/Google.
TypingMind: Catching the AI wave
TypingMind started out as a weekend project, just like a lot of other projects I have built
during the time I ran Black Magic.
Having learned all the lessons from my previous products about shipping fast, building
in public, building with customers, etc., TypingMind turned out to be my most
successful product (so far).
Within just 20 months, it has grown from $0 to $1,000,000 in revenue, and thanks to it I
have learned more things about AI, LLM, and a way for an indie hacker to get to B2B,
bringing my indie journey to the next level. It helped me truly unlock financial freedom
for the first time.
This is how it started.
My first contact with LLM
I created TypingMind in 2023, but the first time I experimented with LLM was in
September 2022, when OpenAI still only had the "text davinci" models. I've built several
products that make use of the API like EmojiAI and AskCommand.
For those who are not early into the LLM space, the text-davinci models are the earliest
LLM models that OpenAI released as an API in 2020-2021. It was a simple text
completion API powered by GPT-3, you can send it a string of text, then the API returns
the next tokens. It was simple, but already people have built awesome products with it,
most noticeably 
Copy.AI
, which made use of this to generate creative marketing copy.
I attempted to build a few products with this since it started becoming available. I did
make $20 from one of the products, AskCommand, an AI powered developer assistant
macOS app. But despite me having more than 50,000 followers and my tweet about it
getting more than 4,000 likes, not many people paid for it, the product didn't kick off (I'll
talk more about these failed products in the next chapter).
I shared this so that you have an idea that TypingMind didn't just come out of nowhere. I
was constantly looking for product ideas all the time, even when Black Magic was doing
great in 2022 (before the incident).
By continuously experimenting with new technology and trying new ideas, I was able to
get to know and become familiar with LLM early, and applied it in my day-to-day work,
generating a few ideas to try. One of the ideas I came up with was TypingMind.
How I came up with the idea
ChatGPT was released in November 2022, people went crazy with it, OpenAI really
opened a new era of AI. I was no exception, I started using ChatGPT heavily in my day to
day for everything.
The more I used ChatGPT, I started to find out there were a lot of inconveniences with
the default chat user interface OpenAI had at the time. For example:
I had to log in again every day (very annoying!)
ChatGPT typed out the answer very slowly.
There was no way to search past conversations.
There were a lot of other smaller pain points, but those were the most annoying for me.
Then, on 2nd March, 
OpenAI announced ChatGPT API
, I immediately came up with
the idea: I must create a new chat UI to solve my own pains! I started working on the first
version of the product and registered the domain name 
typingmind.com
 on the very
next day.
Over the next few days, I worked on TypingMind on and off. This was the same time
where I needed to deal with the Black Magic Twitter crisis, so I was quite distracted. But
I still wanted to finish this side project, so when the weekend came, I decided to put all
of my weekend into finishing it.
On Monday, 6th March, 5 days after OpenAI announced their API, I finished the first
version and shared it on Twitter:
[46]
The tweet immediately got a lot of attention, even though it didn't go viral as much as
the last AI product I created, I still decided to add payment to the product just to see if
people would be willing to pay for it. I added a simple early bird price for $9 (one-time
purchase) to unlock premium features.
To my surprise, I got my first sale for TypingMind just a few minutes later:
That feeling of the first sale never gets old! I was so excited!
In the following days, I continuously added more and more features. Every time I added a
new feature, I tweeted about it, and more sales came in!
Within 1 day, I hit $1K in revenue. One more day, $2K. One more day, $4K. By 10th
March, I hit $10K in revenue from my weekend project. Most of the sales were from my
Twitter reach.
I worked on the project like crazy. Added new features, improvements, and bug fixes
every single day, multiple times a day. Every time I added a new feature, I tweeted about
it, and that was my entire marketing strategy. You can see a timeline of all of my tweets
about the features 
on this page
, I used my tweets as a changelog page for the
product.
People love the product because:
[47]
It was faster than ChatGPT (at the time)
A lot of convenience features like search chats, build an AI character, prompt library,
etc.
People can use their API key, paying only for what they use. No monthly fee!
And most importantly (in my opinion): people don't have to re-login every single day
😂
Worth noting that as of today, most of these problems are no longer a problem, OpenAI
has already improved the speed and their UI quite a lot. But by the time OpenAI fixed
their problems, I had already gained a lot of users and improved TypingMind even more,
people had already fallen in love with TypingMind for various other unique features!
On 11 March, I launched the product on Product Hunt. With a lot of help from my
audience on Twitter and my newsletter, TypingMind got to #1 product of the day.
Revenue went from 
$10K → $22K
 in a day.
I was extremely surprised by the result. When I made my first sale, I thought, "Okay,
perhaps I could earn a few thousand dollars here." Making $22K was crazy and totally
out of my expectation. I totally underestimated the hype around AI.
From this awesome kick start, and with Black Magic being sold due to the Twitter crisis,
TypingMind has became my primary focus ever since.
Growing TypingMind
I spent the next few months with dead focus on TypingMind, improving the product,
talking with customers, and adding 
new features
, 
improvements
, and 
bug
fixes
 almost every day. I felt like I had done so much more in just a month than all the
work I had done in the last 3–4 months.
Picture: How TypingMind looked like in the early days
I applied everything I have learned from my previous products into TypingMind.
No over-engineering
I didn't over-engineer; I used the most simple tech stack that I had been using for the
last few years: NextJS, TailwindCSS, Vercel.
The first version (MVP) of the app only had a few features, but those features were very
valuable, so people didn't mind paying for it. Then I started to slowly add more features.
[48]
[49]
[50]
At first I used local storage to store the chat data, which browsers only allow you to use
up to 5MB, and then only when customers started to hit that limit (already making $30K
revenue at that point), I refactored the app to make it use IndexedDB where the app can
use all the available storage in the user's device.
The whole app is also a static web app, without a backend. Only after a while when
users started to use it more frequently, I added a cloud sync feature that allows users to
sync their data across multiple devices.
Ship fast, ship small, ship frequently
I took an extremely lean path to build out the product, one feature at a time. I don't plan
too far ahead, but instead, I iterate the features and "refactor the product" based on the
state where the product is at. For example, I just knew that at some point I would need
to support other models from Gemini, Anthropic, but instead of thinking too far ahead, I
released the app with only OpenAI model, and only came back to add the other models
later.
I shipped several releases to production a day. I always broke down tasks to much
smaller units that I could complete, test, and release within just a few hours.
Build with customers
Thanks to the audience I had spent building for the past 2 years, I was able to kick off
the product with a great start. I shared every update with my audience as I was building
the product, and many of my followers became my customers.
I talked to the customers every day. I did it via emails, Twitter, and watching people talk
about it on Discord, Reddit, etc. I really liked to make a customer happy by adding a
feature or fixing a bug that they reported to me within the same day.
Distribution with Product-led Growth
Since building Black Magic, I learned that Product-led Growth is the best way that I can
use to distribute my product. I still did a lot of other marketing activities, but my key
focus to help reach more customers is to make use of the existing customers.
I wanted to make the product so good and make the existing customers happy, so that
my customers could help me do marketing for me by recommending the product to
other people. That way I didn't have to spend much time on marketing and had more
time to build. A win-win for both me and my customers.
Product Market Fit
Unlike Black Magic where I needed to spend a few months and make a big pivot to start
reaching PMF, with TypingMind, I felt PMF almost immediately.
New customers were coming in daily and paid for the product without me doing any
marketing (other than just tweeting about new features).
People talked about TypingMind like they had found a secret sauce. TypingMind allowed
people to use ChatGPT on a "pay-as-you-go" basis directly with their API keys, instead
of paying for a subscription. It was new at the time and TypingMind was the first of its
kind to offer this, so it was special.
My inbox was flooded with feature requests and suggestions from customers. I created
a page on Canny to keep track of all the feature requests and allow people to vote for
what they like the most. I implemented the highly voted features that fit well with the
product vision.
Influencers promoted TypingMind on YouTube, Twitter, Reddit, newsletters, etc. without
me having to pay for it. VCs wanted to talk to me and invest in TypingMind.
I even got praised by Guillermo Rauch, the CEO of Vercel!
And because I was building in public and sharing revenue, copycats started to pop up
like crazy. I tried to keep track of more than 15 copycats in a list, but eventually I gave up
because it was too much and it was meaningless.
In the first month, TypingMind made $81K of revenue, then consistently made around
$30K/month in the months after.
And all of this was from one-time purchases. Let's talk about pricing a bit.
Pricing: one-time purchase or recurring
TypingMind started out as a one-time purchase product, which means people only need
to pay for the app once, then they can unlock all premium features forever. There are
many reasons why I decided to price it that way.
Firstly, one-time purchase is much easier to sell. People are tired of subscriptions, so by
selling TypingMind licenses this way, I can get more paying users early on, which is
important for kicking off the word-of-mouth effect in the early days. If I needed to
change the pricing to a subscription model later, I could always do that for new
customers (while keeping the same benefits for all existing customers who already
paid).
Secondly, TypingMind was a static web app, it doesn't have a server, a backend, or a
database. The app also uses the user's API key, so I don't have to pay for OpenAI usage
myself. Overall, I don't have much recurring cost to run the app, for that reason, I don't
need to charge my customers recurring pricing.
Shortly after the app was released, I added a "Cloud Sync" plan where I do need a
database and a server to allow users to sync changes between devices. This cloud sync
service has recurring pricing ($10/month). However, I added this in as an optional add-
on, only people who want to use it and enable the feature will need to pay for it. The
core app values are still in a static web app and can function normally without the cloud
sync module.
I spent a lot of time to make it sustainable with this pricing model. Every feature I added
to the app, I needed to ask myself whether this feature would include recurring costs for
me. At the end of the day, I want to keep the core product simple, just a static web app,
people can even download it as an HTML file and run the app on their own computer or
host it on their private server.
Lastly, I think the one-time purchase price gave me an advantage and unique selling
points over all other similar products on the market. There are just so many ChatGPT
wrappers out there that try to sell you $10/month subscriptions to access "unlimited" AI
chat with all the models, and they keep a thin profit margin by adding rate limits. I
wanted to make TypingMind different. There is no limit, no subscription, people can just
pay once and use as much as they want with their own API keys, or as little as they want.
Some customers reported that by using TypingMind, they saved more money. When
they only use AI a few times a week, pay-as-you-go with their API (plus TypingMind
one-time cost) is still much cheaper than a $20/month subscription. Plus, they can
easily switch between model providers (OpenAI, Anthropic, Gemini) without having a
subscription with each one.
On the other hand, even though I don't have a lot of recurring costs, I still think it would
be nice to have recurring revenue. I still need to do customer support after all, and if the
number of customers keeps growing, I may need to spend money on that.
I have already added some recurring pricing for the cloud sync add-on, but it wasn't
enough, not many people opt in for the subscription. So I thought the next obvious step
would be to go B2B.
Getting into B2B and getting recurring revenue
Every time my product started to get traction, I started thinking about going B2B. In my
opinion, the big money is always in the B2B market.
For TypingMind, I decided to create a team version, where people can create a chat UI
similar to TypingMind but for the team. This would be a multi-tenant SaaS that allows
people to create a team workspace, and invite their team members to it.
I built and released the first version on Mar 29, 2023. I made a separate landing page for
the new team version and called the existing one the "personal version". Then I added a
small self-advertisement in the personal version and tweeted about it.
The team version was priced at $79/month. Within the same day, I had already gotten
my first B2B customer, and people started creating internal AI chat UI for their teams.
Ever since the team version was released, every time I added a new feature, both the
personal version and the team version enjoy the same benefits.
Almost all of the B2B customers of TypingMind came from the personal version. People
who were using the personal version liked the app so much that they introduced it to
their manager to implement it for the whole team, some users are CTOs or CEOs
themselves. This still happens even today (2 years after the product was released);
many new business customers tell me on our demo calls that they have been using the
personal version for years, then decided to "upgrade" to the team version because they
like it.
Going B2B also meant that I needed to learn new skills. I added a "Book a demo" button
to the landing page and started to do demo video calls with potential customers. It was
the first time I had to do this. My first few demo calls were probably terrible, but slowly I
learned to be more comfortable and get better. The main goal in every demo call was to
get to know the customer, show them the product, and answer their questions.
I never want to have meetings, but I still decided to talk to a lot of business customers
during this time via these demo calls. The worst thing about these demo calls is that I
would never know if a customer is potential and serious or not until I talk to them. My
goal was to get to know the overall process and identify the segment of customers I
want to serve, then I would automate everything.
Thanks to these calls, I learned about so many ways I could take TypingMind into the
B2B market. Some businesses want to build an internal AI workspace for their team like
my original intention, some of them want to use TypingMind to build chatbots that they
can sell back to other end users, some want to white-label the whole thing as their own
and use it for their consulting firm. Some want to translate and distribute the product in
their local market. Just so many use cases.
I explored and tried to fit in with all the use cases, but quickly it became overwhelming.
Customers are asking for so many things, but there is only so much that I can do as an
indie hacker. I guess for normal businesses, it would make the most sense to start hiring
more people and expand the business at this point. I considered it too, but in the end, I
would love to keep it lean. I decided to narrow down and focus on one single use case
that I think was the most optimal in terms of cost-benefit: making TypingMind the
internal AI workspace for teams, ignoring every other path.
By observing other B2B products, I learned that case studies are quite important in the
decision-making process for the customers. I started to interview my existing B2B
customers to understand how they are using TypingMind for their team, then compiled it
down into case studies and published them on my website (
you can see it here
).
These case studies helped a lot, not only did they provide social proof for future
customers, but they also acted as proof to boost my own confidence in the product and
stopped me from doubting whether the product is truly valuable or not.
I also learned to set up the product to meet industry standards that many business
customers require. I hired a security firm to do pen-testing for the system (cost
[51]
$10,000), I learned to get the SOC 2 Type II certification for the product (cost another
~$10,000). I will share more detail about SOC 2 in part 2 of the book (Coffee chats).
By July 2023, the B2B side of TypingMind reached $4.5K MRR. The product as a whole
combined was making around $30K/month and was steadily growing.
Other than the cloud-hosted SaaS that I allow B2B customers to self-serve online, which
brings in MRR, I also allow big customers who need a higher level of security to self-host
the system on their own infrastructure. These customers are typically big enterprises. I
landed my first big contract with an enterprise company in Europe in August 2023,
bringing in $20,000 of revenue for that month. My biggest enterprise deal was $90,000
with a US company closed in July 2024.
Reaching $1 million
By November 2024, combining all revenue from the personal version (one-time
purchases), the B2B SaaS (recurring revenue), and enterprise contracts, TypingMind
reached $1,000,000 in revenue for the last 12 months.
The revenue from B2B was slowly growing and starting to catch up; in November, it was
almost 50% of the total monthly revenue.
Some thoughts on TypingMind
Going B2B as an indie hacker
Companies are much more willing to pay a subscription revenue; $49/month for a
company is cheap. So I have always wanted to get into the B2B segment. However, it's
difficult for an indie hacker to get into this space.
Traditionally, getting to B2B means you must have a sales team, get a lot of leads, reach
out to them, get on a lot of meetings, do cold emails, cold phone calls, etc. A lot of indie
hackers did these activities, but for me I just find that it's a lot of work, and I don't quite
enjoy doing these activities.
My strategy has always been using existing customers to reach companies. For
example, I first sell to individual customers (B2C), then I rely on these customers to
reach their companies. I later learned that this way of reaching business customers is
called a "bottom-up adoption curve."
I did that with all of my previous products. When I started building DevUtils, I had a
pricing plan for teams where I charged $5/month/user, but I rarely got any business
customers. Same for Black Magic—I created the highest plan where social media
agencies could sign up to manage multiple accounts. I was able to sell some business
plans, but I didn't really know if it would work before the whole product was killed.
For TypingMind, I finally did it. With just a small ad in a corner of the screen in the
personal version, I was able to convert many users to the business plan. TypingMind has
more than 27,000 paying customers using the personal version. By making these
customers happy with the product and letting them know that I have a team version,
they helped me promote TypingMind to their teams.
I was able to verify this by asking every B2B customer who signed up (or when they got
on a demo meeting), and almost always the answer was "
I have been using the personal
version of TypingMind for a long time
" or "
I heard about TypingMind from a friend
."
This comes back to the point about Product-Led Growth. I think it is really an underrated
way to grow your product, especially as an indie hacker with limited resources. It's
difficult and slow in the beginning to gain the initial user base, but when I have it, I can
simply put 100% of my focus on making the product awesome, and new customers will
naturally come in.
Working with B2B customers is not much different from working with individual
customers. Most of my B2B customers (99%) are self-served—they just sign up, pay,
and use. Only a small percentage of business customers request meetings or demo
calls. I minimize meetings as much as possible, but sometimes if the company is serious
and the deal size is big enough, I still do it.
To serve these B2B customers, I spent a lot of time building enterprise features like
single sign-on, team members, roles and permissions, audit logs, along with tons of
features in the admin panels. It was all worth it, as these B2B customers pay a recurring
price. Besides, other than the admin panel features, any user-facing features I built for
the B2B customers also benefit the individual customers, so there was great motivation
to serve this market.
Other than that, there are some other one-off cases, but nothing is unsolvable. Some
enterprise customers are concerned about security, for which I hired a security firm to
do a pen-test and security audit for the system and send them the report. I also learned
how to get SOC 2 certified (which I share in a separate chapter). Some enterprises also
require custom contracts, for which I hired a US attorney to help me go through
everything. It cost a lot, but I only did this for big customers on big deals (more than
$20,000/year), and I have only done it a few times.
Overall, I think it's possible for indie hackers to target the B2B market, and the easiest
way to do that (in my opinion) is to get to the B2C market first.
Listen to customers, but don't do everything they say
One of the big lessons I learned from TypingMind was how to effectively work with
customers to build the product.
The main strategy I used to grow my product is to make my existing customers happy,
but it's often difficult. People have opinions, some are good, some are bad. I had to
balance between building the product into something of my vision, or to build something
people asked for. I learned an important skill: 
listen to all your customers have to say,
but don't just do everything they say
.
When building TypingMind, people suggested a lot of new features and ideas, many of
them made sense to me, but it would take a lot of time and effort to implement all of it.
Some features sounded like a completely new product, some were just complicated. It
was also the time when I was trying to get into the B2B market, and business customers
also needed a lot more things that I wouldn't normally think of like version control, audit
logs, collaboration, team sharing, etc.
It's hard to tell exactly when to do what customers say and when not to. I treat it more
like an intuition. You have to have your own vision of how you want the product to be,
and then try to match it with what your current customers want. It's impossible to please
everyone. As an indie hacker with limited resources, this is even more important, as you
can only do so much as one person or a small team.
One example, when TypingMind started to get popular, some customers wanted to use
the app without an API key. For context, TypingMind requires people to bring and pay for
their own AI models, TypingMind only provides the chat UI. What these customers
suggested is to include some basic AI models into TypingMind by default that they could
just use, and they were happy to pay for a subscription like $8/month for it.
The younger, foolish version of me thought this was a great idea, so I created a similar
version called "
ChatOrigin.com
" that was exactly like TypingMind, but without having to
enter an API key to use it. I managed to get some subscription (recurring) revenue from
it, but in the end, I had to shut it down. It was too much work to maintain two different
versions because the ChatOrigin version was too different in terms of the pricing and
cost model.
Think about it, if people don't bring their own API key, that means I have to use my own
key to serve the AI models, and I had to pay for it. And then I have to limit people's
usage so that they don't send too many messages that make a loss for me. I also
needed to take care of my OpenAI account budget, rate limits, moderation, etc. And I
had to do that with not only OpenAI but also for Gemini, Anthropic, and other AI model
providers out there. It was a headache.
In the end, I thought back about the way I intended to build TypingMind in the first place.
One of the big things was to allow people to have a chat UI with AI models without
paying a subscription, and that's what made it special. I don't need to chase another
product and distract myself, instead I should focus on my original vision, which was
working quite well. So after a month, I decided to shut down ChatOrigin and
cancel/refund everyone's subscription.
In another example, when OpenAI released their Assistant API, the most popular feature
request on my idea board was to implement that API to TypingMind. However, after
looking at the API document and exploring a bit, I had a feeling that the API wasn't
exactly the best fit for TypingMind. I think the API was bad because it mixed the concept
of "agent" and "chat completion" into one flow, and it wasn't a good flow; the API was
overcomplicated with too many steps required to do a simple thing. I decided not to
focus on it yet and wait it out a little bit. A year later, OpenAI deprecated the Assistant
API—I dodged a bullet. The capabilities provided by the Assistant API were later
replaced by a set of much better APIs, which TypingMind supported along with MCP
tools.
Over the past 2 years of building TypingMind, there were many cases like this, and I got
better and better at identifying which ideas and suggestions I should spend my time on,
and which ones to just "keep in mind." I still think it's important to listen to all the ideas
that customers share, as it will help me keep track of what's happening and act as a way
to communicate with customers.
About hiring and building a team
When TypingMind reached around $45K/month, I started to feel the pressure to grow
the product. There are two main sources where this pressure came from.
The first was from the customers, people suggested a lot of cool features and ideas that
I thought were quite good. I wanted to have them added to the product, but I just didn't
have enough time, even with another developer helping me at the time.
The second source of pressure was from myself. While building TypingMind, I realized
many potential new products that could come out of it and rely on the existing
customers to grow. For example, I was using another product named CarbonAI to handle
the Knowledge Base module (RAG, vector database, document parsing). I thought I was
in a perfect position to build this product myself and use it in TypingMind instead of
paying for CarbonAI. I saw all the necessary steps that needed to build and grow it, it
was a good opportunity but I didn't have the time to explore it.
It was at the point where I think many people would have started to scale up, build a big
team — or if they want to go the VC path — prepare a pitch deck and go seek funding,
hire a great team, and expand the business.
I considered that too. On one hand, I wanted the product to grow bigger, reaching for
higher achievements. On the other hand, I value my freedom more than anything. So I
tried to do something difficult: growing a team while maintaining the same level of
freedom that I had.
For a while, I started to hire and trying to set up a structure that would allow people to
work in the same way that I and my two contractors were working, all remote and async.
I interviewed more than 20 people, onboarded 1 sales person, 1 developer, 1 designer.
However, for various reasons, both subjective and objective, none of the new hires
worked out past the 3 months probation time. I would be lying if I said that experience
didn't leave a huge scar on my morale. I was really excited when someone joined the
team, and then felt terrible when they left. I decided to stop all hiring altogether. I was
sad, but I learned a few more things.
Hiring and managing a team require a non-trivial amount of time and effort. I needed the
hard skills to find talents, interview, evaluate, set up incentives, etc., and also the
necessary soft skills. I wasn't ready for it, and I also didn't enjoy it. I just wanted to build
products, not teams. I felt the same pressure similar to when I was at a full-time job, I
didn't want to report to a boss, and I also didn't want to be anyone else's boss. This is
probably my biggest insecurity and weakness. Sometimes, I still can't believe I've built a
million dollar business without the necessary skills to lead and manage a team, that it
was probably all luck.
Another big lesson I learned from this experience is that just because the product makes
a lot of revenue doesn't mean I should start hiring. Things were going well and still are. I
was under absolutely no stress. TypingMind's core features are stable, new releases
coming out every week like clockwork, and the product follows the AI new developments
really closely (TypingMind is always among the fastest adopters to any new AI models
on the market). The business has all the metrics within what I considered a healthy
range: growth, churn, support requests, bug reports, etc.
Customers always suggest new features and ideas to the product, and I implement the
ones that have a good fit with the product vision and core values. For the rest of the out-
of-scope requests and my new related-product ideas, those are nice to have. Honestly,
even though I have a strong existing customer base and a 100K+ followers audience, I
was still not even sure if those new products would turn into profitable products on their
own. In my opinion, it was definitely not a good reason to start hiring.
It looked like I started hiring just because I could, not because I needed to. And that was
bad. Through all of this, I decided to continue growing TypingMind the same way I have
been doing. I still haven't fully given up on the quest to build a team yet. Maybe I don't
like to learn to do it now, but 5 to 10 years into the future, things can change, who
knows. It was a good learning experience for me, so I hope by sharing this, it would be
helpful for you too.
Reflection after 3 years of indie
hacking
To be more exact, my journey is more like 4 years because even before quitting my job, I
have already started indie hacking.
2020: started preparing for going indie
2021: built a product, reached ~$1,400/month and quit my job
2022: reached $10K MRR
2023: reached $45K MRR
2024: reached $1M ARR
These 4 years look like 10 years to me. I have learned so many new things and achieved
more than I could have ever imagined.
I have always assumed that successful indie stories I've read are extreme outliers, but I
could never have imagined becoming an outlier myself. I tend to think that I got lucky
"more than usual." Typical indie hackers usually take 2-3 years before they have
something considered successful.
Even though I have written and shared a lot during these years, by writing this book, I
gained more clarity on what worked well for me and what I would have done differently.
The strategy that worked
If I have to condense down everything in my journey into 3 most important points, it
would be this: be good at building products, build an audience, and product-led growth.
This is not the golden formula, it's just what worked for me, but I hope it will work for you
too.
The steps:
1. 
I can build a fully functional product from zero.
2. 
Then I share it with my audience to gain some initial traction and initial users.
3. 
Then I use the existing users to reach more users by relying on product-led growth.
Not all the steps work all the time, so I have to really make each step count and I put a
lot of effort into building the foundation for each step:
To know how to build product, I spent 7 years to hone my coding and product
building skills from my day job and side projects.
To get a distribution channel, I spent 4 years to build a 100K+ followers audience on
Twitter.
To grow the product, I learned to talk to customers and make the product so good
that my customers help me reach more customers naturally with product-led
growth.
I also have had to attempt many times with different products, learned new things each
time, and also increased my luck surface.
This strategy works well for me, and it can also work for you. However, don't force
yourself too much into replicating exactly what I've done. It's better to understand why I
did what I did and see if you can pick some of that to apply to yourself.
