For example, I built an audience because I needed a way to get my product to be seen
by other people, essentially building a distribution channel. There are so many ways to
build a distribution channel. I just happened to choose building an audience because it's
fun for me and it worked well for me.
Things get easier but not guaranteed to work
The most difficult period in my journey was when I got from $0 to $1K MRR. It was
extremely challenging, stressful, but also exciting and hopeful. I also think that's the
period where you will learn the most and relate the most to my stories.
As I started to get some foundation from the revenue and a distribution channel via my
audience on Twitter and newsletter, making successful products became easier.
I haven't been particularly good at making use of the money for product growth, but I've
leveraged my distribution channel a lot to test, validate, and build new product ideas.
I think this is an important point to keep in mind when reading my stories. Depending on
where you are and what you've already achieved, you may find different parts of my
stories more or less relevant to you.
For example, if you currently have nothing - no revenue, no audience, no distribution
channel - you may find the most useful lessons from the period where I struggled to
build and grow DevUtils, and build an distribution channel.
However, even with the strong foundation I have today, not everything I build
automatically succeeds. People tend to think if someone has 100K followers, they can
make money with a todo app. That's wrong.
In between the 4 successful products that I've built, I have many failed attempts, just to
name a few: AskCommand, EmojiAI, VoiceDual, SpeakDual, ImageSocial,
LandingFeedback, and a bunch more that never reached to the point I released them.
Just search for those names on my Twitter timeline and you'll see how I attempted them.
All of these failed products were built when I had 20K, 50K, or 100K followers, but they
didn't succeed for one reason or another.
Life hasn't changed much after $5K MRR
I remember while building Black Magic and reaching $5K MRR for the first time, I
decided to cut down on working hours, go travel, and live a balanced life that I had
always wanted.
Before $5K MRR, I was working way too much, probably 12 hours a day. Partly because I
was still unclear whether I could reach sustainable revenue that I could stick with for the
long term or not. I could live comfortably with a much lower revenue range at about $2K
or $3K a month, but if I lived too close to the line, it was risky. So $5K MRR was the point
where I thought I was safe.
Ever since I reached the goal and changed the way I approached work, that's when I
truly started living the indie life: working when I feel productive and doing other things
when I feel like it.
From $5K MRR to $10K, to $40K, and $100K, my lifestyle largely hasn't changed. I have
more money, but I'm not very good at making use of it, so it's all just put into
investments. My day-to-day is still almost the same as 3 years ago: coding, surfing,
traveling, working from cafes. I just feel good working with code and building meaningful
things for the world.
I chose to go indie because I want to enjoy my freedom, not because I want to change
the world or become famous or create a million-dollar company. Many times in my
journey, I've had the opportunity to scale up the team, receive funding, hire more
people, and expand to more products. If I were to do that, my life would have been
significantly different.
The fact that my life hasn't changed much from $5K MRR to $100K MRR is proof that I
love doing it this way. Stay small, independent, and free.
If I were to start everything again today
I would probably follow the same path as I did, but with some changes.
After I graduated, I went to work for other companies for 7 years. It helped me to hone
my skills and accumulate coding experience, building my advantage. I would do that
again for sure. Maybe I would try to shrink that down to 5 years. It was still a valuable
time in my journey, absolutely no regrets there.
When I feel ready and one of my side projects starts to make some money, I would quit
my job to do it full-time. It's what I have always wanted to do.
One thing that I would do differently is to 
build a distribution channel way earlier
 in my
career if possible.
I have learned that a successful product consists of two most important things: product
and distribution.
When I started my indie journey, I had max product building skills but zero distribution.
Only after I failed to grow DevUtils did I realize that and started to look for a distribution
channel.
So by building a distribution channel earlier, I might be able to shorten my learning time
way more.
In terms of what distribution channel to build, I would probably choose "build an
audience" as my main distribution channel again. I think it fits well with my personality.
During college, I have always enjoy doing presentation and have the class listen to me. In
the first few years of my career, I join a developer community group created by Google
where I write tech blog posts, speaking and conferences, and create demos for people
to use. I like to share things and I like to see people's reactions to what I say, and when
it's something useful, I feel happy.
Once I have good building skills and a good distribution channel, I think it's just a matter
of time until something clicks. I would probably build a few different products and
maybe fail a few times. And when I get to $5K MRR, things would be the same again.
Keep building, stay updated
This is one thing I learned from Pieter Levels (a very famous indie hacker): sometimes,
you just need to participate in an upward macro trend, then the market will bring your
product to the moon with it.
Pieter did this with nomad and remote working between 2014 and 2020, his two
products NomadList ($30K/mo) and RemoteOK ($50K/mo) grew like crazy with the
remote working trend. Then he did it again with the AI trend by creating PhotoAI
($120K/mo) and InteriorAI ($41K/mo).
In my case, my indie journey would have been very different without the AI boom. I was
pretty early in the AI space when I released TypingMind, and it became the biggest
product I have built, over 10 to 20 times greater in magnitude compared to all of my
previous products.
But I wouldn't have been able to capture that opportunity if I hadn't spent 2 years prior
to building an audience, gaining experience, practicing shipping real products to
customers, achieving some small successes and learning from some failures.
What I get out of this experience is that you have to keep building and experimenting
with new products all the time, stay curious, and keep opportunities open.
Being solo means maximum efficiency
Another factor that I think contributed a lot to my success is to be able to work solo.
By working solo, I eliminate all the meetings, discussions, communication overhead, and
go straight to building and selling. I'm able to make decisions that optimize the entire
stack across technical, design, product, support, marketing, sales, etc. - things that you
can't easily do if you have a co-founder or hire a team.
Having a team to build out your product is fine, but when you do that you are giving up
the efficiency benefits. Maybe it's just me, but I just think the more people participate,
the more time is spent on talking and less time on building. This inefficiency comes with
a very high cost in the early days.
The most powerful advantage of being an indie hacker is that you can operate at a very
low cost (doing everything yourself), which allows you to approach products that
traditional VC funded companies canʼt operate on, and make a living for yourself.
Future & Goals
My initial goal when I started indie hacking was $5K MRR, the further I go from that
point, the safer I am to not having to go back to the rat race. Over the past years, I have
gone way further than I could have ever imagined. Now my goal is no longer about
money or revenue.
Now I don't have any big goals, only general guidelines to live by. The most important
things I care about in life: health, relationships, freedom, and meaningful work.
What I'm currently doing with my products is to fulfill meaningful work, and my favorite
way to do meaningful work is to build internet software products. It's what I enjoy doing
and what I'm good at. I will do it in a way that doesn't compromise my other values,
especially the freedom.
Many indie hackers who have achieved success usually turn their business into a real
company by starting to scale up the product and hire more people, itʼs a common next
step for successful indie hackers. I have tried that for a bit and decided thatʼs not how I
want to grow and itʼs not my next goal.
I enjoy staying small and independent, working alone or in a very small team.
Building products is not the only thing I do and I don't identify myself by my products.
Nowadays, I spend more than half of my awake hours on improving my health (surfing,
boxing), relationships (family, friends, communities), and freedom (travel and
experiences).
I have been doing this since $5K MRR, it hasn't changed over the past few years, so I
don't think it will ever change.
The other products that didnʼt make
it
Other than the main products that I spent my primary focus on throughout my journey, I
have built quite a lot of other products. In this chapter, I'll share the list of products that I
attempted but failed to bring to a meaningful revenue level.
I will only list products that I have launched publicly (real attempts), not some unfinished
side projects. For each product, I will share the reason behind how I came up with it and
my opinion on why it didn't reach success.
My other products
The Simple API (2019) - Shutdown
Context:
Followers at time of building: 0
Revenue: $0
I rarely talk about this product because it was very short-lived. Technically, this was the
first ever product that I tried indie hacking with.
The idea was to turn a Google Spreadsheet with existing data and expose that data to
an API easily. I launched it on Product Hunt in May 2019 with zero votes.
Reason why it failed:
I wasn't being serious about building a product; the product was really scrappy, had
no proper landing page, unacceptable design, no clear pricing, etc.
The product was extremely niche, and had no purpose other than a reason for me
to tinker with the Google API.
I spent no effort to look for customers other than just posting it on Product Hunt
waiting to get lucky.
What I learned:
Be serious about building a product, a simple product doesnʼt have to be a half-
assed one.
Don't rely on luck.
I need to think about how to find customers instead of just building it because I like
to build.
Log Viewer App (2019) - Shutdown
Context:
Followers at time of building: 0
Revenue: $0
I have already shared about this in the first chapter of the book, so I'll just be brief with a
short summary here.
The Log Viewer App is a native macOS desktop app to view extremely big logs or text
files. The main idea was to allow developers to scan through system log files easily and
find patterns, identify error messages, etc.
Reason why it failed:
I over-engineered and spent too much time on building.
I didn't validate the idea to see if it really solved a valid problem; I was just inventing
a solution for a non-existing problem.
What I learned:
Don't over-engineer.
Validate the idea before spending too much time building it.
DriveStats (2021) - Sold
Context:
Followers at time of building: 460
Revenue: $53
Sold for: $1,800
DriveStats was a micro SaaS that helped people visualize their Google Drive storage and
find which files/folders were the biggest, so they could delete large files and save money
on Google storage.
The visualization looked like this:
I was able to make some small revenue from the product, but eventually it was difficult
to grow, so I sold it to a buyer from the MicroAcquire platform.
Reason why it failed:
Visualizing is fun but most people don't have a need to "save storage".
It was difficult to find customers.
What I learned:
Pick the right target customer. Google Drive storage is cheap, only $10/month. If I
build a product for people who want to cheap out on $10, I will have an incredibly
difficult time to make money from that customer segment. It would be much better
if I target and sell to rich people.
AskCommand (2022) - Shutdown
Context:
Followers at time of building: 50,000+
Revenue: $103
AskCommand is a native macOS app that helps developers with bash commands, saving
time to look up documentation from Google or from man pages. I used the GPT-3 API to
implement this feature, added a credits-based pricing and charged people on their used
credits.
The tweet where I announced the product went viral, but I only made a bit more than
$100 from selling credits.
Reason why it failed:
I think the product just wasnʼt useful enough. Developers are difficult to sell to.
What I learned:
Keep experiment with new products and ideas. GPT-3 API was really new at the
time. I didnʼt regret building it, you can never know if something is useful enough for
other people or not until you show it to them and ask them to buy. I only spent a few
days building this app, so I it was all well spent.
Chat Origin (2023) - Shutdown
Context:
Followers at time of building: 80,000+
Revenue: $73
ChatOrigin was a version of TypingMind that doesn't require API keys, and people pay
for a subscription instead of a one-time payment.
I created this for people who prefer to pay a small subscription to access multiple AI
models instead of a big one-time fee (which is the TypingMind's pricing model).
Reason why it failed:
The market was very competitive with tons of other ChatGPT wrapper products
offering the same thing with a cheaper price or even free.
It was difficult to find new customers.
What I learned:
Offering both the subscription model in ChatOrigin and one-time model in
TypingMind became too confusing. I would have been much better focusing on one
customer segment where I'm more comfortable.
I don't have to always listen to what customers suggest. A big reason why I started
ChatOrigin was because I listened to a subset of customers who wanted to have it.
It turned out to be a wrong decision.
VoiceDual (2023) - Shutdown
Context:
Followers at time of building: 110,000+
Revenue: $0
VoiceDual is a SaaS that allows people to change their voice using AI-generated voice
that would match their speaking style. People can upload or record a video of their own
voice, then the app allows them to select a new voice to replace their voice with.
I spent a lot of time making it work using various APIs. However, I wasnʼt able to make a
single sales (other than my own test transaction on Stripe).
Reason why it failed:
 The product didn't solve any painful problem. My initial thoughts
when I first built it was for entertainment, but I couldn't think of any specific use case to
use it myself other than a one-off fun thing.
ImageSocial (2024)
Context:
Followers at time of building: 120,000+
Revenue: $55 MRR
Image.Social is a SaaS that helps you automate your social poster images (open graph
image), and it does this by using the website screenshot as the open graph image,
optimized for all social media sites like Twitter, LinkedIn, Facebook, etc.
I built this because every time I create a new page on my existing products, I had to set
up a social graph image (for better display on social media sites). Since I created this
product, I've used it on all of my pages in TypingMind and DevUtils.
Reason why it failed:
Technically, it hasn't failed yet. The product is still operating at 
https://image.social
,
and I'm using it for my own products. It only has a few customers, making only $55
MRR.
It hasn't grown for the past year because I didn't spend a lot of time and effort to
promote it and do marketing for it (was focusing on TypingMind).
Maybe the product was in the category of "useful but not useful enough".
Part 2: Coffee chats
.
Chapter 1: The Indie Mindset
.
Should I read indie success stories?
I first learned about indie hacking around 2019-2020 because I stumbled upon an indie
success story on the Indie Hackers podcast. It surprised me that people can actually
make a living this way.
Before that, I had heard stories of people who build mobile apps and get paid by
Apple/Google, or build websites/blogs and get revenue from AdSense, but most of those
always seemed like they made use of some arbitrage money-making opportunities, not
"real" businesses.
Building a real product with real customers who pay you directly with their credit card?
And doing that all by yourself without funding or a big team? That was completely new
to me.
The stories got me hooked and inspired. I love building software, and if I can do that,
make a living from that, while not having to work for someone else, that would be my
dream.
By reading and listening to these success stories, it opened my mind and I learned that it
is possible to do such a thing, and it has been done before by other people. Those
people are mostly like me, so maybe I can do it too.
I also learned that there are just so many ways to achieve the indie dream, and there is
no single formula.
For example, one doesn't have to quit their job to go full-time indie to be successful. Jon
Yongfook quit his job to focus 100% on indie hacking, spent 1.5 years exploring many
different products until hitting great success with Bannerbear ($60K MRR). In another
story, Dan Rowden didn't quit his job until his product, ilo Analytics, reached $5.5K MRR.
Another example: Dashiell Bark-Huss got her product idea from a friend who wanted to
receive online gifts without giving up their privacy, then she built WishTender.
Meanwhile, Nghia Tran got his idea from his own problem while using an outdated proxy
debugger app and decided to build a modern one named Proxyman. Both products
achieved wonderful success at more than $40K/month in revenue.
One more example: Uku and Marko relied on content marketing and SEO (blog posts) to
grow Plausible Analytics to $1M ARR, while Tibo and Tom heavily used social media and
influencer marketing to grow Tweet Hunter to achieve the same revenue of $1M ARR.
There are just so many ways to success and so many ways to do things. There is no
singular formula. Everyone needs to find things that work best for their situation.
Reading these stories also helped me to boost my motivation; I'm inspired by all of these
success stories. I always tried to relate myself to each story, asking questions like: How
were they able to do what they do? What position were they in that allowed them to start
doing that? And how can I put myself in those positions? etc.
I'm well aware that the success stories I have read are outliers. For every successful
indie hacker, there are probably a hundred failure stories that I didn't know about. But
they say you miss 100% of the shots you don't take, so it was a big part of why I decided
to try to do the same.
Where to read the success stories
The single biggest source of success stories I've read is from 
IndieHackers.com
 and its
podcast. I used to listen to their podcast at lunch hour when I was alone during the
Covid lockdown.
On the Indie Hackers podcast, Channing Allen and Courtland Allen (two hosts of the
show) invited successful indie hackers to share their stories, and they did a really good
job uncovering many small details during the most difficult period: the starting up from
zero, or "building in public".
Obviously, sharing the revenue numbers was a big part of it, but the more important
things in my opinion are the steps and their thinking process on things like: what to
build, how they validate their idea, how they built it, how to get customers, what steps
were taken to achieve certain revenue milestones, the struggles, how things changed as
they grew, etc.
Other than the Indie Hackers forum/podcast, many of my sources of success stories and
inspiration come from Twitter/X, some from Reddit, and other blog posts as well.
The caveats
Like everything else in life, a healthy dose is healthy, but too much of it is not good. You
don't want to get too deep into spending all your time reading success stories - what's
more important is your actions.
Some of these stories can be like porn: some people want to get attention, so they only
share what's exciting and fancy revenue numbers in their stories, glossing over the real
difficulties they faced or a secret advantage they have, making everything seem easy
and fun.
So, to answer the question: yes, I think you should read the success stories from other
indie hackers, but keep in mind is that everyone's situation is different, and every story
is different. You shouldn't try to replicate any of these stories, but only pick what works
for you and experiment with it.
Build your unfair advantages
Unfair advantages are things that only you (or very few people) have that make it unfair
in your favor and give you a better chance of success. You leverage these advantages to
get a head start when building a new product.
It could be anything that's useful:
Some people have experience in running paid ads because that's what they do in
their day job, so they leverage that to sell their product.
Some people have a big network and know a lot of names to ask for help or
introductions, which also helps bootstrapping their product.
Some people have friends or parents working in niche industries (could be anything
like laundry, YouTuber, hotel, import/export, even porn) and are able to draw market
insights and pain points from there and test their products easily.
Some people are just geniuses at marketing and going viral.
Some people have 100,000 followers on Twitter or run a 20K-member Facebook
group.
When I first started indie hacking, I considered my coding skills an unfair advantage. I
was among the top of my class during college, I had 7 years of experience as a
professional software developer that I would consider "successful" (based on the salary
I was able to negotiate). And above all, I love coding and learning about tech, so it
doesn't feel like I need to try really hard to acquire those skills — it mostly feels like
playing to me while it feels like work to others.
I can work on both frontend, backend, database, devops, mobile, a bit of UX/UI design,
and I can build very fast. I also have experience working on some decently big systems
during my past jobs, so I know which trade-offs or tech debt to take when building a
product. Because of that, I was able to experiment with different products without
wasting too much time on building and figuring things out.
However, coding skills alone weren't enough, as my first attempt failed, so I tried to build
other unfair advantages.
That's the thing: you can build your own unfair advantages.
At some point after I built DevUtils, my 2nd product, I struggled with finding customers. I
realized I needed a distribution channel; I had zero at the time.
After considering all the ways I could reach customers, I decided to build an audience on
Twitter.
Having an audience is a massive unfair advantage. Imagine if you have 10,000 people
following you and are interested in what you have to say, then you would have a constant
flow of traffic going into your product simply by putting the link in your bio. If 1% of that
audience decided to buy, you'd already have 100 customers!
So I spent 2 years building a personal branding Twitter/X account, from 100 followers to
30,000 followers. It was a long and difficult journey, but well worth it.
Since I have an audience, every product I build afterward becomes much easier to reach
customers. The initial user base is so important, especially in the early days of your
product. You can talk to them, ask for feedback, and iterate from there. If the product is
good, you can even get a chance of going viral.
So, my advice is to identify your unfair advantages, or if you don't have one: build it.
It will take time, but the benefit compounds. Everything you do later will become easier.
And the more unfair advantages you have, the easier it is to build more. So you should
start today.
To find an unfair advantage that works for you, it should be valuable and be something
you love, so it doesn't feel like work to you.
Indie hacking with a full-time job
Not everyone can afford to quit their job to pursue indie hacking full-time when they first
start - it's too risky. Instead, the "default" is always to keep the job and work on side
projects first. That's how I did it too.
In this post, I'll share some of my personal experience with indie hacking while having a
full-time job.
Find time
I think this is the most difficult thing when you have a full-time job. Time is something
you can't get more of. So, to get time for indie hacking, there is not much you can do
other than spending less time on other things.
This may sound easy to some but can be very difficult for others. Everyone's situation is
different - one may need to change their entire lifestyle to have more time. But indie
hacking is never easy, so that's the sacrifice you have to make: working on your dream
business at night and weekends while others are spending time on leisure activities.
For me, luckily, I had it easy. When I started indie hacking, I was young, single, and had a
corporate job with a great work-life balance. All I had to do was spend less time on
Netflix and gaming to build my side projects. Actually, I have been doing that since
before I started indie hacking.
Iʼve always have some side projects going on for most of my 7 years working full-time
jobs. Iʼm the type of person who “write code for work” then in my free time “write code
for fun”. So when I started indie hacking, I was just simply trade out the “code for fun”
part to “code for my future business”.
If you are like me, then great! You are in a perfect position to start your indie hacking
journey.
For people who are in a much tighter situation, maybe you have a stressful job with a lot
of overtime work, a family with kids, or other responsibilities. Iʼm probably not qualified
to give you advice on time management given my experience, but I just now for sure that
it is possible, because I have seen other people done it.
A friend of mine, Daniel Nguyen, had a full-time job and is a parent of two kids. He was
able to squeeze out time to work on KTool, a Kindle app, and was able to reach
sustainable revenue to quit his job to be a full-time indie hacker. Damon Chen, founder
of Testimonial.to, is also a parent of two kids and had a full-time job when he first
started. Now he has become a successful indie hacker, running two products at $100K
MRR. If these two dads can find time, you can too.
Fight procrastination
Another problem when indie hacking with a full-time job is procrastination.
Since you have income from the job, indie hacking becomes "nice to have." You are less
serious because the consequence of failing is not that bad. So you may find yourself
procrastinating - every small "inconvenience" becomes a reason for you to stop working
on your project.
When I had a full-time job, there were some periods where I was more free than usual,
and I could come up with side projects and spend time on them. But as soon as work
became busy, or something else in life happened that had higher priority, the projects
got abandoned. From my experience, these "free periods" usually only lasted for a week
or two.
I think the best way to fight procrastination is to find motivation. Most of the time, you
procrastinate because you don't feel motivated. So find something that motivates you
about the project. Using your own product is a very good way to do it.
When I was building DevUtils and having my full-time job on the side, I used the app
almost every day even when it was still not finished. It was actually a great setup: I built
the app at night and on weekends, then during the day job, I used the app for my work. It
was like doing quality assurance for my own product.
Using my own product helped me find bugs, create new feature ideas and
improvements. Whenever I found a bug or had an idea to improve the app, I'd take note
and come home to fix it after work. I was never get bored.
If somehow you can't use the app by yourself, try getting some early users as soon as
possible. Receiving feedback and talking to users can also help increase morale and
motivation.
When you are motivated, all you think about is "when is the next time I'm free so I can
build this feature / fix this bug?" There will be no procrastination.
Legal implications
Depending on what kind of contract you have with your employer, there may be some
legal implications for you working on side projects.
In some cases, the employment contract you have with your employer will grant them
ownership of everything you create during the time of your employment. This means
they own your side projects too.
In other cases, the contract is less strict, and you can have legal ownership of your side
projects if you work on them outside of your official employed working hours, not using
any of the employer's resources, and not working on something that is a "conflict of
interest" with your company.
You can read your contract to make sure which case applies to you, or consult with
lawyers to be certain.
As a general rule, when you work on your side projects:
Don't work during office hours as defined in your contract (e.g., 9 am to 5 pm).
Don't use the company's resources (computer, table/chair, electricity, internet,
online accounts, source code, etc.)
Don't work on anything that is related to your company's products. Stay away from
ideas that are somehow related to your company's product.
Don't incorporate a company while you are still in an employment contract.
All of these are to prepare for the worst case.
I still see people using the company computer to do personal work (even freelance
work!) all the time. It depends on what risk level you want to take and how strict things
are at your company/environment. None of what I say here is legal advice.
Avoid burnout
Working on a full-time job can sometimes be stressful and exhausting. Working on your
side projects at night and on weekends on top of that can lead to burnout if not
managed well.
It's important to listen to your body and take necessary rest when needed.
Here are some tips that have helped me:
1. 
From 
the beginning, pick a side project that interests you
. Have a clear definition
of "done," clear goals, and interesting rewards to look forward to. When you can
clearly see the progress of reaching the goal closer every time you work on the side
project, it will affect your general mood, and that will keep you motivated and
energized.
2. 
Join a club/community/group who are also building side projects like you
. Ideally,
you should find people around you, so you can meet them in person and have a
chat. Meeting people online is also ok, but in my experience, it is difficult to stay
"relevant" with people you find online. You will have to try both to see what works
best for you. A mix of both is perfect.
3. 
Spend time outdoors.
 You should have a sport, go for a walk frequently, or do
things outside of the keyboard frequently during the week. While I had a full-time
job, I played table tennis every other day with co-workers after work. During COVID-
19, I took all the chances I got to go outside and see friends for dinner or for a drink.
Spending time outdoors is a great way to improve your mood and help reduce
burnout.
4. 
Don't skip your sleep, hobby, or entertainment time
. Remember, this is a
marathon, and it may take a long time. For me, even though I always have side
projects, I still keep the habit of playing video games or watching movies once a
week.
5. 
Strong body, strong mind.
 If you are not already doing some type of exercise or
sport, do push-ups, it's easy. You don't need to go to the gym. No equipment is
needed, nothing to prepare. Just your bare hands and a flat floor (which is
everywhere). Whenever you feel like you are "physically strong and energized," do a
push-ups session. Just push-ups until you literally can't anymore, then rest for a
few minutes and do it again. I usually repeat it 4 times, and it takes about 10-30
minutes for each session. Do it 3 - 4 times a week.
Do you need a group, friends, or
community?
If you are doing this full-time, I think you should find a community or friends who are
doing the same thing as you.
Building a business solo gets very lonely. It's going against the mass mindset of grinding
in the rat race. You will slowly find that there are fewer and fewer topics to talk about
with your colleagues or friends who work a full-time job. Having a community of people
like you will help a lot in terms of mental health and is good for learning and sharing.
I would be cautious of getting involved in communities where people talk too much and
aren't actually doing anything - they are talkers, not builders like you. Make sure you vet
people who you want to get involved with.
I always want to surround myself with builders, people who actually make something,
create something useful for the world, whether coding software, designing websites,
teaching, or even non-digital things like building a coffee shop.
The easiest way to find the community is to go online on Twitter, Reddit, Facebook. I've
also been in some group chats on Telegram, Signal, WhatsApp, etc. too - anywhere
works, as long as you enjoy spending time there to talk about the ups and downs of
building your own solo business.
For me, Twitter is a big thing — that's where most indie hackers hang out, including me.
But Twitter as a whole is too big and it can be difficult to blend in quickly. I'm also in a
few group chats of friends who I have met in real life that are also indie hackers. Even
though these groups are not very active (and they don't need to be), occasional catch-
ups with friends can help a lot with morale.
In terms of offline community, I have to mention the Hackagu community in Bali,
Indonesia.
It's an indie hacker group consisting of mostly indie hackers who want to pursue going
indie full-time. They meet with each other every Thursday to work together in a
coworking space (called B-Work).
The format is very casual: a quick standup at 9am, then everyone goes to work, then has
lunch together, then works again, then everyone will gather at 5pm to do a short demo
of what they've accomplished during the day, then they go for dinner.
I've been there once and I really like the vibe they have created. Even though everyone is
working on their own product, they're doing it together and sharing what they do.
Unfortunately, I don't live in Bali so I can't participate very often. But if you have access
to an offline community like this where you live, it would be a great way to keep your
sanity high and boost your morale, keeping you motivated in this long and difficult
journey.
Chapter 2: Finding and Validating
Ideas
.
The maker mindset
When I tell my TypingMind story about how I came up with the idea and build a solution
for it, there was a comment from a user that stood out to me.
The person mentioned that they also used ChatGPT, they also saw the problems of it
being slow, repeatedly getting logged out, etc., and they find it annoying.
But they didnʼt have the urge to do anything about it (even though they are a developer).
They just find that it was just “mildly annoying”, and ChatGPT will fix it soon somehow,
and it wasnʼt anything they should spend more time thinking about.
This is when I realized not everyone have the same way of thinking about problems.
Iʼm the type of person who always looking for things that can be solved by code, and I
optimize to squeeze out the last drop of productivity from everything I do.
Sometimes, because of that, I spent way too much time automating a task that could
have been done manually for much shorter time just because I enjoy solving problems
with code. But some other times, it help me create a new product that has never existed
before.
TypingMind is one example, a chat frontend app that produce output faster than
ChatGPT (at the time), I released the app literally the next day after ChatGPT open its
API because I use ChatGPT a lot and I couldnʼt stand its slowness.
The point here is that I constantly looking for problems around me and find opportunities
where I can make improvements or solutions (often by using code or automation).
I call it the maker mindset.
You should train yourself to see more problems in everyday activities - small problems
and big problems.
Some people always seem to be happy with their current position, their current process,
and their current ways of doing things. They don't seem to see anything wrong, nothing
worth fixing, or nothing worth improving.
I don't think it's a bad thing as a person, but it's bad for makers. If you don't see
anything "worth fixing" or "worth improving," you'll not spend time thinking of a solution,
and you'll never come up with new ideas.
If you want to become a maker, you should change that mindset.
Always ask yourself:
Can this be done faster? (Save time)
Can this be done cheaper? (Save money)
With only these two questions, you can already come up with a whole class of product
ideas.
Let me take another example. Xnapper is another product that was born out of my
observation about a small problem with screenshots.
I take a lot of screenshots and often want to make them beautiful before I share them on
Twitter.
Usually, I'll take a screenshot and put it in a photo editor like Figma or Photopea. Then I'll
add a background and other effects to make it beautiful. It takes about 15 seconds. The
output looks like this:
Usually, people accept that this is the way to go if you want to make such a beautiful
output. With the existing tools, it only takes 15 seconds. If you are familiar with the tool,
maybe it'll take 8 seconds.
But I did not accept it. I knew it was possible to make it faster, and I tried to find a faster
way. To my surprise, there was nothing on the market that could help me do it faster.
That's how I came up with the idea for Xnapper.com - a screenshot app. It reduces the
time needed to get the output to about 2 seconds with equally beautiful quality. The app
ended up making more than USD$10,000 in revenue in its first month.
The maker mindset will help you find new, original, authentic ideas, and it is a must-have
tool to have as an indie hacker. So think about it next time you see something annoying
in your life.
Scratch your own itch and product-
founder fit
Among ways to come up with new product ideas, this is my favorite: build to solve your
own problems. All of my products are created this way.
DevUtils was created because I needed a quick way to format JSON, convert UNIX time,
decode base64, etc. I knew that there were online tools to do it, but it takes time to open
a new browser tab, and I didn't want to paste my JSON data to random online websites.
Having a native app with a shortcut that can do it for you with one click is much more
convenient.
Black Magic was created because I needed a tool to help me build my audience on
Twitter, and I wanted the tool to be in the form of a browser extension for easy access.
Xnapper was created because I needed to create beautiful screenshots fast.
TypingMind was created because I was so sick of waiting for ChatGPT to slowly output
its responses.
Another common thing about all of the successful products I have created is that at the
time I built them, nothing on the market was good enough to solve the specific problem I
had. So the products I created were new and were the first of their niche category.
By building a product to solve my own problems, I automatically have “product-founder
fit”.
Product-founder fit means you (the founder) “fit” the product you are building, meaning
the product aligns well with your personal strengths, interests, and expertise. I think it is
especially important for indie hackers to have product-founder fit. It is the most
important indicator I look for when finding and validating an idea.
A great product-founder fit will help you in the following ways:
Your unique view and vision for the product that nobody else (or very few people)
can see.
It makes you happy when you work on the product, and this motivates you to
continue working on it, especially during hard times (e.g., before launch, slow
revenue).
You can save a lot of time doing market research or customer interview because
you are a customer yourself, you know what a customer need and how to deliver
them.
I usually come up with these ideas by observing the problems I have in my daily life. I
keep a note of problems that I find annoying and potential ideas and solutions I have.
When I see any problem that occurs to me more frequently than usual, it's a sign that
this is a repeated problem and maybe it's a good idea to build a product for it.
To avoid wasting time building products that nobody wants, I usually ask myself:
Is there already a product to solve this problem?
Can I build a product for this with my skills?
Is it painful enough to have a dedicated product?
Does it happen frequently enough for the user to pay for the product?
Do I have advantages in building this product compared to other people?
Every time the problem occurs to me, I come back to the idea and refine it a bit more.
Over time, when I have seen the problem happen frequently enough and decided to
build the product, I have already thought out almost everything and have a plan laid out
in my notes (or my head) of how I want the product to be. It helped me to build the
product much faster.
All of my products are created this way, and I still have a bunch more in my "idea list"
that I'm still monitoring whether or not they should be products. If you have been
following my Twitter, you may see I repeatedly tweet about these ideas all the time.
As an indie hacker, solving your own problem will also give you a massive competitive
advantage.
You understand the problem, you know what to build and where to focus. When you
build a solution for yourself, you don't need to guess what features matter most. You
already know the pain points and can zero in on what truly needs fixing.
This clarity helps you avoid the common startup mistake of building too many features
that nobody wants. Instead, you can focus your energy on the core solution that
matters.
When I was building Xnapper, the screenshot app, there was a very special feature that
absolutely no other screenshot apps on the market offered: balanced padding.
Did you ever take a screenshot and want the spacing of the background to be even, but
it's difficult to eyeball it and the screenshot always ends up with one side just a bit wider
than the others, then you have to painstakingly retake the screenshot with your trackpad
or open an image editor to crop it?
(Picture: The left is a bit too narrow compared to the right, not balanced and ugly!)
It's an itch that if you're not someone who takes 10-20 screenshots every day and wants
to make them beautiful for social media, you'll never know about. I made Xnapper
automatically balance it out for you, so even if I take an uneven and ugly screenshot,
Xnapper balances it and makes it beautiful. When I released this feature, people's minds
were blown away (and many copycats showed up and copied the idea).
When you scratch your own itch, even if your product doesn't take off commercially
right away, you still have something valuable - a tool that makes your own life easier.
This means your time building wasn't wasted, regardless of market success. You've
already created value for at least one person: yourself.
I use my own products every day, all of them! My dream is to replace all the products I
use with my own, as long as I can build them. Being a developer is awesome because
you can make software products that you can use, and you can decide how the product
works however you want it to be.
By using your own product (the term for this is "dogfooding"), you have the perspective
of a customer. This will be very helpful when you build the product. You'll notice bugs
and usability issues immediately because you're actually using the product daily. You'll
feel the friction points that need smoothing out. This real-world testing is far more
valuable than theoretical feedback sessions or surveys.
However, this approach also has some disadvantages.
Obviously if I scratch my own itch, I can only come up with problems that I see on my
own. For example, I would never come up with something like a "snowboarding schedule
and stats tracking" app because I don't play snowboarding and I have never set foot on
snow in my entire life, even though it is a huge potential market. In a sense, if I only look
to solve my own problems, I'm limiting myself.
Another point, not all of my problems have the same value of usefulness to other people.
Sometimes, I find problems that I think are very painful and worth fixing, but nobody else
thinks it's valuable. Before investing too much time, it's worth checking if enough other
people share your pain point to create a viable business.
The best products solve problems that are common enough to build a customer base
but specific enough that existing solutions don't quite work.
Research and solve other peopleʼs
problems
Listening to other people's problems is also a good way to generate ideas. Maybe your
friend is doing something, and they don't know that it can be done faster or cheaper. By
observing their workflow or talking to them, you may discover these problems.
Dashiell Bark-Huss was learning to code and was looking for a project idea to build. She
talked to her friend, who is a content creator, and discovered that there isn't a good way
to receive gifts from the audience that doesn't leak the creator's address.
So, Dashiell decided to build a product for her friend to fix that. Her friend can now use
her product (
WishTender.com
) to receive gifts safely without revealing the address.
Dashiell continues to improve the product, do marketing to find more content creators
like her friend, and grow the product to 
$36K/month in about a year
.
For me personally, I have never had any success with this approach, but it is a valid way
of coming up with ideas.
Back in the day, I used to ask people around me questions like, "Do you think any of your
current workflows can be better? Is anything inconvenient?" I was trying to find a
problem that I could solve with software, but the answers were not very helpful.
Very often, they don't have enough context and background in software/tech to be
aware of what software can and cannot do. They also usually don't know that something
can be done better/faster/cheaper with software until you show them.
So, I think in order to crack this, you will need to talk to them a lot, understand what they
do, and observe their process. You will need to be a good observer with good intuition.
Then, you use your background and knowledge in tech to evaluate and come up with
ideas to improve their process.
[52]
Make a simpler but better version of
an existing big product
Uku Taht is a software engineer. In 2018, he was working at a regular job. His company
tasked him to add analytics to their website. Instead of going with Google Analytics like
the team suggested, Uku raised his concerns about Google Analytics and began looking
for a simpler, better, and more privacy-friendly website analytics solution.
Google Analytics is the go-to way to add web analytics for most people. It's free and has
all the features. But over the years, the product has become way more complicated with
too many features, hard to use, and Google's practice of selling web visitor data for
advertisements started to become a concern for many companies at the time.
That's when Uku got the idea to build Plausible, an open-source, simpler, more privacy-
friendly web analytics platform. In his words: "I believe we can get 80% of the benefit of
Google Analytics with 20% of the complexity and cruft around it."
Within the next 4 years, through many challenges and hard work, Uku and his co-
founder grew Plausible to $83K/month revenue, all while maintaining the product's core
value to be a simpler and privacy-focused alternative to Google Analytics.
I hear stories like Plausible all the time:
Derrick Reimer, an indie hacker, created 
SavvyCal ($20K MRR)
, a simpler and
better version of the tech unicorn Calendly ($3 billion valuation in 2021) by focusing
on a simple selling point: "sending a meeting schedule link shouldnʼt feel feel
weird."
Tally Form ($100K MRR)
 was created by Marie and Filip to compete with
Typeform - a well-funded VC company valued at $935 million in 2022.
Justin Duke created 
Buttondown ($5K MRR)
, a newsletter sending service to
compete with Mailchimp - a $12 billion company.
All of these stories have a few things in common:
[53]
[54]
[55]
Small indie hackers competing with a well-funded VC company or billion-dollar
established tech giant.
They focus on a single feature and make it better than the product by the big
players.
They get to the revenue level that can sustain their own small team by capturing a
small percentage of the market.
I think this is a great way to get new product ideas for indie hackers.
Since you're an indie hacker working alone, you actually don't need a lot of revenue to
survive - this is an advantage. For example, Calendly is a $3 billion-dollar company with
tons of features, you just need to pick one single feature and do it better than Calendly.
Extracting $10K MRR out of that market would already be a significant number for you.
Find a successful product in a validated market, pick a feature, and build a better version
of it. The idea won't be new or original, but that's okay, as long as you can add your own
flavor and make your product better than the existing ones in some way.
Very often, when a company gets big, it'll have a lot of features, a big team, turn into an
enterprise, and move very slowly. This is your opportunity. If you can pick a feature from
that product and use your skills to make a better version of it, you can make a
successful standalone product.
By chasing a successful product, you already have a validated idea with a lot of
customers ready to pay. So, you don't need to spend as much time exploring and
validating your product (you will still need to spend a lot of time on marketing).
Some examples:
Intercom is huge enterprise software, very expensive, with a lot of features, the live
chat widget being one of them. Idea: Build a better live chat widget product with
some special features that Intercom doesn't have.
Google Analytics is the most popular web analytics platform. It has a lot of features
but it's very hard to use and privacy-invasive. Idea: Build a smaller, simple-to-use,
and privacy-friendly version of Google Analytics.
Webflow is a famous no-code website builder. It also has a lot of features. However,
if you just want to build a personal one-page website, it feels overwhelming and
complicated to use Webflow. Idea: Build a simple one-page website builder with
beautiful templates that anyone can use without technical knowledge.
To look for opportunities like this, there is a popular framework you can follow:
1. 
Find a popular product, something the mass market knows about.
2. 
Go to Google and search for "[Product name] review".
3. 
Find the 1-star reviews or negative reviews, and learn what other people are not
happy about the product.
4. 
Find a way to make a smaller, more specific, and better version of the product that
solves the issues pointed out in the 1-star reviews.
It's always better to pick a product or problem that you are interested in or have some
advantage to build, instead of choosing any random idea.
When you choose to improve on an existing product that's already successful, you get a
big advantage from the start. You don't need to wonder if people will pay for what you're
building. The market has been tested and proven by someone else.
Keep in mind that there's a very thin line between "making a better version" and being a
copycat. Being a copycat is bad, and you don't want to be one. Make sure to have your
own way of doing things.
The key is to add your own unique spin and perspective to the problem. Think about
what makes you different - maybe it's your design philosophy, your approach to
customer service, or a unique feature that the original product doesn't have.
Plausible didn't just copy Google Analytics - they completely reimagined what simple,
privacy-focused analytics should look like. That's the difference between inspiration and
imitation. Your product should feel like a fresh take on the problem, not a cheaper
alternative to the original.
Validate your ideas
When I was building my log viewer app in 2021 (my first attempt at indie hacking), I was
so fallen in love with the technical challenge of the app that I spent all of my time on
coding. It took me 6 months to solve the technical challenges I'd put out for myself and
to invent some more new challenges.
I was building a solution and then hoping to go looking for people with those specific
problems.
The log viewer app was a native desktop app that was designed to work smoothly on
terabytes of log files; it's difficult and challenging to do that technically. But the reality is
that no one stores terabytes of log files on their computer. I just invented a non-existent
problem to satisfy my technical curiosity and give myself reasons to use Golang,
Protobuf, and other fancy tech.
It would have been good if the project was meant to be a demo or to show off my
technical ability, build my portfolio to put in my CV for my future employment. But at that
time I was trying to build a profitable product. In the end, I learned two things:
Just because a product is insanely challenging technically, it doesn't mean the
product has any value.
And the other way around: A product doesn't need to be technically challenging in
order to provide value for its users.
I learned that to avoid wasting too much time building a solution for a non-existent
problem, one should validate their ideas. It means to test and see if the problem you are
trying to solve is "really a problem" or just something in theory.
Why you need to validate your ideas
Sometimes, I spend time on building projects that are meant to be a learning experience,
to have fun, or to gain some engagements on social media. For those types of ideas, I
don't need to validate. For example, I built this 
16-bit computer
 purely for fun and
learning, and it's time well spent.
Some ideas are in the category of "mixed," meaning I was building it for fun and
learning, but maybe if it gains traction it could be a business. I won't spend time to
validate those ideas too, I just do it. In the end, I'll get something out of it anyway, it only
costs me my time.
However, if I have already spent time to build a project, even though I will learn and will
have fun with building it, it's better if I can pick one that has potential business value and
solves real problems. It gives me a better chance of "getting lucky" in case the product
gains traction or goes viral.
Having said that, if you are serious about building a business from your product, you
should pick an idea that solves real problems, and you test if it is really solving real
problems by validating it. If you are not doing that, you're just playing around, having
fun.
Very often, the product that solves real problems can be very boring. You may not learn
any new technical knowledge while building it, it may not look impressive to put in your
CV, it doesn't require the latest JS framework, it definitely doesn't need to handle 1,000
concurrent users, so you will have to give up all that desire to learn, and be dead
focused on solving the customers' real problems.
Also keep in mind that once an idea is validated, it's not done. You still need a lot more
to build a successful business out of it: your execution, distribution, marketing, etc. But
we will discuss that in the later chapters.
In this chapter, we'll discuss a few methods that I have used to validate ideas and to
choose which idea to build.
Self-validation for product-founder fit
[56]
As mentioned earlier, product-founder fit is important for indie hackers. Working alone is
difficult, so you would need to get all the advantages you can have. Having a great
product-founder fit is a massive advantage.
To increase the chance of success, here are the signals I look for:
 Do I have any advantages in building this product compared to others?
 Do I use the product by myself? (Product-founder fit)
 Can the product market itself? (Product-led growth)
 Out of 10 people around me, how many will use the product? (Sanity check for
potential customers)
The more checks I have in the list above, the more likely the idea will be a success.
I'm the kind of person who can't work if I'm not motivated. If I build a product that I don't
use myself, I get bored and unproductive very quickly. So this self-validation step is a
must for me.
Test ideas with your audience or community
If you have an audience or you're active in a community somewhere, you can use that to
see if people are interested in the product.
I have an audience on Twitter, and I make use of that to test new ideas. Every time I
come up with a new idea, I make a very quick prototype and tweet about it. What
worked best for me was to share a demo using screenshots or record a short video.
For each idea I want to test, I usually post a few tweets about the product to see if
anyone is interested. Here are a few examples:
I tested an idea about a customer support bot.
I tested an idea about scheduling calls
This approach works best when you have a decent-sized audience.
It can also work if you're an active member of a community - for example, on Reddit,
Telegram Group Chat, etc.
The output I got out of these tests:
The general interest from people in my audience/community
Feedback and ideas around the prototype
Potential customers
Keep in mind that people's interest does not mean they are willing to buy the product if
it exists. So, you can use this as a data point, then combine it with other data points to
validate your idea.
Build a simple MVP
This is my favorite way of validating an idea.
"MVP" stands for "Minimum Viable Product": the first version of a product with just
enough features to be 
valuable and usable
 by early customers. It only works if you can
build an MVP fast. How fast? Faster than the alternative ways of validating ideas.
Build fast doesnʼt mean you ship sloppy product full of bugs. Instead, try to narrow
down the scope of the MVP version to make it simple, but fully functional.
You must have put a lot of thought into this product idea in the background for a long
time. You've already decided what the MVP will look like, what features are included,
and what's not included. When you actually build it, you just do.
Build your MVP and label it as "Beta". This isn't just a naming convention - it sets the
right expectations with early users and gives you room to evolve the product based on
feedback.
The key is to focus on one core feature that delivers clear value. Don't get distracted by
nice-to-haves. Your MVP should do one thing really well, even if that means leaving out
functionality that you plan to add later.
When I built the first version of TypingMind, it only took me around 48 hours in total, and
the app simply solve one single problem: being faster and less annoying than ChatGPT.
There was no landing page, no sign-up form, no account needed, but it has already
provided incredible value to the users.
At this stage, don't waste time building a fancy landing page. Get the actual product in
front of real users as quickly as possible. Show it to people directly and gauge their
genuine interest through their actions, not just their words.
Once you see positive signals from users actually using your MVP, then you can create a
simple landing page. Let people start using the product immediately, but make sure to
collect their email addresses so you can keep them updated as you improve the
product. Be upfront that this will be a paid product eventually, even if you haven't
figured out the exact pricing yet.
Your north star metric at this stage should be active users - people who actually use
your product regularly, or if you can manage to get paying users, itʼs an even better
metric to stick to. This is far more valuable than vanity metrics like page views or signup
numbers.
How do you build an MVP this quickly? The strategy is simple:
Stick to technologies you already know well
Leverage existing code and components wherever possible
Ruthlessly cut any features or work that isn't absolutely essential
The biggest trap I see founders fall into is treating their MVP like a full product launch.
Remember: your MVP needs to be built quickly. If you're spending months perfecting it,
you're missing the whole point of rapid validation.
Create a landing page with a waitlist or pre-order (and
discount)
This is a popular way to validate a product idea.
Build a landing page to introduce your product, what problems it solves, what features it
offers, how much it costs, etc. Then, you allow people to enter their email and join a
waitlist so they will get notified when you actually build and release your product.
You will start collecting emails from people who are interested in your product. Then,
you wait until enough emails are collected before building the product.
Let's say you have 1,000 emails in the list, and when you launch it, only 1.5% end up
being customers (typical conversion rate) — you will have 15 customers. Simple math.
The key to this approach is that you have a way to reach out to potential customers: the
distribution channel. I often say a successful product consists of two main parts:
distribution and product. If you don't yet have a product (only an idea) but you have
distribution, using your distribution to validate your idea with a waitlist is a good
strategy.
I used a waitlist when I was building Xnapper. When I first shared a short demo of the
prototype I made, there was a lot of interest and people really wanted the app.
So I created a product on Gumroad that I used as the waitlist, I named it "
Xnapper
(Beta) - Waiting List
" and shared the link on my Twitter. I got 360 sign-ups, some even
paid for it in advance (because Gumroad allows users to pay-what-you-want when you
sign up as a donation).
Then, I released the Beta version and opened up for early bird purchases at a 50%
discount and sold $10K worth of licenses during this period. At the time of launching
Xnapper, I already had more than 4,000 beta users.
If you are going to use this method. Here are a few more tips if you want to increase your
chances:
Use a heavy discount to encourage people to sign up or pre-order. The main goal of
this step is to see if there is any real interest in the product, so don't be too
concerned about losing revenue. I typically give a 50% discount for all "early birds",
some other people even give lifetime deals (paying once and using forever) to
attract interest. Having paying customers before the product even exists is the
greatest signal that proves the product has value.
Build multiple different landing pages with slightly different messages/target
audiences to see how to communicate your idea to people best. Maybe tweak your
product features a bit on each landing page. See which landing page has the
highest "Visit → Email Subscribed" conversion and build your product in the way
that best fits the customer.
Promote your landing page everywhere you can. It works best when it's put in front
of the targeted audience of your product.
If you are willing to spend some cash, you can even do paid ads to test the idea.
There are so many channels for you to run ads; here are a few I know: YouTube Ads,
Facebook Ads, Google Ads, Reddit Ads, and Twitter Ads.
After building the MVP, when sending out the announcement to the email list, do it
slowly. Reach out to 10 people at a time, let them use the product, talk to them to
see if it solves their problem, and ask for their feedback.
Tools and resources to build and test your landing page:
Gumroad.com
 is a good option. It has the feature to set your product price to $0+
(free, but people can pay whatever they want). I have used this in the past; it's good
to get your idea out there quickly and collect emails.
Typeform.com
 is a very flexible platform for running a quick waitlist sign-up site. It
has a great design and has the option to add payment too.
You can even use 
Google Forms
; it's free.
Airtable.com
 has the option to create a form so you can embed it on your
landing page. More flexible than Google Forms.
[57]
[58]
Carrd.com
 is a simple website builder that you can use to set up a quick landing
page.
Chapter 3: Building Your Product
.
Be a generalist
You are more efficient
If you decide to become an indie hacker, that means you will need to work alone on
many different things. This is difficult, but it gives you the efficiency advantage. You can
make decisions fast, with no need to communicate and coordinate between multiple
people/teams. As the only person building the product, from tech to business to design
to marketing, you can make decisions that optimize absolutely everything.
Many times, when I was coding, there were difficulties where if I would just change the
way the product works a little bit, the technical aspect of it would become much easier
and take just a few hours instead of days. I have the power to just change the way the
product works to optimize for technical simplicity. No meetings needed, no discussions
needed.
For example, when building Xnapper (the screenshot app), I initially wanted to have an
overlay that follows the user's cursor when they are drawing a screen area where they
want to capture the screenshot.
That overlay UI is technically complicated and requires using a lot of Apple's SDK API to
implement. So I decided to change the way it works right then and there: the user drags
the area first, then I show the overlay later. It is 10% less awesome for the user (which is
totally ok), but 10,000% easier to implement technically, so I did that.
And this applies to everything:
Building the UI for this configuration screen takes too long to add a bunch of
buttons, textboxes, checkboxes, etc.? Just allow the user to enter the configuration
as a raw JSON string. The users who get into this screen are likely highly technical
anyway, so they donʼt mind JSON. In fact, they may even like it more because now
they can easily save/copy/paste a set of configurations from somewhere to the app
easily.
You are updating the main flow of the app but there is a small feature that becomes
very difficult to integrate with the new flow? Just remove that feature (and
announce the feature removal to all users); if someone complains, we will find a way
to add it back later.
Setting up a plugin store is too complicated with so many boring steps like submit a
plugin, review, approve, listing, sorting, categorize, etc.? Just let the user share their
plugins on a GitHub repo and import them to the app via a URL.
I make decisions like this all the time, these are just some examples.
The key here is to make "calculated" shortcuts. These are almost like taking on technical
debt (or "product debt") that you have to eventually pay back at some point in the
future. By leveraging your expertise and experience, you can make good compromises
to help build the first version of your product much faster.
Be open-minded and ready to wear many hats
Being a generalist means you must be the one involved in all parts of building a product:
frontend, backend, devops, database, mobile app (if your product needs it), UI/UX
design, etc. If you are currently a frontend developer, learn some backend, and vice
versa. If you can't do it in your day job, do it in your side projects.
During the first few years of my career, I was mainly working on the frontend. However, I
always spent time at night and on weekends to work on side projects that involved
backend and servers. This helped me gain experience and later helped me land my first
full-stack developer position where I could learn even more about backend at larger-
scale systems.
I also think the best way to start your career is to be a frontend developer first, then
learn and move to be a backend developer later. I started my career by learning
HTML/CSS/JS and getting familiar with the web browsers.
Itʼs easier to approach for beginners as you can see your results right away by building
simple web apps that you can use. Working on frontend and implementing beautiful
designs created by designers, you will slowly pick up your "design sense" - this will help
you a lot when you design your own product in the future.
Later, when you are used to programming, software complexity, and the software
engineering industry in general, slowly move to backend or full-stack positions and bring
your frontend skills to this next phase.
Working on backend while having experience in frontend is a valuable skill that will not
only help you land more jobs but also help with your indie hacking journey later on.
You have to be comfortable building a complete product from zero, and thatʼs still not
enough. Building the product is just one part of building a business.
To be able to sell the product you have created, you need at least some distribution
channels, and to build the distribution channels requires a whole lot of different skills:
writing, design, sales, marketing, story telling, presenting, communicating, etc.
You have to be open-minded to explore, learn, and acquire these skills. You also need to
accept the risk that it will take a lot of your time and effort without any guarantee that it
will work.
When I started building my own distribution channel, because I chose to build an
audience on Twitter and my newsletter, I needed to learn to write well. Itʼs the most
difficult part of it.
At that time, all I have ever written are just technical documents in my job. I didnʼt really
have a blog and I barely write anything anywhere. Writing tweets and newsletters are
different. I needed to learn how to catch people attention and how to tell stories in ways
thatʼs not boring but also can give useful information to the readers so they coming back
for more.
Depends on which distribution channel you want to build, there are more skills you need
to learn. Basically, you will have to wear many hats and do everything thatʼs needed to
build a profitable business.
All indie hackers I know are generalist. They are able to do everything by themself:
identify a problem, build a solution, design, code, create a website, setup payment, find
customers, sell. Some indie hackers hire people, but only after the product starts to
make some good revenue and profitable.
Small input doesnʼt mean small output
Being a generalist pays off very well as an indie hacker. Building and selling software is
one of the few rare professions that allows you to create an incredible amount of value
for the world while working alone.
If you compare with other industries, let's say a civil architect, they can also be a
generalist but there is only so much they can do while working alone. They may be able
to build one house by themselves, but to build that same house 100 times, they'll need
to gather more people doing all types of specialized roles and coordinating with each
other.
Software is different. When you build software and it delivers value to your customers,
no matter how small it is, selling it one time or one million times is essentially the same
to you. Your output is not tied to your input.
That means you can stay independent as a one-person team and still provide massive
value to the world. When you provide value to society, you will be rewarded with money
- the social credits for everything.
Build MVP the indie way
You probably have heard about the term MVP (Minimum Viable Product) a lot if you have
been in the startup world for a while. But it seems to me that everyone has their own
version of what it means for a product to be "viable" and how to achieve it.
The same goes for indie hackers. I have my own way of building an MVP and I really
think it fits well with the indie way. The spirit of building an MVP is to allow you to test
the market, validate the product, get feedback, while still delivering value to the
customers, and do all that without spending a massive amount of time building without
direction.
MVP is a finished product of a simpler version
I always try to make my MVP the finished product of a simpler version of my real product
vision. Then I give the product to the customers right away.
If the MVP works well, I will improve the product to be more comprehensive, add more
features, and make it more useful. But every step along the way, every release, every
iteration, the product is a finished product that the customers can use and get value out
of.
When Pieter Levels first went digital nomad in 2014, he faced a problem of lack of
information about cities he wanted to visit next - things that nomads care about in each
city like cost of living, weather, internet speed, hotels, safety, etc. He decided to build a
solution for it.
But he did not build NomadList right away. The first version, his "MVP", was just a
spreadsheet containing a list of cities and information. It was very bare-bones, simple,
but had already provided value to its users.
He later made it into a website, adding more information and features, but along the
way, at every single step, the product was always operational and provided value. Today,
NomadList (now renamed to 
nomads.com
) is making $50K/month with more than
30,000 members.
I learned the way of building MVP from Pieter and other indie hackers.
When TypingMind was first released, it wasn't the powerful private AI workspace for
teams, it was just a simple static web app that allowed people to chat with ChatGPT via
their API key.
The MVP of TypingMind had no landing page, no fancy design, no backend, no
database, no sign up needed, no marketing copy, no video demo, none of that fancy
stuff. Just a simple client-side static web app and a buy button. With just that, from the
very first version of TypingMind, people already found value and were willing to pay to
use it because it solved their problem.
Over time, I slowly improved and expanded TypingMind to make it solve more problems,
and solve those problems more effectively, eventually creating a team version with a
fully featured AI workspace that is used by Fortune 500 companies. I also increased the
product price as the product became more useful and provided more value (but only for
new customers, all previous customers will still enjoy the old price).
I think that's the way all products should be built. It is way too risky to spend months or
years to build a "complete" product only to find out that it doesn't work the way the
customers want.
From my observation, too many people are afraid to release their product because it's
not "finished". "I will build an MVP and release that first", but in reality, the "MVP" in their
mind is not actually an MVP, but a complicated, over-engineered, feature-bloated
product. Many people don't know how to simplify their product into a simpler version.
Instead of trying to do so many features at an "ok" level, try to do one core feature at an
"exceptional" level. I would never ship a product with 10 features but 9 of them are
empty or "coming soon"; instead, I would just ship the product with only 1 feature, but it
is actually a very good one!
Be aggressive about cutting down all unnecessary things
There are just so many things you don't need when building the MVP.
Some people want to give the impression that their product is "professional," "trusted,"
and "sophisticated" by putting a lot of things on their website and product - a lot of
features, buttons, labels, useless marketing copy, irrelevant illustrations... It's all
unnecessary. Just focus on the core value of your product in the MVP.
For example, if you're building an Invoice Generator product for electrician contractors,
the first version of your app should focus on generating the invoice specifically designed
for electrician contractors, not anything else.
There are a lot of things that you may not need for the MVP:
You may not need login, logout, forgot password, or account management stuff
You may not need licensing management
You may not need fancy features like customizations, personalizations, etc.
You may not need a beautiful design
You may not need a landing page
You may not need legally well-written terms of service and privacy policy
You may not need social media pages
These may all become necessary at some point, but all of that will mean nothing if the
core value of your product is not solid. So don't spend too much time on them in the first
version; instead, do the one thing that really matters: the core feature. Everything else
can be added later via updates.
The MVP doesn't have to be crappy, make it simple by
design
Let's say you are using a design template for your landing page, it has all the sections
like testimonials, video demo, a beautiful footer full of links to pages like /about, /terms,
and social media links. If you have none of that, instead of creating fake testimonials, or
writing a scrappy page with AI-generated content, or recording a scrappy video, just
remove them all - you don't need those.
Embrace simplicity, maybe don't even use a template for your landing page. Just use a
white background page with a text header and a 2-sentence description to explain your
product, then a link to download the product, maybe a screenshot and a bit of CSS to
make it looks decent. A simple landing page like that works great to explain your
product, it has a clear call-to-action, simple to displays well on mobile, all with just a
little effort. You can always come back to improve it later on.
Don't add features that aren't ready just because you want the app to be "feature-rich,"
and don't try to make the app more comprehensive and more complicated than what it
is.
A product can be both simple and useful at the same time. That's what you should aim
for the MVP to be. Make sure the one core feature works and it works reliably;
everything else is optional. Then you deliver it to the customers and iterate from that,
slowly expanding your product.
How to do UX/UI design?
If you are a developer learning indie hacking, UX/UI design may be a tough challenge
you will need to solve.
Yes, you can hire designers to design your product, but that will take a lot of time (and
money). Not to mention that product design is not something you do once, but
repeatedly throughout the product's lifetime, continuously updating with new features
and changes. Outsourcing it means more friction, more communication overhead, and
you lose your advantage of moving fast by working alone.
I don't consider myself an expert in UX/UI design, but I think I am somewhere at 6-7 out
of 10, which is good enough to build products. Here are some of my learnings and
experience in doing UX/UI design as an indie hacker without having to hire a designer or
become one yourself.
User Interface (UI) vs. User Experience (UX)
Just to make sure we are on the same page, I want to clarify the terms UX and UI here.
