UI means the aesthetic aspect of the design: the colors, images, decoration items,
animations, etc. Its purpose is to make your app look professional, beautiful, and
attractive.
UX means the experience of the users when they use the app. Things like where
users will look first when they open the app, where they will click, what messages
they will see, and what steps they need to go through to do what they want to do
with the app. It's about making the app easy for users to use and helping them
achieve their goals when using the app.
UX and UI are closely related to each other but also very distinct.
How to do UI design
In terms of UI design, most of the time, having a beautiful, custom design for your
landing page or application UI is 
not necessary
. There are, of course, exceptions, but I
think this applies for most kinds of apps.
In my opinion, the value of the product rarely comes from the UI design of the product.
I believe if your product brings real value to the users, no matter how ugly the app is,
people will go through with it just to be able to use your app.
But it doesn't mean you can be half-hearted with the UI design. An ugly, messily
designed app will give a "low reputation" vibe to the users. You don't want to ship a
product that looks like a scam website or something that's been put together by a
student/intern.
My best strategy is to always make the app look just "decent" enough to gain trust.
There are a few ways to achieve this.
Use existing UI components
There are tons of pre-built UI components/templates that you can use. Just search for
"UI component library" on Google. There are plenty of free options and paid options for
you to choose from.
If you are building a macOS/Android/iOS app, use the platform design UI system.
If you are building a webapp using Tailwind CSS, a popular library is the "Tailwind UI
components" that has Marketing pages and Application UI pages. There are also many
other popular component libraries like RadixUI and shadcn UI.
Using an existing UI component library will solve 80% of your UI design problems.
Keep your design simple
If you have the core feature completed but the page feels a bit empty and you don't
know what else to put on the screen, it's OK to not put anything else.
It's OK to have a simple design, focusing on one simple core feature that your app
delivers.
There is no need to add useless stuff just to make the product look "complete" and
"professional." Things like a dashboard, sidebar, navigation, footer, dropdown menu,
about page, etc. — they are all optional.
If you add those things just to make it look busy and "professional," and the user tries to
hover/click on it and finds out it's just a dummy link "#" or a link to a 404 page, you lose
trust immediately.
You rarely need a custom UI design
Don't spend too much time thinking of a new design for your button or textbox. In fact,
don't spend any time on it at all. Use an existing component library.
A custom designed landing page or a design system for your application UI only makes
sense if you already have an established product. Doing it in your indie product is a
waste of time, and it doesn't make a difference for the users.
In the matrix of important/urgent tasks, creating a custom UI design is at the bottom of
your list. As an indie hacker, there are just so many more important and more urgent
things to take care of.
How to do UX design
Unlike UI design, UX design is incredibly important.
Ensuring a smooth and easy onboarding/using experience for your users is the most
critical thing you must spend your time on. If a user opens your app and doesn't
understand what it does or how to use it, they will leave.
A product with good UX will lead users to the "aha moment" within a few clicks/actions
after opening/signing up. The "aha moment" is when your users achieve their primary
goal when they download/visit your product.
To do this, you'll need to plan ahead and decide on things like:
What do the users want when they sign up?
What will they see after signing up?
Where do they click next?
What messages do they see? What's the content of those messages?
Where should this button be placed? On the top-left or bottom-right? Should it be
shown at this step or a later step?
etc.
It's hard to put together a standard way to do UX because it heavily depends on the
product you are building. Here I listed some of the techniques I frequently use to ensure
a smooth UX for my products.
Put yourself in the user's perspective
Become a user yourself and use your own product. At least the product should be easy
to use for you because you built it. Then try to imagine the perspective of your users,
without knowing the internal workings of the products.
All of my products are created from my own problems (scratch my own itch), so I rely on
this technique heavily.
Watch your users use the app
When you have an early version of the product, send it to the early users and ask them
to record a video of them using the product for the first time. This is the easiest and
most powerful way to see your users' real reactions and experience when using your
product.
I used this technique when building Black Magic.
When I finished building the new feature called "Magic Sidebar," I asked my early users
to help me record a video, with their face camera on, and go through the product
onboarding and use the main feature of the product. For every 10 people I asked, about
2 or 3 people agreed and sent me the recordings, some even commented with their
thinking and thought process as they recorded. I learned so many things by watching
those videos.
Interview your users
Similar to the "watch your users" technique, but you can do this in real-time via a video
call or in real life with the user. Then you can pause and ask questions right away.
I don't have a lot of opportunities to do this because my users are often not close to me
in real life, but whenever I have a chance, I love watching my users use my product.
Every time that happens, I always come up with new things to improve the flow to make
it better.
Don't always listen to your users
This applies to UX design but also for general feedback/suggestions from users: don't
just do everything your users say.
I'm not talking about cases where there are some random/small number of users asking
for highly specific things or they want the product to behave in certain ways that fit their
unique workflow — those cases are obvious. I'm talking about cases when even a
majority of users want a certain thing, you still don't have to always do it.
Sometimes, you have to trust your instinct and your unique idea of how you want the
product to work. It may be the only thing that makes a difference and makes it special.
Of course, don't take it to an extreme level (because then you'll have no customers), but
the right balance applied at the right time can do wonders.
When I first created TypingMind, there were so many users who asked me why the app
didn't include a built-in API that charged on credits instead of asking every user to
provide their API keys. I didn't make that change because if I did, TypingMind would
have been a different type of app — it's just not what I wanted to steer the product
toward.
Just learn UX design
UX design is interesting; spend time learning it. There are many ways to learn UX
design: read books, watch videos, buy a course, follow designers on social media, etc.
My favorite method to learn UX: pay more attention to how other successful products
are doing it. It starts by being able to tell the difference between bad UX and good UX.
Then you learn to avoid bad UX in your products, then you learn to create new good UX.
Learning UX design will make you a better product designer; it's a long-term skill that
compounds with your indie journey and will never be obsolete.
About the tech stack
Mindset
Donʼt use the “best tool for the job”, use the “best tool for you”. Don't use that new
JavaScript framework that they just released last month for your indie product.
If your goal is to learn that framework, then fine, do it. But if you're serious about
bootstrapping a business, then you should try to eliminate as many unknowns as
possible. Using a new JavaScript framework or a new technology is a big unknown.
Instead, use the framework you're most familiar with, so you don't have to spend time
learning new ways of doing things in the new framework and can use that time to talk to
users, learn marketing, and sell.
The same thing applies to everything else in the tech stack: the database, tooling,
server, service provider, everything.
Ideally, by the time you start indie hacking, you should already have a solid stack that
you're familiar with and can build a product straight from zero without much hiccup.
Then just double down on that tech stack and build every new product with it to save as
much time as possible.
My tech stack
For me, I'm most comfortable working with JavaScript. My tech stack is:
JavaScript (not TypeScript, because TypeScript slows me down with all the type
syntax).
ReactJS, NextJS for frontend and API.
ExpressJS if I need a separate backend, used with 
`
pm2
`
 for process management.
If I need a background worker in my product, I'd just use NodeJS with
`
setInterval
`
 or a 
`
setTimeout
`
 loop, or depends on the situation, I also use cron
jobs.
MySQL for database.
AWS EC2 and Vercel for deployment, depends on type of project.
AWS RDS for database.
TailwindCSS for UI, I also bought and use Tailwind Components UI library, but there
are many free alternatives.
Sentry for error reporting
Cloudflare for DNS
Mailgun for sending emails
My top priority when it comes to tech is to keep it as simple as possible. The fewer
dependencies, the better. I want to write code in such a way that a junior developer can
read, understand, and modify.
With a simple tech stack, I can move fast at my own speed instead of having to struggle
with the limitations and guardrails of frameworks/libraries.
Some of the unconventional things I do that you may find strange:
For authentication, I always use magic links sent via email. It simplifies user
management and is the easiest to implement securely. I don't want to rely on any
3rd party to handle my users' data. I never use passwords in my apps, and I don't
use social logins. Even if I do use social login, I will implement the flow within the
app instead of relying on services like Firebase or Supabase.
I don't use any ORM. All of my database queries are in the form of prepared
parameterized statements with raw SQL. For me, ORMs just create an additional
layer of complexity and new rules to follow while offering little or no benefit.
I don't use TypeScript. I think TypeScript is better when you work in a big team
where types help add extra protection to avoid bugs. I work alone, so it just gets in
my way. Besides, I enjoy the flexibility and the dynamic nature of JavaScript - it
helps me build faster.
I use NextJS but I only use the bottom 10% most basic features of the framework. I
don't use things like Link, Image, app router, font optimizations, lazy import,
Suspend, etc. The reason is that I want to have as few framework-specific things in
my code as possible and stick with React and Web standards. After over-
engineering so many times in my past projects, I believe this is the best way to
ensure the product lives for the long term (10+ years). This applies to any
technologies, frameworks, libraries, and programming languages I use - always
stick to the basics and only use the advanced things when absolutely necessary.
I always prioritize the simplicity of my tech stack, so I tend to use as many product
offerings from a service as possible to avoid having to set up extra dependencies. For
example, I use Vercel for deployment, but also analytics, logging, etc.
Very often people ask why I use a specific piece of tech for a project and expect me to
come up with a long list of pros and cons. The answer is often very disappointing: it's
because I'm most familiar with that tech. I choose the tech because it's the best tool for
me, not the best tool for the job.
The trade-off
This practice of choosing the tech stack comes with trade-offs, of course.
By continuing to use the same tech over and over again, and stopping myself from
updating with new tech, I risk becoming outdated. I haven't changed my tech stack for
years!
I try to keep myself up to date with new tech from time to time, but it hasn't been the
priority like it used to be. It hurts a bit, as I used to really love learning new things in tech
and trying out new frameworks, libraries, and services. But to became an indie hacker, I
have to accept it and use my time on other things.
Build many products
Why I build many products
At any given time, I always have one main product where I spend most of my focus time.
However, I always build "side products" all the time.
Most of these "side products" don't have much financial success, but they've helped
motivate me (not get bored), keep me up to date with the market, can be used as
marketing devices for my main product, and some of them have opened up new
opportunities and become my next big product.
The key here is that I only have one thing as my main focus at one time.
When I first started, DevUtils was my main focus. Then, I built Black Magic, which then
became my main focus. When Black Magic reached $14K MRR and Twitter changed
their API pricing, effectively killing the product, I was already working on TypingMind for
a few weeks. TypingMind grew like crazy and has become my main focus ever since.
I love building products, and if I just keep building one thing over and over again without
anything new, I will get bored and stressed. I need meaningful work, so I start new
projects all the time. It also helps open new opportunities, keeps me active, and up to
date with the tech world.
If I had just spent all of my time focusing on DevUtils, my other products would have
never existed: Black Magic, Xnapper, TypingMind. I love DevUtils, it is making around
$7,000/month, but if I get comfortable and chill out, I will limit myself and slowly drift out
of the market. DevUtils is a very well-scoped niche product, and it's almost feature-
complete - there aren't many exciting things to add to the app these days without
making it feature-bloated.
Black Magic was different; it was fun, exciting, and I had so many ideas to build and
experiment with. I had a lot of fun with it. But when ChatGPT and AI became popular, I
knew I had to build something in the AI space, so I started building a lot of small side
projects. TypingMind was one of those small side projects and luckily it got a lot more
traction than any other projects, eventually turning into a full-blown product like today.
Imagine if I only worked on Black Magic at the time and completely ignored everything
else, then Twitter changed their API pricing and killed the product - that would have
been bad.
Coming back to the point of having one main focus at a time: even if I want to, I can't
give my full attention to two parallel products. It's part of the reason why I sold Xnapper.
Xnapper started out as a side project like many other side projects, but then it took off
and became a product on its own.
I was able to work on it for a few months, but at the time Black Magic was my main
focus. I didn't have a lot of time to continue working on Xnapper, I felt like I was wasting
its potential, so I decided to sell it to another indie hacker so they could continue
developing the app while I continued focusing on Black Magic.
So my advice for new indie hackers is to always have one main product to focus on, but
never stop experimenting with new products. It can be your side projects that you can
also use as a marketing opportunity for the main product, or something else completely
to experiment with new tech, new APIs, new opportunities in the market.
When to call it quit and move on to the next product?
When I say "having one main focus," I assume the main focus is a profitable product you
are building (or at least the product is generating some revenue).
But when you are just starting out, most likely you are building multiple products and
don't really have a main one, or you are building one product but aren't sure if that's
going to be a success. It can be difficult to decide if you should continue to focus on it
or if you should move on to the next one.
You're not going to like my advice on this, but here it is: 
use your intuition
.
There isn't an easy way to make the decision to continue investing your time into the
current product or move on to the next one. If there were, indie hacking would have
been too easy.
When Nathan Barry started ConvertKit (an email marketing platform) in 2013, even with
an existing strong online audience via his books and online courses, Nathan struggled to
reach his target of $5,000 MRR within the first 6 months. ConvertKit was only able to
reach $2,000 MRR. For the next year, ConvertKit stagnated, even going down in revenue
to $1,300 MRR.
In late 2014, Nathan doubled down on marketing and massively changed his strategy for
customer acquisition, finally unlocking the magical hockey stick product growth.
ConvertKit went from $1,300 MRR to $2,000 MRR the next month, then $15K MRR by
July 2015 and $98K MRR at the beginning of 2016. Today, ConvertKit is an $8M+
business.
On the other hand, Jon Yongfook started his indie journey in 2018 by setting out to build
12 products in 12 months as a challenge. By forcing himself to build and actually ship a
complete and simple product with a strict deadline, he learned not to procrastinate and
not to give up on unfinished projects halfway (like most developers do with their side
projects). It gave him the freedom to explore all the ideas he wanted to pursue.
By the time he reached the 7th product, he concluded the challenge as he started to
feel a bit burned out. However, he learned many things from the challenge and gained
one valuable insight about himself: he likes to work in the API/image
automation/generation space. That insight and the skills he learned helped him create
Bannerbear - an image automation as an API SaaS. Bannerbear took 1 year to reach
$10K MRR and continued to grow to $83K MRR today.
I just gave you two real examples of both scenarios: continue on the current thing or
move on to the next thing until you find success. All this is to say that there isn't the
"right" way to decide whether to continue focusing on your current product or move on
to the next one (or when). You'll have to trust your instinct and make that decision based
on your specific situation.
Only one thing I know for sure is that when I attempt to build a product that I hope will
become a real business, I always do my best and give it a serious attempt. I won't just
throw everything against the wall and see what sticks.
Donʼt just “see what stick”, give it a real try, inspect every
failure
The daydream of every developer is that they will build a product, then post it
somewhere, then it will go viral, then customers keep coming in, then people will tell
other people about the product, and revenue will keep flowing in, product grow bigger
and bigger.
I have seen so many indie hackers building 10+ products, but every time they finished
one, they post the product on Product Hunt, Twitter, or Reddit, see it got less than 10
upvotes, zero revenue, disappointed, then they give up and move on to the next one.
Let me show you something, here is the 5 different posts of DevUtils on HackerNews,
posted by 5 different people, at 5 different time, all with the same link and the same title.
(Important note: these are all posted by my customers, not me creating fake accounts)
Four of those posts have less than 5 points, and only the last one posted by
“kyawzazaw” get to 178 votes and got featured on the front page, or you can say “go
viral”.
This is a classic example of what I have learned: just because your product is good,
doesnʼt mean it will get attention immediately. If your entire marketing and distribution
strategy is to post on social media and hoping to get lucky, you should really spend
more time on thinking about marketing and distribution.
So many people just focus on building the product and when it comes to marketing, they
just hope to go viral. This line of thinking is delusional and there are just so many things
wrong with this.
First, statistically, you will not go viral - don't plan on it and don't rely on it.
Secondly, even if you go viral, it doesn't mean you'll make revenue from the traffic.
Your first product will probably suck, so it's unlikely someone will buy it, it's a waste
of opportunity.
Third, even if you manage to get viral and get some revenue from it, the traffic will
only last for about a few days at most, then everything will be back to normal.
Relying on getting viral to get revenue is not sustainable, you can't just "go viral again"
anytime you want. Going viral is a valid way to reach customers, but it's the "one-time"
type of activity - you can't reliably repeat it unless you have a lot of money to put into
marketing and content.
The viral marketing that you often seen on social media rely on repeating the same type
of content / videos and every 100 videos or so, one will go viral. I have seen companies
spend more than $30K/month continuously for months across multiple different
channels to achieve that result (TikTok, Instagram, Twitter, Facebook, YouTube,…). If you
hope to just post one post and go viral, statistically itʼs not going to happen.
With any product attempts you make, you should be serious about building, marketing,
and distribution. Learn to build a product that is simple (so it won't take too long) but
valuable. Just because you build a product in 5 days doesn't mean the product has to
be a half-baked potato with an ugly design.
When it comes to marketing and distribution, don't just rely on luck. Find or build a
sustainable distribution channel that you can repeat over and over again.
Chapter 4: Marketing and
Distribution
.
Build first or find distribution channel
first?
Building the product is just step 1 of N steps
If you are a developer like me, building the product is the easy part. The hard part is how
to find customers and how to sell to them.
No matter how good your product is, no marketing means no revenue.
Building a product and finding distribution channels for it is much like two sides of a
multiplication operation: if one side is zero, the result is zero.
[Product Value] 
✖
 [Distribution] = [Success]
If the product is not useful and has no value, no matter how many people you show
it to, nobody will want it - you get nothing.
If the product is really valuable and useful, but you can't get anyone to see it, you
will also get nothing.
These are the two big bosses of any indie hacker: build and sell.
I started my indie journey knowing how to build, but with zero knowledge on how to sell.
I didn't realize that until I failed to grow DevUtils. The product was useful, functional,
with some happy customers, but I struggled to increase revenue above $50/month
because people didn't know about my product.
With the limited free time I had, it was easy for me to keep building more features for the
product because that's what I knew how to do and enjoyed doing. I was hoping people
would somehow find out about the product from existing users and come to me to buy it
- the word-of-mouth way - but that didn't happen.
Even though DevUtils was good, I needed a substantial initial user base for the word-of-
mouth effects to kick in. And even if I had had that good initial user base, I also wasn't
confident enough that my product was good enough for word-of-mouth to have any
effect. In any case, I needed that initial user base to find out. I needed to spend way
more time finding customers than building new features.
When I learned the lesson, I switched from spending 90% of my time on building to
spending 90% of my time on selling and looking for a distribution channel. During the
whole period from November 2020 to November 2021, I focused on building a
distribution channel. Along the way, I also managed to build several small products
(while still developing DevUtils), and one of them reached decent success (Black
Magic).
All of this is to say that if you find yourself continuing to build new features and writing
code while the product has little or no paying customers, you should stop and think
about how to sell. Even better if you start to think about the distribution channel before
you build it.
To clarify more on this, let's talk about when to build first or when to find a distribution
channel first.
If you have never built a complete product before, just
build first
A "complete product" here means a totally functional product that someone can open
the website, sign up, use, and pay for. Note that the "pay" part is not optional.
You may have built side projects before, but how many of them did you actually add
payment, asking the users to pay? If the answer is zero, you should try to do it at least
once in your side project.
The reason I'm suggesting "build first" in this case is because, in my experience, a
"complete product" consists of many other things other than just coding. And if you are
like me, most side projects stop when the fun coding part ends, so I wouldn't consider
them "complete".
By building first, and maybe building several of them, you actually experience all these
other steps that you would need to make your side project become a real product:
Having a website/domain/or some sort of URL that people can visit and share
Having a decent introduction to the product and the problem it's solving
Having a way for users to download/sign up and use the product
Having a way for people to pay for it and give them an invoice/receipt
You may think these are easy things that you'll figure out when there are people willing
to pay, but there's actually a lot of nuanced stuff in there, and it's better to strengthen
your "product building muscle" as early as possible.
This way, when a real opportunity arrives, you can capture it much more quickly because
you have done it all.
If you have built several complete products before: find a
distribution channel before building a new product
I would never start a new product without knowing how I will reach the potential
customers.
Unless the main goal of the new product is to have fun, learn something, or experiment
with a new tech/framework/service, then you don't have to care about distribution.
If the main goal is to generate revenue and become a real business, you should start to
think about how to sell it since day one. Even go a bit further by trying to sell the product
before you build it.
The distribution channel is what most developers will have difficulty with. Developers
already know how to code, so it's easy and fun to start building the product without
thinking too far ahead. But if you keep doing that, you're just having fun, not seriously
committed to turning it into a real business.
By thinking about how to reach customers and distribute your products, it can help
shape the way your product works from the early days and help you save time on doing
unrelated things.
For example, if I'm an admin of a Facebook group that has 200,000 members and I want
to use it as my main distribution channel, I'd add "Login with Facebook" as the main way
to login instead of spending time supporting all login methods out there.
Another example, if I'm building an invoice app for freelancers, and I plan to use paid ads
as the distribution channel, I would use paid ads to test a few ad units to figure out
which type of freelancers need the app the most (developers, designers, content
writers, etc.), then I will use that data to make my app more specialized for that
audience.
When you have a product idea, assuming you already have experienced in building
several products, you should spend time to think about how you would sell to the
customers before building it. If you canʼt figure it out, donʼt start building.
You can do both at the same time
I like to do things that have compound benefits.
When I first started indie hacking, I didn't have any distribution channel. If I wanted to
get a lot of people to look at my product, I'd probably have to spend money to buy ads,
sponsorships, or other paid marketing methods.
By building an audience around my personal branding - an indie hacker who builds and
sells products on the internet - I can both build a distribution channel and build products
at the same time. It is quite a perfect mix, I was building products and talking about
building products, or "building in public".
Most of my product attempts didn't work out, but I still have something out of it: new
lessons learned, new skills unlocked, and a new story to tell. By telling these stories and
sharing these lessons along the way, I gain more followers, which can help me with my
future product attempts.
Even if the majority of my audience is not my target customers, if only 1% of my
audience is, that would have already helped a lot to gain that initial user base, which will
later help with word-of-mouth.
In the end, I only need to succeed one time to help me reach ramen profitability and quit
the rat race. By committing to do it seriously and stay consistent, I was confident that I
could make it. The strategy works, the benefits compound, everything is aligned, I
trusted the process, it was a big part that encouraged me to quit my job when I was only
at about 8,000 followers.
Obviously my strategy is highly specific to me and not easily repeatable. Not everyone
wants to build a personal branding account on social media. But the idea remains the
same.
As an indie hacker, your resources are limited. If you can somehow build a distribution
channel and build products at the same time while doing one thing, it will help a lot.
Some examples of compounding things that I can think of (but don't have much
experience with):
Build commercial open source projects. You can both get progress on your product
and attract open source and developer communities at the same time.
Educational content. Create YouTube videos or online streams on Twitch that help
people learn to code while building from one complete product to another.
Build a community around your niche and build products for that community. It can
be a newsletter, chat groups, or Slack/Discord.
You can also do a mix of all those things.
You don't have to do it alone
If you find that doing marketing or mastering a distribution channel is not something you
want or enjoy spending your time on, remember that you can always use a bit of money
(if you can afford it) to help make things easier, or even partner with someone who has
their distribution channels nailed down.
When Tibo was building TweetHunter (sold for $2M), a tweet scheduling and content
automation platform, the biggest jump in growth was when he started to partner with
Twitter influencer JK Molina on an equity split deal.
Another example, Uku started Plausible alone and was able to reach $403 MRR but
struggled to grow further. Then he partnered with marketer Marko, and together they
brought Plausible to an $83K/month business in the next 2 years.
Indie hackers don't have to be lone wolves. In fact, a lot of indie hackers work in pairs:
one building and one marketing. The spirit is to stay independent and free. If you find
working in a pair with your co-founder is a better setting to solve the building-marketing
problem, donʼt hesitate to do it.
Personal branding as the initial
distribution channel
The best way to describe my marketing and distribution strategy is "personal branding
as a distribution channel." I'm not very good at marketing in general. I'm only good at
one single small subset of marketing: building an audience.
I'm using my personal branding as a kickstart to get my new products out to the market.
But my personal branding alone isn't a very strong distribution channel. It doesn't work
for all types of products, and doesn't work all the time. It only helps in the beginning of a
product, but sometimes that's enough.
To grow a product from its initial point, I heavily rely on product-led growth. I focus on
making a good product so that it grows naturally via word-of-mouth. I also make use of
viral elements in my products to encourage people to talk more about my products to
other people - basically using existing customers to reach new customers.
So, to put my entire marketing and distribution strategy into a few words, it would be
"Personal Branding" and "Product-led Growth."
In the past few years, I have tried a lot of methods to build more distribution channels:
SEO, sponsorships, Google Ads, LinkedIn Ads, hiring influencers, cold outreach, and
setting up a sales team. None of that worked for me.
Maybe I wasn't very serious about investing time and money into it, which is why I didn't
succeed. For example, I spent around $2,000 on LinkedIn ads in one campaign and got
zero sales. People tell me that it was too small to see any significant results. Maybe I
don't have enough skills to do it.
Anyway, after trying for a while, I decided that since I have succeeded with the building-
an-audience approach, I should just double down on it instead of looking for another
channel and thinning out my effort.
Since the early days, my main distribution channels have been my audience on Twitter
and my newsletter. I spent 2 years building an audience of 50,000 followers. At first, I
intended to build an audience to have a distribution channel for DevUtils, but then I
found it so much fun to talk to and interact with an audience that I switched to building
an audience around my personal branding instead.
Because of that, my audience is not always a 100% match with my product's ICP (Ideal
Customer Profile), but that's okay. Having someone look at your new product is still
better than having no one. So even if 1% of my audience is interested in the product that
I'm building, that's already a big win.
My primary approach to marketing and distribution is this:
Leverage my audience to gain an initial user base. This is done by sharing about the
product while I'm building it over a long period of time (sharing progress,
screenshots of unfinished features, new ideas, etc.) And when the product is ready,
I announce it to my audience and invite people to use the product. Often I can get at
least 5 to 50 users to sign up on a new product I built. If the product is really
interesting and valuable (like when I built Xnapper), I can even get hundreds of
signups.
Product-led growth: rely on this initial user base to reach more customers. This is
done by adding some "viral loop" elements to the product, or by making the
product so good that people just naturally talk about it to other people.
Building an audience is probably not the best way to do marketing. This approach is not
repeatable for everyone, it's not even repeatable for me every time I build a new
product. Not everything I build automatically succeeds just because I have a lot of
followers.
When I started building VoiceDual - an app to change your voice using AI, and another
app shortly after, SpeakDual - a real-time chat experience with AI, both products failed
to gain any significant traction despite me having 100K+ followers and doing everything
exactly the same as I was doing with TypingMind.
Still, it's fun to build new products in public and have an audience to interact with.
Having an audience helped a lot, but I don't use my audience solely as a distribution
channel. It's primarily my community and playground to share and learn with each other.
If anything, nowadays my audience helps me with my mental health, morale, and
motivation more than a distribution channel.
Product-led growth is different, I think it's the best way to grow a product.
I truly believe that if you build a really good product, no matter what marketing channels
you use, it's just a matter of time until you reach success. Time is your friend, doing
marketing will help speed up the process, but eventually you will succeed if you have
built an excellent, unique, useful, valuable product.
I have seen this play out with a lot of products. Photopea, TablePlus, and Proxyman are
some examples I can think of. All of these products are built mostly by one person out of
passion, and they actually created a really, really good product.
They don't have a master marketing plan, never spent a dollar on marketing, only
occasionally mentioning the product with friends, posting on some online forums and
communities. It reaches customers very slowly in the beginning, but every customer
who finds out about the product loves it and sticks with it for a very long time. It's a slow
organic way of growing a product, and I think it's the best type of marketing.
If you are looking to use the same strategy as mine for marketing and distribution, there
are dedicated chapters in this book talking about how to build an audience and product-
led growth. I hope it will be helpful.
Launch your product
What does “launch” mean?
In startups, "launching a product" means putting out the product you have been building
for the public to use for the first time. It's like the opening ceremony of a coffee shop
after months of preparation and construction to finally welcome customers coming in.
Traditionally, "product launch" is a big event. Startups would build a product in secret
for months and years, prepare all the marketing and sales, all building up to the launch
event, then they welcome customers to use the product for the first time. In some cases,
product launch can be a big bet on whether or not the product is going to survive in the
market, and a lot of preparation is needed to make sure the bet is worth it.
Nowadays with software products built with agile development processes, product
launches no longer have to be an all-or-nothing type of event. People split the launch
into multiple steps: private launch, soft launch, beta launch, full launch… The idea is to
let people use the product early and slowly adapt to the market as more and more
people use it, then iterate based on customer feedback and behavior.
As for indie hackers and building in public, I'm seeing an even bigger shift in how
software products are launched. I always let customers get involved in the development
process way earlier. I find the target customers and invite them to be early users as soon
as I have an MVP, then I iterate from that. I apply this method to all the products I have
built.
Way before you launch: find early users
When the product is still early in its development process, as soon as you have an MVP
version, you can add a "Beta" tag to it and give it to users to use right away.
The theory is that if your MVP is valuable and useful, people will go a long way to use it.
They will not care about the product not having a fancy design, a website, weird design
issues, or some small bugs. As long as their problem is solved, they will use the product.
By letting early users use your MVP, you can slowly build an early user base, talk to
them, ask for feedback and suggestions, and use those insights to help iterate your
product.
There are many ways to get early users.
When I was building DevUtils, I sent it to my friends and coworkers to try my app and
give me feedback. When I was working on the first version of Black Magic, I created a
private invite-only list and invited my audience on Twitter/X as early users to try it out.
Even when I was writing this book, I sent it to some friends to get their first impressions
and feedback on the drafts.
When I send the MVP to the first batch of early users, the MVP is really rough, poorly
designed, has a complicated onboarding process, and the UI is full of typos — almost to
the point of embarrassment. The only thing that the MVP does well is solving the
problem it is supposed to solve (the product core value); all other things are secondary.
I only invite a handful of early users, so even though I'm embarrassed, it's not in front of
thousands of people. As I invite more and more early users, the MVP slowly gets better, I
get more time to fix the bugs, cover the edge cases, and smooth out the experience. Up
to the point of public launch, the product has become highly polished and something I'm
proud of.
You'll have to find a way to reach these early users, even before you write your first line
of code.
This way, you can have their early reaction to the product and the problems they are
trying to solve with your product, then you can iterate on their feedback. You'll need to
talk to these users closely, make them happy, and make sure they love the product and
use it regularly. Ask them if they would pay for the product and how much.
The more early users you have, the better. Ideally, you should invite early users to use
your product as soon as the MVP version is ready. By letting early users use the MVP,
you force yourself to focus on the one thing that truly matters: your product core value.
For example, if I'm building a Meeting Summarizer app and I want to give it to my users
early, the first thing I need to work on should be the meeting recording and summarizing
feature. Not the signup/sign-in, not the landing page, not the onboarding flow, not the
fancy UX/UI design, not the test cases, not the scalable web architecture framework,
etc.
If your product solves a real painful problem, no matter how ugly the product is, people
will use it, especially when there are no other alternatives that they know of.
Let your early users pay at a discount
There is a very clear distinction between "users" and "customers": customers pay to
use the app. If possible, always try to convert your early users into "early customers".
I have seen too many people who are too scared of monetizing their product and instead
only focus on the number of users or active users with the excuse: "if there are enough
users, we will find a way to monetize later." That may be true for VC-funded startups
with millions of consumer users, but as an indie hacker, you may not have enough
resources to survive until that point. The app may not even be able to reach that point in
the first place.
That's why I advocate for building products that directly provide value and charge the
user as soon as possible. There is no stronger signal than paying customers and
revenue.
When you recruit early users, always make it clear that the product is going to be a paid
product, or it will have paid tiers. Ideally, you should only talk to users who are willing to
pay for the product. You'll be surprised how many people become very enthusiastic
about the product and how you should build it, but when you ask them if they want to
pay, they'll decline. So this is some early validation signal for you. Free users have a lot
of opinions, ideas, and suggestions, but if they are not paying, then they are not your
target customers.
The easiest way to encourage your early users to pay is to give them an early bird
discount. I always give at least a 50% discount for whoever is willing to use my MVP
version, which usually has only one feature but is full of bugs and has an ugly design.
When the MVP version of TypingMind was released, the early users paid only $9 to get
the app for lifetime with all features unlocked; the price for the equivalent plan is now
$99. Same thing for Black Magic - the Pro plan now costs $39.99/month, but some of
my customers are still enjoying all the benefits paying $4/month because they signed up
to be early users since the beginning. Early users of Xnapper only paid $14; the full price
after beta was $79.99.
It's a token of appreciation from your side to say thank you to the customers for being
supportive in the early days and giving you their feedback. It's also a signal for you to
know if your product is really valuable enough for them to put down their credit card.
When, where, and how to launch
In my opinion, "launching" is purely just a marketing activity. You should have your
product ready and used by at least hundreds of users before launching the product. If
you don't already have some early users who use your product, don't even bother
launching it and waste a marketing opportunity.
Find users first, launch later.
Once you have early users, you should build the product with them for a while, fix most
of the annoying bugs, smooth out the onboarding flow, create a simple marketing
landing page, etc.
When you launch, you'll already have an initial user base. You can then ask them to help
with your product launch by supporting you with upvotes, comments, reviews, and
testimonials.
The magic of "launching" is that you can make it a special day, a special event, so that
you can ask people to support you (your early users, friends, etc.). You don't want to do
it too often; otherwise, you're wasting your users' and friends' goodwill when they go
out of their way to support you on launch day.
Usually, I would launch when:
I have 10 or more early users who really use the product and get value out of it.
The product core value is stable, meaning no major crashes/bugs.
I have a simple and direct landing page to explain the product to new users.
I always want to make my launch day special, as that way it feels memorable and
significant.
After I pick a launch date and time, I send out a "notification" to my early users and
audience, letting them know that the product they are using is going to be officially
launched for public users, and I would really appreciate if they show up and share their
experiences with reviews and testimonials.
Sometimes, I message people one by one and ask if they would like me to ping them
when the launch is live.
Be careful not to become a spammer. If you make your launch day special, all the
websites and forums will welcome your post, as some websites are specially designed
to launch new products like Product Hunt. But if you launch new products every week,
every month, or multiple times a month, you will become a spammer and will get
banned/blocked from these sites.
Then, you should pick a main launch site. I've always launched on Product Hunt, but
other people have used Twitter or Reddit, or their own website.
Once you pick a main launch site, you should point all other posts from other sites to
that one.
The idea here is that you pick a site that has decent traffic and network effect. For
example, Product Hunt has millions of visitors a month, so if you push all your effort into
the Product Hunt launch and the launch gets traction, you may get featured on its home
page, which helps gain even more traction - it's a compounding effect.
This applies the same for other places with network effects like Twitter, Reddit, or
maybe Facebook. This is also the reason you don't want to use your own
website/landing page as the main launch site, as you will miss out on the compounding
effect.
So for example, I picked Product Hunt to be my main launch site. On the launch day, I
would add a badge showing that I'm launching on Product Hunt and actually put a link to
Product Hunt from my website!
Many marketers will tell you that it's stupid to put an external link at the front of your
website that drives your traffic away, increasing your bounce rate and hurting
conversions, but in this case, it has a purpose. If your post on Product Hunt gets enough
votes and gets featured, it can easily 10× or 100× the number of views and visits to your
website.
What I would do on launch day:
Start the launch on the main launch site.
Make sure the launch discount is working.
Update the website to show the announcement. I usually have a sticky banner on
top to announce the launch and link to the main launch site.
Post the announcement on other places: Twitter/Reddit, Indie Hackers, if relevant
also post on HackerNews.
Announce the launch to the early users and my newsletter subscribers via email.
Watch for users' comments, reviews, and reply to them.
If you are lucky and get attention: watch for product metrics, revenue, monitor the
system, visitors' activity, check crash logs, errors, etc.
This will go on for about 24 hours, and usually this is the most exciting 24 hours that I
experience whenever I build a new product.
14 ways to find customers without
using money
Why not use money?
Acquiring customers using money is a completely different game.
With money, you can buy paid ads, sponsorships, hire marketing/sales people, you can
buy an existing Instagram page with 10,000 followers and do your marketing on it, you
can even pay for people to use your product. It's a game of input vs. output - the cost of
acquiring customers. I'm not saying it's easy, just different.
Just to clarify, "using money" means continuously spending a significant amount of
money, probably $1,000 or more, to continuously reach new customers. If the revenue
from those customers is more than your spending amount, you win.
In my opinion, in order for this to work, you will most likely have to spend $1,000 to
$10,000 (or even more) to start seeing some results. When I first started out, I didn't
want to spend that much money. It seemed like a big risk I didn't want to take because I
didn't know anything about running paid ads or doing paid marketing.
I actually tried paid ads on Google and Twitter, but the amount was small, from $200–
$400. Later, when I had some decent revenue, I tried again with a $2,000 campaign - all
resulted in zero sales. I learned that I didn't succeed with paid ads partly because I
wasn't serious enough with my attempts, and people have also told me that a $2,000
campaign is essentially "nothing" - it's too small to see any results.
Using paid marketing is a valid way to reach customers, but it's just not my thing. I'm
bad at it and I don't have much experience with it, so I won't discuss much about it in
this book.
I think what's more relevant for indie hackers is to discuss ways you can reach
customers using your time and effort - the grinding way. I've had success with a few
methods that I listed here, and I hope they will work for you too.
Talk about your product offline
The easiest way to reach potential customers is to talk about it to people around you.
Talk about your product with everyone you know: friends, coworkers, classmates, family,
your neighbors, etc.
You can attend local events, meetups, or virtual gatherings and talk about it there.
Ask them probing questions about the problem you are solving with your product, then
ask them to try the beta version for free in exchange for their feedback.
You are the biggest advocate for your own product - if you don't do this, who will?
When I first started building DevUtils (a macOS app for developers), I let my friends use
it first. Whenever I go to an Apple store, I open the store laptops, download my app to it,
and open it to the main screen. Partly to test it, but also I want other people to see it.
At some point, I even printed some T-shirts with the app logo and gave them out for free
to friends.
List your product online
Some websites allow you to post your product when you first launch it. This is a one-off
opportunity to get your product seen.
Here are some websites that I frequently use the most:
Hacker News (Show HN) -
https://news.ycombinator.com/
This is a developer-focused website, there is a section called "Show HN" where people
can show off what they have built. If you are building a developer product, this is a good
place to post.
Posts on Hacker News are ranked by votes; if your product is good (or you get lucky),
your post might be upvoted by other users and will be ranked at the top for other people
to see.
My first product (DevUtils) got its first revenue from Hacker News, when I 
posted it
in 2020. I was lucky enough to get some attention (240+ votes) and the post stayed on
the home page for a few hours, bringing me a few hundred dollars in revenue!
Product Hunt -
http://producthunt.com/
Product Hunt is a favorite place to launch new products for indie hackers. This website
collects cool products on the internet every day and features them on their homepage.
The products are also ranked by votes, and each product has 24 hours to "compete" to
rank higher on the home page.
Every day at 12:01 AM PST, the list of products gets refreshed and new products show
up.
If you are able to get your product to the top 10, it will be featured on the home page and
the newsletter; if in the top 5, it will be featured in the weekly/monthly newsletter (these
rules may have changed).
Product Hunt is where I launch all of my products — it's fun and the community there is
supportive of indie hackers.
Indie Hackers -
https://www.indiehackers.com/
I've mentioned Indie Hackers a few times; it's a forum, a podcast, but also a place to
post your product.
Indie Hackers has a profile page where you can post the products you have created,
create a timeline, and post updates for the product as you build. Many people use it to
share their progress, revenue, and learnings when building their products. It's the place
where I learned about indie hacking and got inspired by many other indie hackers.
[59]
You can post your product there, or post your progress and learnings as you are building
it. Keep it interesting, informative, and tell your stories.
Other places
Above are just some of my favorite places to post my products. There are a lot more
sites like Beta List or the r/sideproject subreddit; I don't have much experience on those
sites so I can't share much here, but you can search Google for "places to post your
startup online" — there are a lot of sites like these.
Build an audience or a community
Become an influencer yourself. The idea is simple: the more people follow you on
YouTube, Twitter, Facebook, Instagram, the more opportunities you have to get your
product seen.
The internet has allowed any ordinary person to have unlimited reach potential to any
kind of audience, even bigger than TV or newspapers. The only thing you need is the
internet, a phone, a laptop, and your skills.
You can build an audience anywhere:
Social media: Twitter, YouTube, Facebook, Instagram, etc.
Create your own newsletter where you send emails directly to your audience
Create a community on Reddit, or a forum, or even a group chat.
In the first two years of my indie hacking journey, I decided that becoming an influencer
was the way I would reach my customers, so I spent a lot of time growing my Twitter
account from 0 to 100k followers and my newsletter from 0 to 12k subscribers.
It has become the most impactful thing I have ever done, and all of my future products
used my audience as the kick-start distribution channel. It's my best unfair advantage.
Of course, not everyone can or wants to become an influencer, so you'll have to see if
this fits you.
Cold outreach via email, phone, social media
If you have a list of contacts that might be interested in your product, you can send your
product to other people via email, phone, SMS, or direct message on social media, etc.
No one likes getting spammed, but if you write your message well, it may actually
become helpful for the receiver.
I don't like this method of reaching customers because I feel like I'm spamming people;
however, there are countless stories of people getting a good start for their product just
by emailing people.
You can send 10 emails per day, so after 10 days you have reached 100 people. If one of
them is interested in your product, you have yourself a 1% conversion rate — it's actually
not bad.
You can increase your conversion rate by curating your email list better to find more
relevant people (better leads), and writing your message better to sound more
convincing. Higher conversion rates mean more customers and fewer people get
spammed.
If you can spend just a little bit of money, you can actually gather a great list of potential
customer contacts from online lead generating services. Just search "generate leads for
cold outreach" on Google and pick the best one for you.
Life-time deals (LTD)
A lifetime deal means you offer a discounted, one-time purchase price for customers
instead of monthly/yearly subscriptions. These are usually offered during special events
like when the product is first launched.
It's a way to validate your product
This is a good test to see if people want your product or not and get some quick
revenue. I've seen founders who run LTD during launch, and then when they see that
people really want the product, they change the pricing to subscription (of course,
whoever bought the LTD price will enjoy lifetime access).
In the long term, the subscription customers will generate enough recurring revenue to
cover all the LTD customers. It's a good way to kickstart a product, and Testimonial by
Damon Chen is a great example of this.
However, LTD is also a dangerous double-edged sword.
If your product requires high recurring maintenance costs (server, database, customer
support, etc.), offering a lifetime deal is not sustainable for the business.
I've seen people offering LTDs to grab quick cash, run it for a while, then realize they
can't keep doing that for long, shut down the business and create new ones. It's almost
fraud.
Running an LTD once is fine, but too much will kill the product. Unless your product
doesn't have a lot of recurring costs - for example, a downloadable software that
doesn't have a server or database running 24/7 - you can totally offer lifetime deal
pricing forever.
LTD is easier to market
It is a really good deal, so it's easier to get people to buy it. There are dedicated groups
on Facebook/Reddit that curate LTDs from across the internet. Some big websites are
famous for offering LTDs like 
AppSumo.com
.
If you plan to do an LTD, it's a good idea to reach out to these groups and websites to
partner with them to help promote the LTD. They will take a cut of the revenue for sure,
but at least it will guarantee some good traffic to the product.
Search Engine Optimization (SEO)
By optimizing based on what people search, you can get your product website ranked
higher on Google and get more traffic. I applied SEO to almost all of my products.
For DevUtils, I was writing blog posts. DevUtils is a developer utilities app, so I targeted
keywords like "convert unixtime," "format json online," or "decode base64" and wrote
articles about those topics, hoping to rank high on Google. I always try my best to make
the articles actually helpful for readers, then advertise my own product at the end of
each post.
For BlackMagic, I applied programmatic SEO. BlackMagic is a Twitter analytics page - I
scrape Twitter accounts and their public tweets and metrics. I then create insights,
analytics, and summaries on these profiles and create a page for each of them. The
pages created by BlackMagic contain useful information (I named the feature "Glance"):
what the person tweets the most about (keywords), what time of day they are active,
who they're tweeting/commenting on the most, etc. This way, when you search for
someone's Twitter handle on Google, other than their actual Twitter page, they will also
see my analytics page below (usually the 2nd to 5th link down the page).
For TypingMind, it was simply just blog posts about AI.
Overall, my efforts resulted in some traffic to my websites, some of which converted to
revenue, but not very significantly. My SEO skills are still weak, and I should probably
spend more time and effort on this.
SEO is a long-term game - you will not see the results until 6–12 months later. However,
the results are almost always wonderful when they work. SEO is one of the few methods
that actually gives you passive traffic. Some products survive for decades purely on SEO
traffic.
SEO is a whole book/course on its own, so I won't go into too many details here. If you
want to learn more about SEO, I'm not the expert you should listen to, but there are
plenty of resources that are easy to find online. Some famous indie hackers who have
had success and are experts at SEO, like Danny Postma, have created SEO courses that
you can buy and learn from.
Use marketplaces, App Store Optimization
App stores and marketplaces are good places to get traffic to your product.
Marketplaces let you list your app, and they help with "marketing" by showing your app
to their user base via search results, recommendations, or featured sections.
The downside is that these marketplaces usually take a cut of your revenue and pay you
the rest, so you'll get paid via the marketplace instead of charging your customers
directly.
If you are building an iOS or Android app, you will need to list them on Apple's App Store
or Google's Play Store. You should look into "ASO" — App Store Optimization, similar to
SEO but for app stores. There are many things you can do to improve your presence on
the app store, which will help increase traffic and downloads.
For web apps, there are also many options to leverage marketplaces.
Setapp app marketplace (
Setapp.com
): specialized for macOS apps. I've
successfully listed two of my apps on Setapp; it brought good traffic and recurring
revenue.
Some payment processors also offer marketplace features like Gumroad and Lemon
Squeezy - these can also be good opportunities.
Marketplaces for B2B software: AWS Marketplace, GCP Marketplace
AppSumo is a marketplace specialized in discounted and lifetime deal products.
If you are building add-ons, browser extensions, or plugins for a specific platform,
getting listed and relying on the distribution of the platform's marketplace is also a good
way to reach customers.
Content marketing
Write content by sharing your knowledge and posting it online on your website and other
places. A few types of content that you can create:
Blog posts
Tutorials
Newsletter
Analytics/insights
Ebook
etc.
You can post this content on other platforms or directly on your website. If you are good
at writing, you should get a lot of attention, and then get traffic to your product through
some mentions of your product.
For me, my content consists of my tweets (posted on Twitter), a newsletter at
news.tonydinh.com
 (posted on Substack), and this book (posted directly on my
website).
The most popular example I know that leverages content marketing as an indie hacker is
the founders of 
plausible.io
 (
https://plausible.io/blog/open-source-saas
).
The founders focused their marketing efforts on writing blog posts, and they did a really
good job at it. Some of their blog posts frequently get a lot of attention on HackerNews,
which has contributed a lot to their success.
Product-led growth
Use your users to reach more users.
In its simplest form, product-led growth just means that you create a really good
product, then people will naturally talk about your product with other people. Itʼs the
organic word-of-mouth way to grow.
Other than the organic way, you can also create “artificial” ways to make people talk
more about your product to other people.
You can intentionally embed some social/viral elements into your product so that when 1
person uses the product, it's easy for other people to also want to use it, creating
network effect.
For example, if you use Calendly to schedule your meeting, when you send your meeting
booking link to other people, they will see and know about Calendly, and this keeps
going. People sometimes call this a "viral loop".
Every product can have some sort of viral loop elements in it. Some very strong, some
weak. The simplest way is a referral program, but in my opinion, referral is a weak type
of viral loop.
A strong viral loop is something that people naturally do when they simply use your
product. Like in the Calendly example, when I send out my booking link, I'm not actively
looking to get the other person to know about Calendly, I was just doing it for my own
needs.
Product-led is my favorite way to grow a product. I've always tried to add some sort of
viral loop elements to all of my products.
In BlackMagic, back in the day with old Twitter, when a tweet was scheduled with
BlackMagic, there was always a small label at the end of the tweet saying "Posted via
BlackMagic" (the message was added by Twitter for all scheduler apps, and it couldn't
be turned off), so it was free marketing.
I also intentionally make a lot of beautiful visuals and charts to visualize the Twitter
account data - things that they have never seen anywhere else - which encourages
them to take screenshots and post on their Twitter. This triggers other people to ask
"Where did you get these types of data from?" "How can I see this data for my Twitter?"
- and the poster often mentions BlackMagic, also free marketing.
I used this in my other product Xnapper too; it's a screenshot app that makes your
screenshots instantly beautiful to share on social media. When someone uses it to
capture their screenshot and post on social media, people will get surprised by how
beautiful the screenshot is, and then they'll see the Xnapper's watermark or the poster
will mention the product name to other people. This is somewhat similar to word of
mouth.
In fact, looking back, all of my products rely heavily on product-led growth, as I don't
have many other marketing channels.
To achieve product-led growth, you will need:
An initial mass of user base, so that at least there are people to start the loop.
A strong viral loop element in your product.
In my specific case, I get the initial mass of users by relying on my audience on Twitter
and newsletter, then I use viral loop elements to reach more users from the initial user
base. In some cases, for example TypingMind, the product itself is just good enough
that it will naturally get recommended and referenced by other people all the time.
Community contributions
Do you have a popular GitHub repo? It could be a good channel to reach your
customers.
You can contribute to open source projects, get popular on GitHub, then put your
product website under your profile.
I learned this from the founder of another indie product Proxyman. Proxyman is a
developer app to help debug network traffic. In the early days, the founder spent a lot of
time on Stack Overflow helping answer questions about networking and proxy, which
helped other people but also created awareness for his own product.
I added DevUtils to my profile on Stack Overflow too. I have about 6.5k reputation points
on Stack Overflow, which builds some traffic to the DevUtils website, not to mention the
backlink for SEO is also a great benefit.
Side-project marketing
Build multiple small-sized projects that serve as "satellites" for your primary product.
It could be a small web tool or an open-source project. The goal is to get attention and
go viral, but if the side project turns out to be very valuable, it can turn into a project on
its own.
This is also my favorite way to do marketing. As a developer, I can build new side
projects fast, and I enjoy doing it as it helps me reduce stress from working on one thing
for too long.
When I was building BlackMagic, I created a lot of small tools around Twitter: profile
picture tool, a profile banner tool, the Glance feature that crawls public stats about a
Twitter account, a tool to visualize your followers/following ratio, etc. Some of these
small tools really helped getting more people to use the primary product. It also helped
with SEO too.
When building a side project for marketing, it's good to stick to the most trendy/popular
things at the time to leverage the hype, and it's best to offer these as free tools to
maximize the number of people who can use them.
Getting featured
You can reach out to bloggers, journalists, podcasts, and newsletters asking for a free
mention in exchange for a free license of your product.
This method is somewhat "testing your luck" - there is no guarantee at all it will work. It
depends on how good your product is and how genuinely you approach the
bloggers/authors. Sometimes people will just do it for free without you asking because
the product is good and they just want to share it with other people.
In the early days of DevUtils, I reached out to the author of the iOS Dev Weekly
newsletter expressing my wish to get DevUtils featured, and I was lucky enough to get
featured in issue number 487 - it brought in a lot of sales! The author even bought a
license for himself to support me.
If you use this method, definitely don't spam your product to every blogger you know.
Make sure to choose publications that are highly relevant to your product and do it in a
way that doesn't look like spam. In my case, DevUtils was a developer app and a native
macOS app, which a lot of iOS developers (and macOS developers) really love. It was
also quite unique in the way it was built, so I got the support from the author.
Use review sites
If you already have some users, asking them to leave reviews on review sites like
TrustPilot, G2, Capterra, or on marketplaces like App Store, Google Play Store, etc.,
could be a good way to reach more potential customers and is also good for SEO and
branding.
Of course, you will have to make sure your product is actually good and people love
using it. Receiving negative reviews is the opposite of what we are trying to do.
Don't buy fake reviews - it does more harm than good. Also, don't "bribe" your users to
leave good reviews (e.g., "please give a good review to enjoy a free license"). Almost all
review sites will ban you for buying fake reviews or biased reviews (reviews from you,
your team, or from users you bribe).
To get good reviews, other than making a truly useful product, you should also make
sure to ask "the right way".
My favorite moment to ask for a review is after the user tries out the beta version. During
the beta period, I would talk to the users a lot to understand their problems and learn
how I can make the product better. Over time, these users become highly invested in the
product, and when I see the moment is right, I'll ask them to leave a review. This is all
done manually via direct message. I typically talk to about 10-50 users during the first
few months of the product.
I also ask people to leave a review after a successful customer support ticket, which is
also a perfect moment.
In the case of BlackMagic, it was a browser extension, so I asked people to help me
leave a review on the Chrome Extension store - many were happy to leave very good
reviews.
I later use these reviews as testimonials on my marketing website, which is perfect.
Affiliate and referral
If you already have some initial users, you can ask them to help you reach more users by
sharing revenue with them via some sort of affiliate or referral program.
The most straightforward way to do this is to implement some tracking cookie with a
special referral code for each user. When another user makes a purchase, you will know
who referred them to the app, and pay a percentage of the revenue to the referrer.
I tried affiliate/referral several times but have never had success with it. However, other
indie hackers seem to do it very well, so it may work for you.
Go open-source
Open source your product on GitHub.
This doesn't exactly qualify as a way to reach customers, but in some cases it can help
get people to use your product and, if you get lucky, can gain a lot of attention from the
developer community.
These days, GitHub is almost like social media - you can go viral/trending on it too. And
when you get into the trending list, you will get featured on countless other websites,
newsletters, RSS feeds that collect trending GitHub repositories.
You don't have to open source your entire product, maybe just some modules, or a
smaller-scoped side-project that relates to your product.
And open source doesn't mean you can't make money. Even if you open source the
entire product, you can still make money from Enterprise licenses, a cloud-hosted
offering, and donations.
This means the core application is open source, but you also offer a cloud-hosted
version hosted by you, charging a subscription to your customers. And for enterprises
who want to host the product on their infrastructure, you can charge them on a separate
license.
Many popular products were created this way, just to name a few: Sentry, 
Cal.com
,
Plausible, Supabase, etc.
Chapter 5: Growing Your Business
.
Incorporating a company
You may not need a company
You do not always need to register a company. There are a lot of things you can do using
your personal identity instead of a company. It makes everything much easier to get
started.
Focus on building a product, find customers, and reach profitability first. Legal stuff and
processes are straightforward, and you can always figure them out when needed. If you
donʼt have a revenue generating product but already thinking about incorporation, taxes,
and legal processes, you are focusing on the wrong thing.
In its simplest form, in most countries, there is a "sole proprietorship" legal entity type,
which basically means you are running a business under your personal identity. You are
the business and the business is you, no separation.
Almost all payment processors (Stripe, Paddle) and marketplaces (Apple's App Store,
Google's Play Store) support Sole Proprietorship as a legal entity, and you can receive
payments this way.
You can operate in this mode for as long as you think your product has traction and
customers.
Benefits of registering a company
The main benefit for me when I registered my company was to access Stripe. I live in
Vietnam, and Stripe doesn't support Vietnamese businesses, so if I want to use Stripe, I
have to incorporate my business in a country where Stripe is available.
To be clear, I didn't need to use Stripe. I was using Paddle (
paddle.com
) for a long time
with my personal identity. It worked great - Paddle supports a wider range of countries
including Vietnam, and it was my main payment processor when I was building DevUtils
and Black Magic.
However, I didn't want to be restricted to only one option of payment processing, plus I
wanted to try and enjoy other features that Stripe offers. It was the main motivation for
me to incorporate a company.
Incorporating a company gives you a company name, address, and a business bank
account. It helps a bit to appear more "reputable" with customers when they see their
receipt/invoice or make a wire transfer (they now see a business name instead of just
"some guy").
To be honest, it didn't help as much as I thought it would. I rarely have any business
customers that need to do wire transfers - most transactions are done via Stripe/Paddle
with credit cards - but it feels good to finally have a real business name.
And then on top of that, depending on your situation, you can have more benefits
around taxes, deducting your expenses, separating business assets from your personal
assets, and applying for grants and tax benefits as a startup. This is highly specific to
each person and their country of residence, so I can't be much help here.
With benefits, it also comes with some drawbacks. Since you are now running a
company, you'll need to do all the required paperwork needed to maintain the company
and stay compliant. For me, I used onlinetaxman.com, an online service to file
paperworks for me every year.
When and where to incorporate
Since it costs time and money to incorporate a company, my suggestion is to only do it
when necessary. Again, focus on making a product work first. There is no point in having
a company without a revenue-generating product (unless required by law, I know some
countries don't allow you to conduct business under personal identity, which is weird).
For me, I registered a company when my revenue was around $1,000/month. I actually
did not really need to register a company, but I did it just to learn what it was like.
You can incorporate a company in your home country. But if you're like me, living in a
country where Stripe is not available, or for any other reasons, you can register a
company in a different country as a foreigner.
In my experience, I know you can open a company in Singapore, the US, Estonia,
Canada, and some other countries as a foreigner.
I opened a company in Singapore once, but then I closed it shortly after because the
fees were quite high and I couldn't afford it after one year - it was about USD
$4,000/year in total just to maintain the operation.
I later learned about incorporating a company in the US and created Devuap LLC, which
is the current company I'm using for all of my products. It is cheaper to open in the US,
while enjoying almost the same benefits. At the time I incorporated, it costs about $500
to $1,000 per year to maintain operation and paperworks.
Incorporating in the US
A small note: Iʼm writing this part as a non-US citizen. If you live in the US, you
probably know this stuff better than me. This part is for people outside of the
US who want to incorporate a US company as a foreigner.
To incorporate a company in the US, you can use one of these services:
Stripe Atlas
: this is the well-known way to do it.
FirstBase.io
: this is what I used to incorporate my LLC in 2022
There are probably more services that help you do it. Just search for "Incorporate a
company in the US as a foreigner" on Google.
[60]
When incorporating, you will need to decide if your company is an LLC or a Corp. If you
are a solo founder and don't have an immediate plan to raise funds, LLC is the choice. It
is much cheaper and simpler to maintain in terms of legal requirements and taxes.
You can choose to incorporate your company in many US states. When I did mine, I
chose Wyoming state as the home of the company because it was cheaper (I was broke
back then). But if I were to do it again, I would pick Delaware because even though it's a
bit more expensive, Delaware is a reputable home for a lot of tech companies in the US.
When you use one of the services above to incorporate your company, they'll take care
of all the paperwork for you. In my experience, the overall process takes about 1–4
weeks.
After incorporating, the service you use will probably help you with opening a bank
account. You can use the bank account to receive payments and pay for business
expenses.
There are two popular banks used by startups in the US that I'm familiar with:
Mercury
 and 
Brex
. These are "internet banks" backed by traditional banks. They
allow you to manage your account remotely without having to be physically in the US.
Small tip: when receiving payments from customers or payment providers from within
the US, ask the sender to send the payments via ACH. This is a US-specific fund
transfer network, and it will be processed faster and cheaper.
And of course, you can send payments via traditional wire transfer to any bank in any
country.
One company for multiple products
As an indie hacker, you probably have multiple products. You don't need to register a
new company for each product — it's a waste of time and money.
It's a common practice that one company can have multiple products with multiple
sources of revenue and shared expenses. I use Devuap LLC for all of my products and
[61]
[62]
put the company info into all the product websites that I created.
You only need to create a separate company if, let's say, you have another co-founder
and you want to separate all the revenue and expenses from your other products.
If you have multiple products and one of your products blows up big, you can always
create another company at that time to separate everything if needed.
If you plan to sell one of your products (acquisition), you can do that by doing an "Asset
