Purchase Agreement" instead of a "company acquisition." This means you only sell the
company's product as an asset, not the whole company.
Offload boring admin work
Setting up a company, filing paperwork, doing accounting, taxes, etc. are a lot of work,
as a company of one, itʼs better to offload as much of these admin works to online
services and professionals as possible.
What I did to learn and do all of this much faster was to talk to professionals, lawyers,
accountants, etc. There are services online that you can book to talk to them and ask for
advice or ask them to do things for you. Yes, it costs money, but it is worth it.
For tax and paperwork filing, I have been using 
onlinetaxman.com
 for a few years, they
provide fast and quality service with reasonable prices at $500 to $1,000 per filing (I
have no affiliation with them!)
The service that I used for incorporating (
WyomingLLCattorney.com
) also provide a
wide range of services including banking, accounting, legal services, etc.. I use this
service because my company is founded in Wyoming, but you can search for similar
ones wherever your company is founded.
In general, I think most people should pay to talk to professionals more often as soon as
they can afford it. A lot of things are much easier if you are given some general
directions instead of learning everything on your own.
Being an indie hacker, you have to leverage this even more so you have more time to
work on your product - the thing that really matter.
Getting SOC2 certified
Letʼs talk about SOC2 and why you may (or may not) need it.
What is SOC 2?
In simple terms, SOC 2 is a certificate that shows that your company has good policies
and processes regarding data security and privacy.
SOC 2 certificates are issued by accredited auditors. You would hire one of these
auditors to examine your company's policies, processes, infrastructure, and setup to
determine whether your company follows best industry practices regarding security.
Previously, I imagined SOC 2 would be something so big and "enterprisey" - completely
out of reach for indie hackers because it was either very expensive or very complicated.
However, after a lot of research and actually going through getting my SOC 2
certification, my view on SOC 2 completely changed. Let's get into the details.
Why I want SOC 2
Companies often ask if you have this certificate before making a purchase, and if you
don't, they'll ask you to fill in a list of 50+ security questionnaires.
I've been through this security questionnaire process a few times with my previous
products, 
DevUtils.com
 and Black Magic. It's boring.
When I started offering the 
Enterprise version of TypingMind
, these questionnaires
started to pop up much more frequently.
[63]
Plus, since I was getting a lot more enterprise customers for TypingMind, they care a lot
more about SOC 2 and security in general, so getting SOC 2 certified would help me
increase trust and close more deals, beside helping up level my security standards and
processes for the whole product.
Thatʼs why I decided to look into how to get SOC 2, what how much does it cost and
how much time is needed to get one.
The cost
I thought it would cost me something like $30K/year to get certified and was mentally
prepared for this cost, but in the end, it only cost me 
less than $20K
.
Half of the cost is paid to a consulting service so they can help me prepare all the
documents and guide me on how to get certified. The other half is paid to the auditor as
a fee.
Other than the cost of money, there is also time. It took me, in total, about 80 hours of
work to get everything in place for compliance.
At the time, the team has 5 people, with only 2 full-time employees, so a lot of the steps
are pretty easy. For a team with more people, it will probably cost more time and money.
The process
I started looking into getting SOC 2 by asking on Twitter and from founder friends who
have done it before.
Thanks to them, I've learned that getting SOC 2 certified used to be a complicated and
time-consuming process with a lot of back and forth, but now with consulting and
automation services, it has become very manageable.
One can get certified even with a team of only 2 people and less than $20K.
The whole process can be summarized into the following steps:
1. 
Learn about the SOC 2 requirements
2. 
Implement policies and changes in your company's system to satisfy the SOC 2
requirements. Things like "your database must have a backup," "your employees'
computers must have encryption enabled," etc. (they have a lot of requirements!)
3. 
Prepare documents to prove that your company has implemented the changes and
followed the policies.
4. 
Involve an auditor and present the documents to the auditor.
5. 
Once the auditor has reviewed and approved the documents, you'll get the SOC 2
Type I certificate.
6. 
Continue to monitor your system and policies to make sure your company satisfies
all of the requirements for a long period, and make sure that at no point in time do
you fail any requirement. After 3 or 6 months, you'll get SOC 2 Type II certified.
Here are the costs:
Your time
: you'll need to set up the system and policies. Depending on the current
state of your team and infrastructure, this can take months, weeks, or days.
Fee
: you'll need to pay for the auditor every time they are involved. If you just need
to get Type I, you'll pay them once. If you need Type II and continuously keep that
Type II certified status, you'll need to pay them every time they perform an audit, so
this will be a recurring fee.
Using a consulting and automation service
I imagined getting SOC 2 would involve spending a lot of time going back and forth with
the auditor, having to write a lot of documents, and implementing so many changes to
my current infra and process.
But no, it's not that bad.
These days, people use consulting and automation services to get SOC 2 certified.
I was recommended two services that would help me understand and get SOC 2
certified. They are:
Vanta
: a company from the US, quite expensive but reputable.
Sprinto
: a company from India, cheaper, but less reputable as they are a new player
in the market.
Iʼm sure there are more alternatives that are less popular.
Once you sign up for their service, they will help you with:
Understand your current situation and figure out what is needed to get SOC 2
certified via video calls (gap analysis).
They have a system to connect to your services and automatically detect if
something does not satisfy the SOC 2 requirements. For example, you can connect
your AWS/GitHub account to their system, then they'll notify you if your database
doesn't have a backup or your main branch isn't protected. They'll provide a nice
dashboard with a detailed report of every config and task you should do to satisfy
SOC 2 requirements.
They help you collect the data and create all the documents, policies, and prepare
the final documents in a nice way that will be presented to the auditors.
They will also connect you to auditors who will perform the audit (introduce via
email).
They provide a separate dashboard for the auditor so that it takes less time for the
auditor to go through the documents and the evidence.
Basically, they help a lot, with a reasonable price. My total cost for the consulting service
and the auditor was less than $10K.
How it's actually done
So I connected with one of the two services above. We got on a call to understand my
current team and infra setup.
The actual requirements of SOC 2 are quite boring. I'm sure if you are a decent
developer with a decent workflow, you've already satisfied most of them. Things like:
You must use a source control system like Git.
Don't commit directly to "main" but use a branch/PR.
Have a database backup and verify that the backup is actually working.
Verify and don't install shady npm packages.
Assess the risk of all the vendors you are using (like AWS, Vercel, Mailgun, Slack…)
Have access control to critical resources (i.e., don't give root access to your
employee if not necessary)
…
Some of the requirements are a bit too complicated for a usual small team, but
introducing them is not a big deal, and I was totally cool with it. For example:
All your employees must go through security & privacy training every 6 or 12
months. It's like an online course.
Must conduct a background check for every new employee.
Conduct a pen-testing for your system using a trusted pen testing service (may
cost somewhere $10,000 to $50,000 each time)
…
I didn't have much difficulty setting up my infra and processes to meet all the
requirements of SOC 2. It took me around 10 days on and off to get everything checked.
Every time I hit a roadblock, I contacted the consulting service, and they would help me
via email or video call. Very helpful.
In total, it took me about 2 months since I started contacting the consulting service to
the day I got my SOC 2 Type I certificate, then another 6 months to get Type II.
Security audit and pen-testing
One of the requirements to upgrade from Type I to Type II is to have a security audit and
a pen-testing report. This means I had to hire a security auditor to go through the
system and perform a penetration test.
In my case, the consulting service didn't have any recommendations on which security
auditor I should go with, so I scouted the internet and asked friends for introductions to
security audit firms.
There are quite a few firms that provide pen-testing services. Most of them are very
expensive — a popular one is HackerOne. You can search for "pen-testing service" on
Google.
After scouting for a while, I decided to go with this firm that provides pen-testing with a
reasonable price for startups. They do penetration testing for a lot of YC companies.
I can't disclose their name and their cost at the same time, so I choose to share their
cost with you here: it cost me $10,000 for one round of testing, which was a discounted
price because I got introduced by a friend. The package comes with unlimited retests
and a well-rounded audit report. And I would have to do this every year, so it will cost me
$10,000/year
 for the security audit.
The audit works by choosing a level of testing you want the firm to perform. It could be:
White-box testing: full source code and system design access
Black-box testing: no code access, no info on system
Or Gray-box testing: reveal some info but not all.
I decided to go with gray-box testing since I wanted to give the hackers as much
information as I could without revealing my source code, so they could help catch more
security issues and help me improve the system.
Then they do the pen-test. It takes 2 weeks. There were some security issues found, I
fixed those, then they retested, all usual steps. Once everything was done, they
prepared a comprehensive report of the system.
In the report, they listed what issues they found and how long it took to fix the issues,
along with other related information. This report was later presented to the SOC 2
auditors, but it can also be helpful to provide to customers later on if someone requests
it.
And that's how I got my company security audited. It cost a lot (could have cost so
much more!), but it was well worth it.
Things that are easier than I thought
I thought in order to get SOC 2 certified, I must implement SSO for all of my employees. I
used Okta when I was an employee, it was good and secure and everything, but it's very
expensive (
very very expensive!
).
I later learned that getting everyone on SSO is not a requirement, as long as we have a
way to control people's access to critical resources and have a documented process on
how to deal with cases when, for example, an employee's device is hacked.
So I didn't have to ask everyone to use SSO everywhere. It was a huge save!
The second thing I found easier than I thought is the requirement to install a "spyware"
on your employee devices. I later learned that as long as you can provide sufficient
evidence that the employee's devices are secured, you don't need to ask them to install
that "spyware".
The "spyware" is a piece of software that runs in the background of your OS and
constantly checks if your device is secure as per SOC 2 standards (things like: hard
drive encryption is enabled, lock screen is enabled, installed some sort of
antivirus/antimalware software, etc.)
What I did was to give my employees two options: 1 – to install the software and let it
collect the required data, or 2 – collect the data by yourself with screenshots, it would
be about 6-10 screenshots showing various config and settings of the device to prove to
the auditor that it's secured as per standard.
[64]
And with that, I didn't have to force my employees to install anything.
Things that are harder than I thought
There is a requirement to make sure that your system can be recovered in case of a
disaster (disaster recovery), which is not quite hard, but very time-consuming.
They basically ask you to rebuild your entire infra in another data center (or another
AWS region) and verify everything works normally, then provide the evidence
(screenshots) to them.
I've never done this before. I've always thought that since I built and set up everything
from scratch, I wouldn't have a problem doing it again. But actually spending the time
doing it is still very beneficial. I found some unnecessary environment variables and
some unused components that I later removed.
So in TypingMind I have two data centers (US and EU), which means I had to do it twice,
each time for a region (did I mention time-consuming?).
Do I actually benefit from getting SOC 2?
Yes.
The first thing is that I'm working with some 
resellers
 who help me sell TypingMind to
other markets. Some of them are selling to clients who are very strict about security. So
I've been dealing with security questionnaires for quite a while.
Having a SOC 2 certificate simplifies things a lot for my resellers and me.
Second, I'm in the sales process with some enterprise customers, and most of them
want to see my SOC 2. Now I can show them, so I hope this helps me close more deals.
And the last thing, SOC 2 certificate is given at the company level. It means that every
product that I build from now on will automatically have the SOC 2 label on it as long as I
[65]
continue to satisfy all the SOC 2 requirements. This includes my previous products like
DevUtils. This is peace of mind.
So overall, I think I've already benefited from it. Is it worth the ~$20K/year? Not sure yet,
but I hope it will in the long run.
Do you need SOC 2?
So now you know what I know about SOC 2. Do you need it? Maybe.
I think you can consider getting it if:
You frequently have enterprise customers asking for it.
Your product is in the B2B market, and you want to make the deal-closing process
smoother.
You can afford ~$20K/year + weeks of work to implement the requirements. (it
probably cost more and takes longer if your company is bigger)
That's all
Before getting SOC 2, I struggled a lot to understand the big picture of the process and
spent a lot of time reading so many random articles addressing different aspects of the
process, but I couldn't find a good overview article.
So that's why I decided to write this. I hope this post has been helpful.
Appedix: .
